Infinite energy maps and rigidity Thanks: GD supported in part by NSF DMS-2105226, CM supported in part by NSF DMS-2005406.
Georgios Daskalopoulos, Chikako Mese

Abstract
. We extend Siu’s and Sampson’s celebrated rigidity results to non-compact domains. More precisely, let M M be a smooth quasi-projective variety with universal cover M ~ \tilde{M} and let X ~ \tilde{X} be a symmetric space of non-compact type, a locally finite Euclidean building or the Weil-Petersson completion of the Teichmüller space of a surface of genus g g and p p punctures with 3 ​ g − 3 + p > 0 3g-3+p>0 . Under suitable assumptions on a homomorphism ρ : π 1 ​ ( M ) → 𝖨𝗌𝗈𝗆 ⁡ ( X ~ ) \rho:\pi_{1}(M)\rightarrow\mathsf{Isom}(\tilde{X}) , we show that there exists a ρ \rho -equivariant pluriharmonic map u ~ : M ~ → X ~ \tilde{u}:\tilde{M}\rightarrow\tilde{X} of possibly infinite energy. In the case when the target is Kähler and 𝗋𝖺𝗇𝗄 ⁡ ( d ​ u ~ ) ≥ 3 \mathsf{rank}(d\tilde{u})\geq 3 at some point, u ~ \tilde{u} is holomorphic or conjugate holomorphic. This builds on previous important work by [ JZ ] and [ M ] . We also extend these results to the case when the target is a Riemannian manifold with sectional curvature bounded from above by a negative constant.



Introduction

In his influential paper
[
Siu1
]
, Y.-T. Siu proved that harmonic maps between Kähler manifolds of complex dimension
\geq 2
are pluriharmonic, provided that the domain manifold is compact and the target non-positively curved in a strong sense. This result partially settled a conjecture by Yau made a few years earlier. Siu’s ideas included an extension of the Bochner’s formula and were further developed by others. Notably, Sampson
[
Sa
]
showed that a harmonic map from a compact Kähler manifold to a Riemannian manifold of nonpositive Hermitian sectional curvature is pluriharmonic. The compactness of the domain can be replaced by completeness and finite volume, provided that there exists a finite energy map (cf.
[
Siu2
]
and
[
Co1
]
). However, in many situations, it may be difficult or even impossible to prove that a finite energy maps exists. The goal of this manuscript is to extend Siu’s and Sampson’s work by proving the existence of possibly infinite energy pluriharmonic or even holomorphic maps between Kähler manifolds.

Infinite energy harmonic maps between manifolds previously appeared the work of Lohkamp and Wolf. Lohcamp
[
Loh
]
proved the existence of a harmonic map in a given homotopy class of maps between two non-compact manifolds, provided that a certain simplicity condition is satisfied. The most important case is when the domain is metrically a product near infinity. Wolf
[
Wo
]
studied harmonic maps of infinite energy when the domain is a nodal Riemann surface and applied this study to describe degenerations of surfaces in the Riemann moduli space.

A few years later, Jost and Zuo (cf.
[
JZ
]
,
[
Z
]
) sketched a proof of the existence of infinite energy pluriharmonic maps from non-compact Kähler manifolds to symmetric spaces and buildings.
In a remarkable paper, Mochizuki
[
M
]
rigorously constructed pluriharmonic maps into the symmetric space
GL(r,\mathbb{C})/U(r)
. More precisely, Mochizuki constructed pluriharmonic metrics on flat vector bundles. These metrics correspond to harmonic maps by the Donaldson-Corlette theorem (cf.
[
Do
]
,
[
Co1
]
).
Mochizuki brought into the picture a new Bochner formula (cf.
[
M
, Proposition 21.42]
) for harmonic bundles that can be used to bypass the potential obstruction coming from the non-triviality of the normal bundle of the divisor at infinity.

In this paper, we prove the existence of pluriharmonic maps without assuming the existence of a finite energy maps nor the triviality assumption. One of the contributions of this paper is to broaden Mochizuki’s Bochner formula for harmonic maps. Applying these Bochner formulas, we prove the following theorems:

Theorem 1
.

Let
M
,
\tilde{X}
and
\rho:\pi_{1}(M)\rightarrow\tilde{X}
satisfy the following:

•

M
is a smooth quasi-projective variety with universal cover
\tilde{M}

•

\tilde{X}
is a symmetric space of non-compact type

•

\rho:\pi_{1}(M)\rightarrow\mathsf{Isom}(\tilde{X})
is a proper homomorphism that does not fix an unbounded closed convex strict subset of
\tilde{X}
.

Then there exists a unique
\rho
-equivariant pluriharmonic map
\tilde{u}:\tilde{M}\rightarrow\tilde{X}
of sub-logarithmic growth.

Here,
sub-logarithmic growth
means that the map grows slower than a logarithmic function as it approaches the divisor, cf. Definition
6.9
and Definition
17.3
.

Theorem 2
.

In Theorem
1
, if we additionally assume that
\tilde{X}
is a Kähler manifold and the
\rho
-equivariant harmonic map
\tilde{u}:\tilde{M}\rightarrow\tilde{X}
has
\mathsf{rank}_{\mathbb{R}}(d\tilde{u})\geq 3
at some point, then
\tilde{u}
is holomorphic or conjugate holomorphic.

Theorem 3
.

The conclusions of Theorem
1
and Theorem
2
hold if we replace
\tilde{X}
by a complete simply connected Kähler manifold with strong nonpositive curvature in the sense of Siu and sectional curvature bounded from above by
\kappa<0
.
Furthermore,
the conclusion of Theorem
1
holds if we replace
\tilde{X}
by a Riemannian manifold
\tilde{X}
of Hermitian-negative curvature and sectional curvature bounded from above by
\kappa<0
.

Theorem 4
.

The conclusion of Theorem
1
holds if we place
\tilde{X}
by a locally finite Euclidean building without a Euclidean factor.

Theorem 5
.

The conclusion of Theorem
1
holds if we replace
\tilde{X}
by the Weil-Petersson completion of Teichmüller space and assume only that
\rho
is a proper homomorphism (i.e. we do not need to assume that it fixes an unbounded closed convex strict subset of
\tilde{X}
).

Theorems
1
,
2
and
4
were first stated in
[
JZ
]
and
[
Z
]
where some of the ideas originate.
One of the contributions of this paper is to provide complete proofs. However, our main motivation of this paper is to develop techniques
to study rigidity questions associated to the mapping class group of surfaces. Indeed, in a sequel to this paper, we will use Theorem
5
to provide a proof of the following generalization of the holomorphic rigidity theorem of
[
DM4
]
:

Holomorphic Rigidity of Teichmüller space
.

Let
\Gamma
and
\mathcal{T}
denote the mapping class group and the Teichmüller space of an oriented surface
S
of genus
g
and
p
punctures where
3g-3+p>0
. Assume that
\Gamma
acts as a discrete automorphism group on a contractible Kähler manifold
\tilde{M}
and there exists a finite cover
M^{\prime}
of
\tilde{M}/\Gamma
that is a smooth quasi-projective variety.
Then
\tilde{M}
is equivariantly biholomorphic or conjugate biholomorphic to the Teichmüller space
\mathcal{T}
of
S
.

The above statement strengthens
[
DM4
, Corollary 1.5]
. In
[
DM4
]
, we made the assumption that
M
admits a compactification
\overline{M}
as an algebraic variety such that the codimension of
\overline{M}\backslash M
is
\geq 3
. This condition originates in
[
JY1
, Lemma 4]
, where they use this to prove the existence of an equivariant homotopy equivalence
of finite energy
between
\tilde{M}
and
\mathcal{T}
. This is also the starting point of
[
DM4
, Corollary 1.5]
. With the theory developed in this paper, we will show that we can remove this unnecessary assumption.

Acknowledgements.
The authors would like to thank T. Mochizuki and Y. Siu for illuminating discussions.

Outline of the paper

In Chapter
1
, we record the relevant background material needed in this paper. Of particular importance is the characterization of certain class of isometries in a space of non-positive curvature
\tilde{X}
. To give a rough description of these isometries, first note that a semi-simple (i.e. elliptic or hyperbolic) isometry
I
with translation length
\Delta_{I}
is characterized by the property that there exists a point
P\in\tilde{X}
such that

d(P,I(P))=\Delta_{I}.

We want to allow for isometries that are more general than semi-simple ones. This motivates the following: An isometry
I:\tilde{X}\rightarrow\tilde{X}
is said to have an
exponential decay to its translation length
if there exists a geodesic ray
c:[0,\infty)\rightarrow\tilde{X}
such that

d(c(t),I(c(t)))=\Delta_{I}(1+be^{-at})

for some constants
a,b>0
(cf.
[
JZ
, 3.2.3]
).
We show that all isometries of
\tilde{X}
considered in Theorem
1
, Theorem
3
, and Theorem
4
satisfy this property. We use this to obtain the energy growth estimates for the harmonic map we construct later.

In Chapter
2
, we present
the key tools of this paper: the variations on the Siu’s and Sampson’s Bochner formulas
. If the domain Kähler manifold
M
is compact, then Siu’s or Sampson’s Bochner formulas can be used to prove that harmonicity implies pluriharmonicity. With
M
as in Theorem
1
, neither Siu’s nor Sampson’s formulas are sufficient to overcome the complication coming from the non-compactness of the domain. Indeed, they need to be rewritten in a different form to get the cancellations of the residues near the divisor.
The new formulas, contained in
Theorem
4.9
and Theorem
5.9
, are inspired by Mochizuki’s work on harmonic bundles
[
M
, Proposition 21.42]
.

Chapter
3
, Chapter
4
, and Chapter
5
comprise the proofs of Theorem
1
, Theorem
2
and Theorem
3
. We follow the proof of
[
M
]
with adjustments made on the account of the more general target spaces.
A brief sketch of the proof is given below.

Let
M
,

\tilde{X}
and
\rho:\pi_{1}(M)\rightarrow\tilde{X}
be as in Theorem
1
with
\dim_{\mathbb{C}}M=n
. We construct a pluriharmonic map by an induction on
n
(cf. Chapter
5
).
We can assume that
M=\bar{M}\backslash\Sigma
where
\bar{M}
is a smooth projective variety of complex dimension
\geq 2
and
\Sigma
is a divisor with normal crossings.
The inductive hypothesis implies that if

•

\bar{Y}\subset\bar{M}
is a sufficiently ample divisor such that
\bar{Y}\cap\Sigma
is a normal crossing,

•

Y=\bar{Y}\backslash\Sigma
,

•

\iota:Y\hookrightarrow M
is the inclusion map, and

•

\rho_{Y}:\pi_{1}(Y)\rightarrow\tilde{X}
is the composition
\rho_{Y}=\rho\circ\iota_{*}
,

then there exists a unique pluriharmonic
\rho_{Y}
-equivariant map
\tilde{u}_{Y}:\tilde{Y}\rightarrow\tilde{X}
.
Letting
\{\bar{Y}_{s}\}
be a collection of such subvarieties whose union is
\bar{M}
, we show that the map
\tilde{u}:\tilde{M}\rightarrow\tilde{X}
given by

(0.1)

\tilde{u}=\tilde{u}_{Y_{s}}\mbox{ on $Y_{s}:=\bar{Y}_{s}\backslash\Sigma$}

is a well-defined, thereby proving the existence result for
\dim_{\mathbb{C}}M=n
. To get this induction started, we first need to show existence and uniqueness of pluriharmonic maps in dimensions
n=1
and
n=2
.

First assume
\dim_{\mathbb{C}}M=1
. In other words, we construct harmonic maps from punctured Riemann surfaces of possibly infinite energy (cf. Chapter
3
). For the purpose of explaining this case, we assume that
\tilde{X}
is a universal cover of a compact manifold
X
and let
\gamma:\mathbb{S}^{1}\rightarrow X
be an arc length parameterization of a minimizing closed geodesic in
X
. Denote the energy of
\gamma
by
E^{\gamma}
. A prototype map
v
corresponding to
\gamma
is defined on the infinite cylinder
[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{S}^{1}
and is equal to
\gamma
on slices
\{t\}\times\mathbb{S}^{1}
for all sufficiently large
t
. More precisely, given any Lipschitz map
k:\mathbb{S}^{1}\rightarrow X
, let

v:[1,\infty)\times\mathbb{S}^{1}\rightarrow X

be a Lipschitz continuous map with

\displaystyle v(1,\theta)=k(1,\theta),

\displaystyle\forall\theta\in\mathbb{S}^{1},

\displaystyle v(t,\theta)=\gamma(\theta),

\displaystyle\forall t\in[1,\infty),\,\theta\in\mathbb{S}^{1}.

The energy of
v
on the finite cylinder
[0,T]\times\mathbb{S}^{1}
is bounded from above by
T\cdot E^{\gamma}+C
where
C
is a constant independent of
T
. Let

u_{T}:[0,T]\times\mathbb{S}^{1}\rightarrow X

be the Dirichlet solution on the finite cylinder
[0,T]\times\mathbb{S}^{1}
with boundary values equal to
v
. Since
u_{T}
is an energy minimizing map, the energy of
u_{T}
is bounded from above by
T\cdot E^{\gamma}+C
. Since
\gamma
is an energy minimizing map, the energy of
u_{T}
in any finite cylinder
[0,t]\times\mathbb{S}^{1}
for
0<t\leq T
is bounded from below by
t\cdot E^{\gamma}
. From this, we conclude that the energy of
u_{T}
on any finite cylinder
[t_{1},t_{2}]\times\mathbb{S}^{1}
has an upper bound independent of
T
; namely,
(t_{2}-t_{1})\cdot E^{\gamma}+C
.
Thus, the regularity theory for harmonic maps implies that the family of maps
\{u_{T}\}_{T\geq 1}
have a uniform Lipschitz bound on any compact subset of
[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{S}^{1}
. By the Arzela-Ascoli theorem, there exists a sequence
T_{i}\rightarrow\infty
such that
u_{T_{i}}
converges locally uniformly to a harmonic map
u:[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{S}^{1}\rightarrow X
. We extend this idea to prove the existence of an equivariant harmonic map from any punctured Riemann surface
\Sigma
. In doing so, we replace the existence of a minimizing geodesic
\gamma
with the assumption that the the isometry
\rho([\gamma])
has exponential decay to its translation length for any
[\gamma]\in\pi_{1}(M)
representing a loop around a puncture. We note that the idea of the prototype map already appears in
[
Loh
]
. The main contribution of Chapter
3
to the theory of infinite energy harmonic maps is the extension of the existence result to general NPC target spaces (i.e. not necessarily manifolds) and, more importantly, the uniqueness result (cf. Theorem
6.5
and Theorem
6.6
respectively). The uniqueness is a crucial part of the inductive argument. Indeed, it is used to show that the the pluriharmonic map in dimension
n
(
0.1
) is well-defined.

Next, assume
\dim_{\mathbb{C}}M=2
. In other words, we consider harmonic maps from a complex surface (cf. Chapter
4
). The idea, as in the case of
\dim_{\mathbb{C}}M=1
, is to first construct a prototype map and then use it to construct a harmonic map (cf.
[
JZ
, Sec. 3.2]
). By the assumption that the divisor
\Sigma\subset\bar{M}
is a normal crossing divisor,
\Sigma
consists of a finite set of irreducible smooth components
\Sigma_{j}
intersecting pairwise at most isolated points. We choose a canonical section
\sigma_{j}
of the line bundle
\mathcal{O}(\Sigma_{j})
vanishing along
\Sigma_{j}
and let
h_{j}
be a Hermitian metric on
\mathcal{O}(\Sigma_{j})
. We endow the quasi-projective variety
M=\bar{M}\backslash\Sigma
with a Poincare type metric
g
(cf. Definition
11.1
and Cornabla and Griffiths
[
CoGr
]
) making
M
into a Kähler manifold.
Following
[
M
]
, we construct a fiber-wise harmonic map along the divisor at infinity. In other words, we construct a prototype map
v
as follows:
on any punctured disk
\mathbb{D}^{*}
where
\mathbb{D}
is a disk transverse to
\Sigma_{j}
, let
v
be such that the restriction to
\mathbb{D}^{*}
is a harmonic map. Much like in the construction of the harmonic map from the infinite cylinder
[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{S}^{1}
(which is conformally equivalent to the punctured disk), we use the prototype map
v
to construct a sequence of Dirichlet solutions defined on compact sets that exhaust
M
. This sequence contains a subsequence that converges locally uniformly to a
\rho
-equivariant harmonic map
u:M\rightarrow\tilde{X}
. By the Bochner method, we then show that
u
is in fact pluriharmonic (cf. Theorem
9.8
). Technically, the most challenging part is the analysis near the crossings of the divisor and dealing with the non-triviality of the normal bundle of the divisor. In fact, even when the divisor is smooth, an application of the Siu’s or Sampson’s Bochner formula does not immediately yield pluriharmonicity. We need the variations of the Siu’s and Sampson Bochner formulas provided in Chapter
2
. Integrating these formulas and applying integration by parts, a miraculous cancellation (originally discovered by Mochizuki
[
M
, Section 6.3]
in terms of flat vector bundles) allows us to prove pluriharmonicity and even holomorphicity of
u
. We can thus start the induction process to prove the existence in higher dimensions.

In Chapter
6
, we combine the harmonic map theory to Euclidean buildings developed in
[
GS
]
and to the Weil-Petersson completion of Teichmüller space developed in
[
DM1
]
,
[
DM2
]
,
[
DM3
]
and
[
DM4
]
with ideas in the previous chapters to prove Theorem
4
.
The main difference in the proof of these theorems is that we must deal with the singularities of harmonic maps that apriori may exist because of the singular nature of the target spaces.
We also use the important fact that every element of the isometry group for a Euclidean building (resp. the mapping class group) acts as an elliptic or hyperbolic element. For the isometry group of a Euclidean building (resp. the mapping class group), this follows from
[
RC
, Proposition 2.1]
(resp. Thurston’s classification of mapping classes and
[
DW
]
).

Chapter 1
Preliminaries

1.
Definitions

1.1.
NPC spaces

We refer to
[
BH
]
for more details than provided here.

Definition 1.1
.

A curve
c:[a,b]\rightarrow\tilde{X}
into a metric space is called a
geodesic
if
\mathsf{length}(l|_{[\alpha,\beta]})=d(c(\alpha),c(\beta))
for any subinterval
[\alpha,\beta]\subset[a,b]
. (Note that a identically constant map from an interval is a geodesic.). A metric space
\tilde{X}
is a
geodesic space
if there exists a geodesic connecting every pair of points in
\tilde{X}
.

Definition 1.2
.

An
NPC space

\tilde{X}
is a complete geodesic space that satisfies the
following condition:
For any three points
P,R,Q\in\tilde{X}
and an arclength parameterized geodesic
c:[0,l]\rightarrow\tilde{X}
with
c(0)=Q
and
c(l)=R
,

d^{2}(P,Q_{t})\leq(1-t)d^{2}(P,Q)+td^{2}(P,R)-t(1-t)d^{2}(Q,R)

where
Q_{t}=c(tl)
.

Notation 1.3
.

It follows immediately from Definition
1.2
that, given
P,Q\in\tilde{X}
and
t\in[0,1]
, there exists a
unique
point with distance from
P
equal to
td(P,Q)
and the distance from
Q
equal to
(1-t)d(P,Q)
. We denote this point by

(1-t)P+tQ.

Definition 1.4
.

Let
\tilde{X}
be an NPC space. We say that two geodesics rays
c,c^{\prime}:[0,\infty)\rightarrow X
are
equivalent
if there exists a constant
K
such that
d(c(t),c^{\prime}(t))\leq K
for all
t\in[0,\infty)
. Denote the equivalence class of a geodesic ray
c
by
[c]
. The set
\partial\tilde{X}
of boundary points of
\tilde{X}
is the set of equivalence classes of geodesic rays.

Definition 1.5
.

For any
\kappa>0
, we say an NPC space
\tilde{X}
is CAT
(-\kappa)
if for any
P,Q,R
and
Q_{t}
as in Definition
1.2
,

(1.1)

\cosh d(P,Q_{t})\leq\frac{\sinh(1-t)\kappa d(P,Q)}{\sinh\kappa d(P,Q))}\cosh d(P,Q)+\frac{\sinh t\kappa d(P,Q)}{\sinh\kappa d(P,Q))}\cosh d(P,R).

Definition 1.6
.

We say that an NPC space
\tilde{X}
is
negatively curved
if, for any point
P\in\tilde{X}
, there exists
r>0
and
\kappa>0
such that every
P,Q,R\in B_{r}(P)
satisfies (
1.1
).

1.2.
Maps into NPC spaces

In this paper, we consider harmonic maps into NPC spaces. In many cases, the target space
\tilde{X}
is a smooth Riemannian manifold of non-positive sectional curvature. In this case, the energy of a smooth map
u:\Omega\rightarrow\tilde{X}
is

E^{u}=\int_{\Omega}|df|^{2}d\mbox{vol}_{g}

where
(\Omega,g)
is a Riemannian domain and
d\mbox{vol}_{g}
is the volume form of
\Omega
.
We say
u:\tilde{M}\rightarrow\tilde{X}
is
harmonic
if it is locally energy minimizing; i.e. for any
p\in\tilde{M}
, there exists
r>0
such that the restriction
u|_{B_{r}(p)}
minimizes energy amongst all maps
v:B_{r}(p)\rightarrow\tilde{X}
with the same boundary values as
u|_{B_{r}(p)}
.

In other cases, we consider the target
\tilde{X}
to be an NPC space, not necessarily smooth. Below, we will review harmonic maps in this context, and refer to
[
KS1
]
for more details than provided here.

Let
(\Omega,g)
be a bounded Lipschitz Riemannian domain. Let
\Omega_{\epsilon}
be the set of points in
\Omega
at a distance least
\epsilon
from
\partial\Omega
. Let
B_{\epsilon}(x)
be a geodesic ball centered at
x
and
S_{\epsilon}(x)=\partial B_{\epsilon}(x)
. We say
f:\Omega\rightarrow X
is an
L^{2}
-map (or that
f\in L^{2}(\Omega,X)
) if

\int_{\Omega}d^{2}(f,P)d\mbox{vol}_{g}<\infty.

For
f\in L^{2}(\Omega,X)
, define

e_{\epsilon}:\Omega\rightarrow{\bf R},\ \ \ e_{\epsilon}(x)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\displaystyle{\int_{y\in S_{\epsilon}(x)}\frac{d^{2}(f(x),f(y))}{\epsilon^{2}}\frac{d\sigma_{x,\epsilon}}{\epsilon}}&x\in\Omega_{\epsilon}\\
0&\mbox{otherwise}\end{array}\right.

where
\sigma_{x,\epsilon}
is the induced measure on
S_{\epsilon}(x)
. We define a
family of functionals

E^{f}_{\epsilon}:C_{c}(X)\rightarrow{\bf R},\ \ \ \ E^{f}_{\epsilon}(\varphi)=\int_{\Omega}\varphi e_{\epsilon}d\mbox{vol}_{g}.

We say
f
has finite energy (or that
f\in W^{1,2}(\Omega,X)
)
if

E^{f}:=\sup_{\varphi\in C_{c}(\Omega),0\leq\varphi\leq 1}\limsup_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0}E^{f}_{\epsilon}(\varphi)<\infty.

It is shown in
[
KS1
]
that if
f
has finite energy, the measures
e_{\epsilon}(x)d\mbox{vol}_{g}
converge weakly to a measure which is
absolutely continuous with respect to the Lebesgue measure.
Therefore, there exists a function
e(x)
, which we call the
energy density, so that
e_{\epsilon}(x)d\mbox{vol}_{g}\rightharpoonup e(x)d\mbox{vol}_{g}
. In analogy to the case of smooth targets, we
write
|\nabla f|^{2}(x)
in place of
e(x)
. In particular, the (Korevaar-Schoen) energy of
f
in
\Omega
is

E^{f}[\Omega]=\int_{\Omega}|\nabla f|^{2}d\mbox{vol}_{g}.

Definition 1.7
.

We say a continuous map
u:\Omega\rightarrow\tilde{X}
from a Lipschitz domain
\Omega
is
harmonic
if it is locally energy minimizing; more precisely, at each
p\in\Omega
, there exists a neighborhood
\Omega
of
p
so that all continuous comparison maps which agree with
u
outside of this neighborhood have no less energy.

For
V\in\Gamma\Omega
where
\Gamma\Omega
is the set of
Lipschitz vector fields on
\Omega
,
|f_{*}(V)|^{2}
is similarly
defined. The real valued
L^{1}
function
|f_{*}(V)|^{2}
generalizes
the norm squared on the directional derivative of
f
. The
generalization of the pull-back metric is the continuous, symmetric, bilinear, non-negative and tensorial operator

\pi_{f}(V,W)=\Gamma\Omega\times\Gamma\Omega\rightarrow L^{1}(\Omega,{\bf R})

where

\pi_{f}(V,W)=\frac{1}{2}|f_{*}(V+W)|^{2}-\frac{1}{2}|f_{*}(V-W)|^{2}.

We refer to
[
KS1
]
for more
details.

Let
(x^{1},\dots,x^{n})
be local coordinates of
(\Omega,g)
and
g=(g_{ij})
,
g^{-1}=(g^{ij})
be the local metric expressions. Then energy density function of

f
can be written (cf.
[
KS1
, (2.3vi)]
)

|\nabla f|^{2}=g^{ij}\pi_{f}(\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{i}},\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{j}})

Next assume
(\Omega,g)
is a Hermitian domain and let
(z^{1}=x^{1}+ix^{2},\dots,z^{n}=x^{2n-1}+ix^{2n})
be local complex coordinates. We extend
\pi_{f}
linearly cover
\mathbb{C}
and denote

\frac{\partial f}{\partial z^{i}}\cdot\frac{\partial f}{\partial\bar{z}^{j}}=\pi_{f}(\frac{\partial}{\partial z^{i}},\frac{\partial}{\partial\bar{z}^{j}})

and

\left|\frac{\partial f}{\partial z^{i}}\right|^{2}=\pi_{f}(\frac{\partial}{\partial z^{i}},\frac{\partial}{\partial\bar{z}^{i}}).

Thus,

\frac{1}{4}|\nabla f|^{2}=g^{i\bar{j}}\frac{\partial f}{\partial z^{i}}\cdot\frac{\partial f}{\partial\bar{z}^{j}}.

1.3.
Isometries of an NPC space

Throughout this paper, we denote the group of isometries of an NPC space
\tilde{X}
by
\mathsf{Isom}(\tilde{X})
. Isometries of an NPC space are classified as follows.

Definition 1.8
.

For
I\in\mathsf{Isom}(\tilde{X})
,
let

\Delta_{I}:=\inf_{P\in\tilde{X}}d(I(P),P)

denote its translation length and define

\mathsf{Min}(I):=\{P\in\tilde{X}:d(I(P),P)=\Delta_{I}\}.

The isometry
I
is
elliptic
if
\Delta_{I}=0
and
\mathsf{Min}(I)\neq\emptyset
. It is
hyperbolic
if
\Delta_{I}>0
and
\mathsf{Min}(I)\neq\emptyset
. If
I
is elliptic or hyperbolic, then we say
I
is
semisimple
. Otherwise,
I
is said to be
parabolic
.

Lemma 1.9
.

If
\tilde{X}
is locally compact and
I\in\mathsf{Isom}(\tilde{X})
is a parabolic isometry, then there exists a arclength parameterized geodesic ray

(1.2)

c:[0,\infty)\rightarrow\tilde{X}\ \mbox{ such that }\ \lim_{s\rightarrow\infty}d(I(c(s)),c(s))=\Delta_{I}.

Proof.

Let
P_{i}\in\tilde{X}
be such that

\lim_{i\rightarrow\infty}d(I(P_{i}),P_{i})=\Delta_{I}.

Fix
P_{0}\in\tilde{X}
and let
T_{i}=d(P_{i},P_{0})
. Since
\mathsf{Min}(I)=\emptyset
, we have that
T_{i}\rightarrow\infty
.
Let
c_{i}:[0,T_{i}]\rightarrow\tilde{X}
be the arclength parameterized geodesic from
P_{0}
to
P_{i}
. Since
\tilde{X}
is assumed to be locally compact, by taking a subsequence if necessary, the sequence
c_{i}(s)
converges uniformly on every compact set to an arclength parameterized geodesic ray
c(s)
.
∎

Definition 1.10
.

Let
\Gamma
be a finitely generated group,
\Lambda
be a finite set of generators of
\Gamma
,
\tilde{X}
be an NPC space and
\rho:\Gamma\rightarrow\mathsf{Isom}(\tilde{X})
be a homomorphism. Define
\delta:\tilde{X}\rightarrow[0,\infty)
to be the function

\delta(P)=\max\{d(\rho(\lambda)P,P):\lambda\in\Lambda\}.

We say
\rho
is
proper
if the sublevel sets of the function
\delta
is bounded in
\tilde{X}
; i.e. given
c>0
, there exists
P_{0}\in X
and
R_{0}>0
such that

\{P\in\tilde{X}:\delta(P)\leq c\}\subset B_{R_{0}}(P_{0}).

1.4.
Equivariant maps and section of the flat
\tilde{X}
-bundle

Following Donaldson
[
Do
]
, we will replace equivariant maps with sections of an associated fiber bundle.
Assume we have the following:

•

a complete Riemannian manifold
(M,g)
with universal covering
\Pi:\tilde{M}\rightarrow M

•

NPC space
\tilde{X}

•

action of
\pi_{1}(M)
on
\tilde{M}
by deck transformations

•

homomorphism
\rho:\pi_{1}(M)\rightarrow\mathsf{Isom}(\tilde{X})

Definition 1.11
.

A map
\tilde{f}:\tilde{M}\rightarrow\tilde{X}
is said to be
\rho
-equivariant if

\tilde{f}(\gamma p)=\rho(\gamma)\tilde{f}(p),\ \ \forall\gamma\in\pi_{1}(M),\ p\in\tilde{M}.

Remark 1.12
.

Assume
\rho:\pi_{1}(M)\rightarrow\mathsf{Isom}(\tilde{X})
is a proper homomorphism (cf. Definition
1.10
). If there exists a finite energy
\rho
-equivariant map
f:\tilde{M}\rightarrow\tilde{X}
, then there exists a Lipschitz harmonic map
u:\tilde{M}\rightarrow\tilde{X}
(cf.
[
KS2
, Theorem 2.1.3, Remark 2.1.5]
). In this paper, we are trying to establish the existence of a harmonic map
without the assuming that there exists a finite energy map to start with.

The quotient space under the action of
\pi_{1}(M)
on the product
\tilde{M}\times\tilde{X}
is the
twisted product

\tilde{M}\times_{\rho}\tilde{X}.

In other words,
\tilde{M}\times_{\rho}\tilde{X}
is the set of orbits
[(p,x)]
of a point
(p,x)\in\tilde{M}\times\tilde{X}
under the action of
\gamma\in\pi_{1}(M)
via the deck transformation on the first component and the isometry
\rho(\gamma)
on the second component. The fiber bundle

\tilde{M}\times_{\rho}\tilde{X}\rightarrow M

with fibers over
p\in M
is isometric to
\tilde{X}
is the
flat
\tilde{X}
-bundle
over
M
defined by
\rho
.

There is a one-to-one correspondence between sections of this fibration and
\rho
-equivariant maps

\tilde{f}:\tilde{M}\rightarrow\tilde{X}\ \ \ \ \longleftrightarrow\ \ \ \ f:M\rightarrow\tilde{M}\times_{\rho}\tilde{X}

satisfying the relationship

[(\tilde{p},\tilde{f}(\tilde{p}))]\leftrightarrow f(p)\ \mbox{
where $\Pi(\tilde{p})=p$.}

Since the energy density function
|\nabla\tilde{f}|^{2}
of
\tilde{f}
is a
\rho
-invariant function, we can define

|\nabla f|^{2}(p):=|\nabla\tilde{f}|^{2}(\tilde{p}).

(In literature, this quantity is sometimes referred as the
vertical
part the derivative of
f
, e.g.
[
Do
]
.)
We can similarly define the pullback inner product and directional energy density functions of
f
by using the corresponding notions for
\tilde{f}
given in Subsection
1.2
.
For
U\subset M
,
the (vertical) energy of a section
f
is

(1.3)

E^{f}[U]=\int_{U}|\nabla f|^{2}d\mbox{vol}_{g}.

Furthermore,
for sections
f_{1}
,
f_{2}
, we define

(1.4)

d(f_{1}(p),f_{2}(p)):=d(\tilde{f}_{1}(\tilde{p}),\tilde{f}_{2}(\tilde{p}))

where
\tilde{f}_{1}
,
\tilde{f}_{2}
are the associated
\rho
-equivariant maps to sections
f_{1}
,
f_{2}
respectively.

2.
Exponential decay of an isometry

The distinguishing characteristic of a semisimple isometry
I:\tilde{X}\rightarrow\tilde{X}
is that there is a point
P_{*}\in\tilde{X}
such that
d(P_{*},I(P_{*}))
is equal to its translation length
\Delta_{I}
. No such point
P_{*}
exists if
I
is a parabolic isometry.
This motivates the following:

Definition 2.1
.

We say that
I\in\mathsf{Isom}(\tilde{X})
has an
exponential decay to its translation length
(or simply
exponential decay
for short) if one of the following conditions hold:

•

I
is semisimple, or

•

I
is parabolic and there exist an arclength parameterized geodesic ray
c:[0,\infty)\rightarrow\tilde{X}
and constants
a,b>0
such that

d^{2}(I(c(t)),c(t))\leq\Delta_{I}^{2}(1+be^{-at}).

If
I
is semisimple, we can simply let
c(t)
be identically equal to the point
P_{*}
as above, and thus
I
has an exponential decay to its translation length. On the other hand, for a parabolic isometry in a general NPC space, we may not be able to find a geodesic ray
c(t)
such that that
d(c(t),I(c(t)))
converges to
\Delta_{I}
exponentially fast.

In this section, we prove that isometries of important examples of NPC spaces (i.e. symmetric spaces of non-compact type, CAT(
-1
) spaces, and Euclidean buildings) have an exponential decay to their translation length.

2.1.
Symmetric spaces of non-compact type

In this subsection, we prove that every isometry of a symmetric space of non-compact type has exponential decay. To this end, we introduce the following notations:

•

\mathsf{M}(n,\mathbb{R})
is the algebra of
(n\times n)
-matrices with real coefficients.

•

\mathsf{S}(n,\mathbb{R})
is the vector subspace of
\mathsf{M}(n,\mathbb{R})
consisting of symmetric matrices.

•

\mathsf{P}(n,\mathbb{R})
is the open cone of positive definite symmetric matrices.

•

\mathsf{GL}(n,\mathbb{R})
is the general linear group, or the invertible
(n\times n)
-matrices.

•

\mathsf{O}(n)
is the orthogonal group, i.e. the elements of
\mathsf{GL}(n,\mathbb{R})
such that its transpose is its inverse.

•

\mathsf{A}(n)
is the subgroup of diagonal matices with positive diagonals.

•

\mathsf{N}(n)
is the subgroup of upper triangular matrices with diagonal entries 1.

The group
\mathsf{GL}(n,\mathbb{R})
acts on
\mathsf{P}(n,\mathbb{R})
by the standard left action; i.e.

G.p:=GpG^{T},\ \ \ \forall G\in\mathsf{GL}(n,\mathbb{R}),\,p\in\mathsf{P}(n,\mathbb{R})

where the left hand side consists of matrix multiplication.
Denoting the identity matrix by
e
, we have

T_{e}\mathsf{P}(n,\mathbb{R})=\mathsf{S}(n,\mathbb{R}).

The manifold
\mathsf{P}(n,\mathbb{R})
along with the Riemannian metric given by

\langle V,W\rangle_{p}=\mbox{Trace}(p^{-1}Vp^{-1}W),\ \ \ p\in\mathsf{P}(n,\mathbb{R}),\ V,W\in T_{p}\mathsf{P}(n,\mathbb{R})

defines a NPC symmetric space with isometry group
\mathsf{Isom}(\mathsf{P}(n,\mathbb{R}))=\mathsf{GL}(n,\mathbb{R})
(cf.
[
BH
, II.10.33, II.10.34, II.10.39]
).

Let
e
be the identity element of
\mathsf{P}(n,\mathbb{R})
.
A geodesic line

c:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathsf{P}(n,\mathbb{R})\mbox{ \ with $c(0)=e$ and $c^{\prime}(0)=V\in T_{e}\mathsf{P}(n,\mathbb{R})=\mathsf{S}(n,\mathbb{R})$}

is given by
c(t)=\exp(tV)
. If
p=\exp(V)
, then
d(e,p)=|V|
(cf.
[
BH
, II.10.42]
).

Definition 2.2
.

For a geodesic ray
c:[0,\infty)\rightarrow\mathsf{P}(n,\mathbb{R})
, let
\xi=[c]\in\partial\mathsf{P}(n,\mathbb{R})
be the equivalence class of geodesic rays containing
c
. The
parabolic subgroup
associated to
\xi=[c]
is

{\mathsf{G}}_{\xi}:=\{G\in\mathsf{P}(n,\mathbb{R}):G.\xi=\xi\}.

The
horospherical subgroup
associated to
\xi=[c]
is

{\mathsf{N}}_{\xi}=\{G\in G_{\xi}:\exp(-tV)G\exp(tV)\rightarrow I\mbox{ as }t\rightarrow\infty\}.

We refer to
[
BH
, 10.62]
for details regarding these subgroups.

Following an argument of
[
M
]
, we will first prove that all isometries of
\mathsf{P}(n,\mathbb{R})
have exponential decay.

Definition 2.3
.

For
G\in\mathsf{GL}(n,\mathbb{R})
, define

\rho(G):=\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}(\log|b_{i}|^{2})^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

where
b_{1},\dots,b_{n}
are the eigenvalues of
G
.

Lemma 2.4
.

The translation length of
G\in\mathsf{GL}(n,\mathbb{R})
is at least
\rho(G)
; i.e.

\rho(G)\leq\Delta_{G}.

Proof.

We need to show

(2.1)

\rho(G)\leq d(p,G.p),\ \ \forall p\in\mathsf{P}(n,\mathbb{R}),\forall G\in\mathsf{GL}(n,\mathbb{R}).

Before we proceed, note the following:

•

It is sufficient to prove the inequality of (
2.1
) when
p
is the identity element
e
in
\mathsf{P}(n,\mathbb{R})
.

We prove this claim in two steps.

–

Let
q
be a diagonal matrix; i.e.
q=\mathsf{diag}(\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{n})
and
q^{\frac{1}{2}}=\mathsf{diag}(\lambda_{1}^{\frac{1}{2}},\dots,\lambda_{n}^{\frac{1}{2}})
. Thus,
q=q^{\frac{1}{2}}eq^{\frac{1}{2}}
and hence
for any
G_{0}\in\mathsf{GL}(n,\mathbb{R})
and
G_{1}:=q^{-\frac{1}{2}}G_{0}q^{\frac{1}{2}}
,

\displaystyle d(G_{0}.q,q)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle d(G_{0}qG_{0}^{t},q)\ =\ d(G_{0}q^{\frac{1}{2}}eq^{\frac{1}{2}}G_{0}^{t},\,q^{\frac{1}{2}}eq^{\frac{1}{2}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle d(q^{-\frac{1}{2}}G_{0}q^{\frac{1}{2}}eq^{\frac{1}{2}}G_{0}^{t}q^{-\frac{1}{2}},e)\ =\ d(G_{1}.e,e).

–

Let
p\in\mathsf{P}(n,\mathbb{R})
. Choose
O\in\mathsf{O}(n)
be such that
p=OqO^{t}=O.q
where
q
is a diagonal matrix. If
G_{0}:=OGO^{t}
and
G_{1}:=q^{-\frac{1}{2}}G_{0}q^{\frac{1}{2}}
and, then

\displaystyle d(G.p,p)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle d(GO.q,\,O.q)\ =\ d(O^{t}GO.q,q)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle d(G_{0}.q,q)\ =\ d(G_{1}.e,e)

and
\rho(G)=\rho(G_{1})
.

•

It is sufficient to prove (
2.1
) assuming the eigenvalues of
G
are distinct.
Indeed, if
G
does not have distinct eigenvalues, we can take a sequence
G^{(j)}
of matrices with distinct eigenvalues such that
\rho(G^{(j)})\rightarrow\rho(G)
and
d(G^{(j)}.e,e)\rightarrow d(G.e,e)
.

By virtue of the above two bullet points, we assume that
G\in\mathsf{GL}(n,\mathbb{R})
has distinct eigenvalues
b_{1},b_{2},\dots,b_{n}
and show (
2.1
) with
p=e
.

Let
V\in T_{e}\mathsf{P}(n,\mathbb{R})=\mathsf{S}(n,\mathbb{R})
be a diagonal matrix with diagonal entries

\frac{\log|b_{1}|^{2}}{\rho(G)},\ \frac{\log|b_{2}|^{2}}{\rho(G)},\ \dots,\frac{\log|b_{n}|^{2}}{\rho(G)}.

Conjugating by elemen
Let
c(t)=\exp(tV)
and
\xi=[c]
. We apply the Iwasawa decomposition to write
G=OA_{0}N
where
O\in\mathsf{O}(n)
,
A_{0}\in\mathsf{A}(n)
and
N\in\mathsf{N}(n)
.
Since
A^{\prime}=A_{0}N
is an upper triangular matrix, we can decompose

A^{\prime}=N^{\prime}A_{0}

where
N^{\prime}\in\mathsf{N}(n)
. In summary,

G=OA_{0}N=OA^{\prime}=ON^{\prime}A_{0}.

The diagonal matrix
A_{0}
is the hyperbolic isometry fixing the geodesic
c(t)=\exp(tV)
and the upper triangular matrix
N
with 1’s on the diagonals is an element of the horospherical subgroup
{\mathsf{N}}_{\xi}
associated to
\xi
(cf.
[
BH
, II.10.66]
).
By construction,

c(\rho(G))=\exp(\rho(G)V)=A_{0}A_{0}^{T}=A_{0}.e.

Since the geodesic line
c(t)
intersects the horospheres centered at
\xi=[c]
orthogonally, the point
A_{0}.e
is the closest point to
e
amongst all points on the horosphere containing
A_{0}.e
(cf.
[
BH
, I.10.5]
). Furthermore, any orbit of
N^{\prime}
is contained in a single horosphere (cf.
[
BH
, II.10.66]
), and hence

d(A_{0}.e,e)\leq d(N^{\prime}A_{0}.e,\,e)=d(G.e,e).

This in turn implies

\rho(G)=\rho(A_{0})=d(A_{0}.e,e)\leq d(G.e,e).

∎

Theorem 2.5
.

For a parabolic element
G\in\mathsf{P}(n,\mathbb{R})
, let

•

\xi\in\partial\mathsf{P}(n,\mathbb{R})
be such that
G\in{\mathsf{G}}_{\xi}
(i.e.
G
is an element of the parabolic subgroup associated to
\xi
),

•

c:[0,\infty)\rightarrow\mathsf{P}(n,\mathbb{R})
be the geodesic ray such that
\xi=[c]
and
c(0)=e
(cf.
[
BH
, 10.62]
).

Then there exist constants
a,b>0
such that

\lim_{t\rightarrow\infty}d(c(t),G.c(t))=\triangle_{G}+be^{-at}.

Proof.

We will prove this by an inductive argument on
n
.
Let

V\in T_{e}(\mathsf{P}(n,\mathbb{R}))=\mathsf{S}(n,\mathbb{R})\mbox{ such that }c(t)=\exp(tV).

By conjugating by an element of
\mathsf{SO}(n)
, we may assume that
V
is a diagonal matrix

V=\mathsf{diag}(\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{n})\mbox{
with $\lambda_{1}\geq\dots\geq\lambda_{n}$}.

Let
r_{1},\dots,r_{k}
be the multiplicities of these eigenvalues.
By
[
BH
, 10.64]
,

G=NA

where

•

A
is a block diagonal matrix,

(2.2)

A=\begin{pmatrix}A_{11}&0&\dots&0\\
0&A_{22}&\dots&0\\
\dots&\dots&\dots&\dots\\
0&0&\dots&A_{kk}\end{pmatrix}

•

N\in{\mathsf{N}}_{\xi}
, the horospherical subgroup associated to
\xi\in[c_{0}]
, and
N
is a block upper triangular matrix

(2.3)

N=\begin{pmatrix}I_{r_{1}}&A_{12}&\dots&A_{1k}\\
0&I_{r_{2}}&\dots&A_{2k}\\
\dots&\dots&\dots&\dots\\
0&0&\dots&I_{r_{k}}\end{pmatrix}

where
A_{ij}
is an
(r_{j}\times r_{j})
-matrix and
I_{ij}
is the
(r_{j}\times r_{j})
-identity matrix.

Let
F(c)
be the union of all geodesic lines parallel to
c
. We have that (cf.
[
BH
, 10.67]
)

(2.4)

F(c)=\begin{pmatrix}\mathsf{P}(r_{1},\mathbb{R})&0&\dots&0\\
0&\mathsf{P}(r_{2},\mathbb{R})&\dots&0\\
\dots&\dots&\dots&\dots\\
0&0&\dots&\mathsf{P}(r_{k},\mathbb{R})\end{pmatrix}.

We consider two cases:

Case 1.

The eigenvalues
\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{n}
of
V
are distinct
; i.e.
r_{i}=1
for all
i
.

The assumption implies that
c(t)=\exp(tV)
is a regular geodesic (cf.
[
BH
, 10.45]
). Moreover,
F(c)
is the unique maximal flat containing
c(t)
,
A
is a diagonal matrix
\mathsf{diag}(\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{n})
and acts on
F(c)
by translation (cf.
[
BH
, 10.64, 10.67, and 10.68]
). In particular,
t\mapsto d(c(t),A.c(t))
is a constant function in
t
. Thus,

(2.5)

d(c(t),A.c(t))=d(c(0),A.c(0))=d(e,A.e)=\rho(A)=\rho(G).

Furthermore, letting
\hat{c}(t):=A.c(t)
, we have

\displaystyle d(\hat{c}(t),N.\hat{c}(t))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle d(\hat{c}(t),\,N\hat{c}(t)N^{T})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle d(\hat{c}(t)^{\frac{1}{2}}\hat{c}(t)^{\frac{1}{2}},\ N\hat{c}(t)^{\frac{1}{2}}\hat{c}(t)^{\frac{1}{2}}N^{T})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle d(e,\,(\hat{c}(t)^{-\frac{1}{2}}N\hat{c}(t)^{\frac{1}{2}})e(\hat{c}(t)^{-\frac{1}{2}}N\hat{c}(t)^{\frac{1}{2}})^{T})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle d(e,\,(\hat{c}(t)^{-\frac{1}{2}}N\hat{c}(t)^{\frac{1}{2}}).e).

Since
\hat{c}(t)
is a diagonal matrix with diagonal entries
b_{1}^{2}e^{t\lambda_{1}},b_{2}^{2}e^{t\lambda_{2}},\dots,b_{n}^{2}e^{t\lambda_{n}}
and
N
is an upper triangular matrix with diagonal entries equal to 1, there exists
C_{1},C_{2}>0
such that

\|e-\hat{c}(t)^{-\frac{1}{2}}N\hat{c}(t)^{\frac{1}{2}}\|\leq C_{1}e^{-C_{2}t}

Note that the choice of
V
having distinct diagonal entries is used in the estimate above. Indeed, the constant
C_{2}
is chosen less than
\min\{\lambda_{i}-\lambda_{i+1}:i=1,\dots,n-1\}>0
.
We thus conclude

(2.6)

d(\hat{c}(t),N.\hat{c}(t))\leq be^{-at}

for some
a,b>0
.
Combining this with (
3.2
), the triangle inequality implies that

(2.7)

d(c(t),G.c(t))\leq d(c(t),\hat{c}(t))+d(\hat{c}(t),N.\hat{c}(t))\leq\rho(G)+be^{-at}.

Case 2:

The eigenvalues of
V
are not distinct.

The assumption implies that
c(t)=\exp(tV)
is a singular geodesic (cf.
[
BH
, 10.45]
). We write

c(t)=\begin{pmatrix}c_{1}(t)&0&\dots&0\\
0&c_{2}(t)&\dots&0\\
\dots&\dots&\dots&\dots\\
0&0&\dots&c_{k}(t)\end{pmatrix}.

where
c_{i}(t)
is a geodesic ray in
\mathsf{P}(r_{i},\mathbb{R})
, the block diagonal entry that appear in (
2.4
). If
c_{i}(t)
is not constant, then the block diagonal
A_{ii}
of (
2.2
) is an element of parabolic subgroup associated to
\xi_{i}=[c_{i}]\in\partial\mathsf{P}(r_{i},\mathbb{R})
by
[
BH
, 10.64]
. By the induction hypothesis, there exists a geodesic ray
c_{i}:[0,\infty)\rightarrow\partial\mathsf{P}(r_{i},\mathbb{R})
such that

d(c_{i}(t),A_{ii}.c_{i}(t))\leq\rho(A_{ii})+b_{i}e^{-a_{i}t}.

If
c(t)
is a constant, then
c(t)=e
is a fixed point of
A_{ii}
.
Thus,
there exist
\alpha,\beta>0
such that

d(c(t),A.c(t))\leq\rho(A)+\beta e^{-\alpha t}.

By an analogous proof as in (
2.6
), there exist constants
\bar{\alpha},\bar{\beta}>0
such that

d(A.c(t),(NA).c(t))\leq\bar{\beta}e^{-\bar{\alpha}t}.

Let
a=\min\{\alpha,\bar{\alpha}\}
,
b=\beta+\bar{\beta}
. By the triangle inequality,

d(c(t),G.c(t))\leq d(c(t),A.c(t))+d(A.c(t),G.c(t))\leq\rho(A)+be^{-at}.

∎

Theorem 2.6
.

Let
\tilde{X}
be a irreducible symmetric space of non-compact type. For
I\in\mathsf{Isom}(\tilde{X})
, there exist a geodesic line
c:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\tilde{X}
and constants
a,b>0
such that

d(c(t),I(c(t)))\leq\triangle_{I}+be^{-at}.

Proof.

After scaling, we can assume that
\tilde{X}
is a totally geodesic submanifold of
\mathsf{P}(n,\mathbb{R})
(cf.
[
Mo
, 2.6, pp. 14-16]
or
[
Eb
, appendix]
) and
\mathsf{Isom}(\tilde{X})=\{I=G|_{\tilde{X}}:G\in\mathsf{GL}(n,\mathbb{R}),G.\tilde{X}=\tilde{X}\}
.
Let
I=G|_{\tilde{X}}\in\mathsf{Isom}(\tilde{X})
. Then
I
semisimple if and only if
G
is semisimple in
\mathsf{GL}(n,\mathbb{R})
(cf.
[
BH
, 10.61]
). Thus, the assertion follows from Theorem
3.5
.
∎

We have therefore shown the following.

Theorem 2.7
.

If
\tilde{X}
is an irreducible symmetric space of non-compact type, then every isometry
I\in\mathsf{Isom}(\tilde{X})
has exponential decay.

2.2.
CAT(
-1
) spaces

In the following important cases, every isometry has exponential decay.

Theorem 2.8
.

If
\tilde{X}
is a CAT
(-1)
space, then every isometry
I:\tilde{X}\rightarrow\tilde{X}
has exponential decay.

Proof.

Let
I
be a parabolic isometry and
c_{0}:[0,\infty)\rightarrow\tilde{X}
be a geodesic ray such that

\lim_{t\rightarrow\infty}d(c_{0}(t),I(c_{0}(t)))=\Delta_{I}=\inf_{t\in[0,\infty)}d(c_{0}(t),I(c_{0}(t))).

Let
c_{1}(t)=I(c_{0}(t))
. For each
R\in[0,\infty)
, let
p=c_{0}(0)
,
q=c_{0}(R)
,
r=I(c_{0}(R))
,
s=c_{1}(0)
and
\tilde{p}
,
\tilde{q}
,
\tilde{r}
,
\tilde{s}
be the comparison quadrilateral in
\mathbb{H}^{2}
to the quadrilateral
(p,q,r,s)
in
\tilde{X}
(cf.
[
Re
]
or
[
KS1
, Theorem 2.1.1]
).
Let
\tilde{c}_{0}^{R}
(
\tilde{c}_{1}^{R}
resp.) be the ray geodesic emanating from
\tilde{p}
(
\tilde{s}
resp.)and passing through
\tilde{q}
(
\tilde{r}
resp.). Let
\tilde{\gamma}_{0}
(
\tilde{\gamma}_{1}
resp.) be the geodesic from
\tilde{p}
to
\tilde{s}
(
\tilde{q}
to
\tilde{r}
resp.).
By the quadrilateral comparison (cf.
[
Re
]
or
[
KS1
, Theorem 2.1.2]
),

d(c_{0}(t),c_{1}(t))\leq d(\tilde{c}_{0}^{R}(t),\tilde{c}_{1}^{R}(t)),\ \ \forall t\in[0,R].

Without loss of generality, we may assume
\tilde{c}_{0}^{R}(0)=\tilde{c}_{0}^{R^{\prime}}(0)
and
\tilde{c}_{1}^{R}(0)=\tilde{c}_{1}^{R^{\prime}}(0)
for all
R,R^{\prime}\in[0,\infty)
. Arzela-Ascoli then implies that there exists
R_{j}\rightarrow\infty
and geodesic rays
\tilde{c}_{0}
,
\tilde{c}_{1}=I.\tilde{c}_{0}
such that
\tilde{c}_{0}^{R}\rightarrow\tilde{c}_{0}
and
\tilde{c}_{1}^{R}\rightarrow\tilde{c}_{1}
on every compact subsets of
[0,\infty)
. Thus, by Theorem
2.7
for
M=\mathbb{H}^{2}
,

d(c_{0}(t),c_{1}(t))\leq d(\tilde{c}_{0}(t),\tilde{c}_{1}(t))\leq\Delta_{I}+be^{-at}.

∎

3.
Exponential decay of a commuting pair of isometries

In this subsection, we consider the notion of exponential decay for a commuting pair of isometries
I_{m}:\tilde{X}\rightarrow\tilde{X}
,
m=1,2
.

Definition 3.1
.

We say a commuting pair
I_{1},I_{2}\in\mathsf{Isom}(\tilde{X})
has
exponential decay to their translation length
(or
exponential decay
for short) if one of the following conditions hold:

•

I_{1}
and
I_{2}
are semisimple, or

•

I_{1}
and
I_{2}
are parabolic and there exist an arclength parameterized geodesic ray
c:[0,\infty)\rightarrow\tilde{X}
and constants
a,b>0
such that

d^{2}(I_{m}(c(t)),c(t))\leq\Delta_{I_{m}}^{2}(1+be^{-at}),\ \ m=1,2.

Remark 3.2
.

The following is due to
[
FSY
]
.
If
\tilde{X}
is locally compact,
I_{1},I_{2}\in\mathsf{Isom}(\tilde{X})
commute and
I_{1}
is parabolic, then
I_{2}
is also parabolic and fix the same point at infinity.
Thus, commuting isometries are always both semisimple or both parabolic. In the latter case, there exists a geodesic ray
c:[0,\infty)\rightarrow\infty
such that

\lim_{t\rightarrow\infty}d(I_{m}(c(t)),c(t))=\Delta_{I_{m}},\ \ m=1,2.

Lemma 3.3
.

If the subgroup
\langle I_{1},I_{2}\rangle\subset\mathsf{Isom}(\tilde{X})
is generated by commuting semisimple isometries
I_{1},I_{2}
and

\rho:2\pi\mathbb{Z}\times 2\pi\mathbb{Z}\rightarrow\Gamma

is a homomorphism defined by
(2\pi,0),(0,2\pi)\in 2\pi\mathbb{Z}\times 2\pi\mathbb{Z}
mapping to
I_{1},I_{2}
respectively, then
there exists a totally geodesic
\rho
-equivariant map

h:\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\tilde{X}

such that

\left|\frac{\partial h}{\partial x}\right|^{2}=\frac{\Delta_{I_{1}}^{2}}{4\pi^{2}},\ \ \ \left|\frac{\partial h}{\partial y}\right|=\frac{\Delta_{I_{2}}^{2}}{4\pi^{2}}

where
(x,y)
are the standard coordinates of
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}
.

Proof.

By
[
BH
, II.7.20]
, we can choose
P_{*}\in\mathsf{Min}(I_{1})\cap\mathsf{Min}(I_{2})
. We consider the following cases:

•

Assume
I_{1}
and
I_{2}
are both elliptic. Then let
h(x,y)
be identically equal to
P_{*}
.

•

Assume
I_{1}
is hyperbolic and
I_{2}
is elliptic. Then
P_{*}
is a fixed point of
I_{2}
. Let
c_{1}:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\tilde{X}
be an arclength parameterized geodesic such that
c_{1}(0)=P_{*}
and
c_{1}(\mathbb{R})
is an axis of
I_{1}
.
By commutativity,

I_{2}\circ I_{1}^{k}(P_{*})=I_{1}^{k}\circ I_{2}(P_{*})=I_{1}^{k}(P_{*}),\ \ \forall k\in\mathbb{Z}.

Thus,
c_{1}(k\Delta_{I_{1}})=I_{1}^{k}(P_{*})
is a fixed point of
I_{2}
for any
k\in\mathbb{Z}
which implies that every point of
c_{1}(\mathbb{R})
is a fixed point of
I_{2}
. Define
h(x,y)=c_{1}(\frac{\Delta_{I}}{2\pi}x)
.

•

Assume
I_{1}
and
I_{2}
are both hyperbolic. Then
\langle I_{1},I_{2}\rangle
is properly discontinuous, and we can apply the flat torus theorem (cf.
[
BH
, II.7.1]
) to assert the existence of a
\rho
-equivariant totally geodesic map from
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}
to
\tilde{X}
. After an appropriate reparameterization, we obtain a map
h
with the desired derivative estimates.

∎

Lemma 3.4
.

If the subgroup
\langle I_{1},I_{2}\rangle\subset\mathsf{Isom}(\tilde{X})
is generated by commuting parabolic isometries
I_{1},I_{2}
with exponential decay and

\rho:2\pi\mathbb{Z}\times 2\pi\mathbb{Z}\rightarrow\Gamma

is a homomorphism defined by
(2\pi,0),(0,2\pi)\in 2\pi\mathbb{Z}\times 2\pi\mathbb{Z}
mapping to
I_{1},I_{2}
respectively, then we have the following: there exist
a,b>0
and a
map

\tilde{h}:[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\tilde{X}

such that for each
t\in[0,\infty)
,
\tilde{h}_{t}(x,y):=\tilde{h}(t,x,y)
is a
\rho
-equivariant map and

\left|\frac{\partial\tilde{h}}{\partial t}\right|^{2}\leq 1,\ \ \ \left|\frac{\partial\tilde{h}}{\partial x}\right|^{2}\leq\frac{\Delta_{I_{1}}^{2}}{4\pi^{2}}(1+be^{-at}),\ \ \ \left|\frac{\partial\tilde{h}}{\partial y}\right|\leq\frac{\Delta_{I_{2}}^{2}}{4\pi^{2}}(1+be^{-at}).

Proof.

Let
c(t)
be as in Definition
3.1
. Let

\displaystyle\tilde{h}(t,0,0)=c(t),

\displaystyle\tilde{h}(t,2\pi,0)=I_{1}(c(t)),

\displaystyle\tilde{h}(t,0,2\pi)=I_{2}(t)

\displaystyle\tilde{h}(t,2\pi,2\pi)=I_{1}\circ I_{2}(c(t))=I_{2}\circ I_{1}(c(t)).

For each
t\in[0,\infty)
, let

\displaystyle x\in[0,2\pi]

\displaystyle\mapsto

\displaystyle\tilde{h}(t,x,0)

\displaystyle x\in[0,2\pi]

\displaystyle\mapsto

\displaystyle\tilde{h}(t,x,2\pi)

be geodesics.
For each
x\in[0,2\pi]
, let

y\mapsto\tilde{h}(t,x,y)

be a geodesic.
Finally, we extend
\tilde{h}_{t}(x,y)
equivariantly from
[0,2\pi]\times[0,2\pi]
to
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}
.

The assumption that the commuting pair
I_{1}
and
I_{2}
has exponential decay along with multiple applications of the quadrilateral comparison property of NPC spaces imply the derivative estimates.
∎

3.1.
Symmetric spaces of non-compact type

In this section, we prove that every pair of commuting isometries of a symmetric space of non-compact type has exponential decay.

Theorem 3.5
.

Let

•

G_{1},G_{2}\in{\mathsf{G}}_{\xi}
, and

•

c:[0,\infty)\rightarrow\mathsf{P}(n,\mathbb{R})
be the geodesic ray such that
\xi=[c]
and
c(0)=e
.

Then there exist constants
a,b>0
such that

\displaystyle\lim_{t\rightarrow\infty}d(c(t),G_{m}.c(t))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\triangle_{G_{m}}+be^{-at},\ \ m=1,2.

Proof.

We will prove this by an inductive argument on
n
.
Let

V\in T_{e}(\mathsf{P}(n,\mathbb{R}))=\mathsf{S}(n,\mathbb{R})\mbox{ such that }c(t)=\exp(tV).

By conjugating by an element of
\mathsf{SO}(n)
, we may assume that
V
is a diagonal matrix

V=\mathsf{diag}(\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{n})\mbox{
with $\lambda_{1}\geq\dots\geq\lambda_{n}$}.

Let
r_{1},\dots,r_{k}
be the multiplicities of these eigenvalues.
By
[
BH
, 10.64]
,

G_{1}=N_{1}A_{1}\ \mbox{ and }\ G_{2}=N_{2}A_{2}

where for
m=1,2
:

•

A_{m},
is a block diagonal matrix as in (
2.2
) which we write as

(3.1)

A_{m}=\begin{pmatrix}A_{m,11}&0&\dots&0\\
0&A_{m,22}&\dots&0\\
\dots&\dots&\dots&\dots\\
0&0&\dots&A_{m,kk}\end{pmatrix}

•

N\in{\mathsf{N}}_{\xi}
, the horospherical subgroup associated to
\xi\in[c_{0}]
, and
N
is a block upper triangular matrix as in (
2.3
).

Let
F(c)
be the union of all geodesic lines parallel to
c
as in (
2.4
).
We consider two cases:

Case 1.

The eigenvalues
\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{n}
of
V
are distinct
; i.e.
r_{i}=1
for all
i
.

The assumption implies that
c(t)=\exp(tV)
is a regular geodesic (cf.
[
BH
, 10.45]
). Moreover,
F(c)
is the unique maximal flat containing
c(t)
and
A_{m}
acts on
F(c)
by translation (cf.
[
BH
, 10.64, 10.67, and 10.68]
). In particular,
t\mapsto d(c(t),A_{m}.c(t))
is a constant function in
t
. Thus,

(3.2)

d(c(t),A_{m}.c(t))=d(c(0),A_{m}.c(0))=d(e,A_{m}.e)=\rho(A_{m})=\rho(G_{m}).

Furthermore, letting
\hat{c}(t):=A_{m}.c(t)
, we have

\displaystyle d(\hat{c}(t),N_{m}.\hat{c}(t))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle d(\hat{c}(t),\,N_{m}\hat{c}(t)N_{m}^{T})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle d(\hat{c}(t)^{\frac{1}{2}}\hat{c}(t)^{\frac{1}{2}},\ N_{m}\hat{c}(t)^{\frac{1}{2}}\hat{c}(t)^{\frac{1}{2}}N_{m}^{T})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle d(e,\,(\hat{c}(t)^{-\frac{1}{2}}N_{m}\hat{c}(t)^{\frac{1}{2}})e(\hat{c}(t)^{-\frac{1}{2}}N_{m}\hat{c}(t)^{\frac{1}{2}})^{T})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle d(e,\,(\hat{c}(t)^{-\frac{1}{2}}N_{m}\hat{c}(t)^{\frac{1}{2}}).e).

Since
\hat{c}(t)
is a diagonal matrix with diagonal entries
b_{1}^{2}e^{t\lambda_{1}},b_{2}^{2}e^{t\lambda_{2}},\dots,b_{n}^{2}e^{t\lambda_{n}}
and
N_{m}
is an upper triangular matrix with diagonal entries equal to 1, there exists
C_{1},C_{2}>0
such that

\|e-\hat{c}(t)^{-\frac{1}{2}}N_{m}\hat{c}(t)^{\frac{1}{2}}\|\leq C_{1}e^{-C_{2}t}

Note that the choice of
V
having distinct diagonal entries is used in the estimate above. Indeed, the constant
C_{2}
is chosen less than
\min\{\lambda_{i}-\lambda_{i+1}:i=1,\dots,n-1\}>0
.
We thus conclude

(3.3)

d(\hat{c}(t),N_{m}.\hat{c}(t))\leq b_{m}e^{-a_{m}t}

for some
a_{m},b_{m}>0
.
Combining this with (
3.2
), the triangle inequality implies that

(3.4)

d(c(t),G_{m}.c(t))\leq d(c(t),\hat{c}(t))+d(\hat{c}(t),N_{m}.\hat{c}(t))\leq\rho(G_{m})+b_{m}e^{-a_{m}t}.

Choose
a=\min\{a_{1},a_{2}\}
and
b=\max\{b_{1},b_{2}\}
.

Case 2:

The eigenvalues of
V
are not distinct.

The assumption implies that
c(t)=\exp(tV)
is a singular geodesic (cf.
[
BH
, 10.45]
). We write

c(t)=\begin{pmatrix}c_{1}(t)&0&\dots&0\\
0&c_{2}(t)&\dots&0\\
\dots&\dots&\dots&\dots\\
0&0&\dots&c_{k}(t)\end{pmatrix}.

where
c_{i}(t)
is a geodesic ray in
\mathsf{P}(r_{i},\mathbb{R})
, the block diagonal entry that appear in (
2.4
). If
c_{i}(t)
is not constant, then the block diagonal
A_{m,ii}
of (
3.1
) is an element of parabolic subgroup associated to
\xi_{i}=[c_{i}]\in\partial\mathsf{P}(r_{i},\mathbb{R})
by
[
BH
, 10.64]
. By the induction hypothesis, there exists a geodesic ray
c_{i}:[0,\infty)\rightarrow\partial\mathsf{P}(r_{i},\mathbb{R})
such that

d(c_{i}(t),A_{m,ii}.c_{i}(t))\leq\rho(A_{m,ii})+b_{i}e^{-a_{i}t}

If
c(t)
is a constant, then
c(t)=e
is a fixed point of
A_{m,ii}
.
Thus,
there exists
\alpha_{m},\beta_{m}>0
such that

d(c(t),A_{m}.c(t))\leq\rho(A_{m})+\beta_{m}e^{-\alpha_{m}t}.

By an analogous proof as in (
3.3
), there exist constants
\bar{\alpha}_{m},\bar{\beta}_{m}>0
such that

d(A_{m}.c(t),(N_{m}A_{m}).c(t))\leq\bar{\beta}_{m}e^{-\bar{\alpha}_{m}t}.

Let
a_{m}=\min\{\alpha_{m},\bar{\alpha}_{m}\}
,
b_{m}=\beta+\bar{\beta}
. By triangle inequality,

d(c(t),G_{m}.c(t))\leq d(c(t),A_{m}.c(t))+d(A_{m}.c(t),G_{m}.c(t))\leq\rho(A_{m})+b_{m}e^{-a_{m}t}.

Choose
a=\min\{a_{1},a_{2}\}
and
b=\max\{b_{1},b_{2}\}

∎

Theorem 3.6
.

If
\tilde{X}
is a irreducible symmetric space of non-compact type, then every pair of commuting isometries has exponential decay.

Proof.

The proof is similar to the proof of Theorem
2.6
.
∎

3.2.
CAT(
-1
) spaces

Theorem 3.7
.

If
\tilde{X}
is a CAT(
-1
), then every pair of commuting isometries has exponential decay.

Proof.

Analogously to the proof of Theorem
2.8
, the assertion follows from comparing
\tilde{X}
to
\mathbb{H}^{2}
.
∎

Chapter 2
Bochner Formulae

The goal of this chapter is to prove variations of the Siu’s and Sampson’s Bochner formulas. These formulas are contained in Theorem
4.9
and Theorem
5.9
and are inspired by Mochizuki’s formula in
[
M
, Proposition 21.42]
.

4.
Between Kähler manifolds

In
[
Siu1
]
, Siu introduced the following notion of negative curvature.

Definition 4.1
.

For local holomorphic coordinates
(w^{i})
of a Kähler manifold
X
, the curvature tensor component is

R_{i\bar{j}l\bar{k}}=\frac{\partial^{2}h_{l\bar{k}}}{\partial w^{i}\partial\bar{w}^{j}}+h^{p\bar{q}}\frac{\partial h_{l\bar{q}}}{\partial w^{i}}\frac{\partial h_{p\bar{k}}}{\partial\bar{w}^{j}}.

For
V=(V^{i})
and
W=(W^{i})
, let

R(V,W)=-\frac{1}{2}R_{i\bar{j}l\bar{k}}(V^{i}\overline{W^{j}}-W^{i}\overline{V^{j}})\overline{(V^{l}\overline{W^{k}}-W^{l}\overline{V^{k}})}

We say
X
has
strongly negative (resp. strongly non-positive) curvature in the sense of Siu

[
Siu1
]
if

R(V,W)<0
(resp.
R(V,W)\leq 0
)
for any nonzero
n\times n
complex matrix
(V^{i}\overline{W^{j}}-W^{i}\overline{V^{j}})
.

Remark 4.2
.

It is well known that a Kähler symmetric space of non-compact type has strongly non-positive curvature in the sense of Siu (cf.
[
Siu1
]
).

Lemma 4.3
.

Let
X
be a Kähler manifold with Kähler form
\omega
and strongly non-positive curvature in the sense of Siu. Let
M
be another Kähler manifold and
u:M\rightarrow X
be a smooth map.
If the function
Q:M\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is defined by setting

\displaystyle Q\frac{\omega^{n}}{n!}=-R_{i\bar{j}l\bar{k}}\partial u^{l}\wedge\bar{\partial}\bar{u}^{k}\wedge\bar{\partial}u^{i}\wedge\partial\bar{u}^{j}\wedge\frac{\omega^{n-2}}{(n-2)!},

then
Q\geq 0
.

Proof.

Follows from
[
Siu1
, Proposition 3, (4.2), and (4.3)]
.
∎

Let
(M,g)
and
(X,h)
be Kähler manifolds.
For a smooth map
u:M\rightarrow X
,
let

(4.1)

E:=u^{-1}(TX\otimes\mathbb{C}).

Let

E=E^{\prime}\oplus E^{\prime\prime}\ \mbox{ where }\ E^{\prime}:=u^{-1}(T^{(1,0)}X),\ \ E^{\prime\prime}:=u^{-1}(T^{(0,1)}X).

Denote by
\Omega^{p,q}(E)
,
\Omega^{p,q}(E^{\prime})
and
\Omega^{p,q}(E^{\prime\prime})
the space of
E
-,
E^{\prime}
- and
E^{\prime\prime}
-valued
(p,q)
-forms respectively.
If
(w^{i})
are local holomorphic coordinates in
X
, then

\{\frac{\partial}{\partial u^{i}}:=\frac{\partial}{\partial w^{i}}\circ u,\ \frac{\partial}{\partial\bar{u}^{i}}:=\frac{\partial}{\partial\bar{w}^{i}}\circ u\}

is a local frame of
E
.
Following
[
Siu1
]
, denote the natural projections and inclusions by

\displaystyle\Pi_{(1,0)}:TX\otimes\mathbb{C}\rightarrow T^{(1,0)}X,

\displaystyle\Pi_{(0,1)}:TX\otimes\mathbb{C}\rightarrow T^{(0,1)}X,

\displaystyle\mbox{I}_{(1,0)}:T^{(1,0)}M\hookrightarrow TM\otimes\mathbb{C},

\displaystyle\mbox{I}_{(0,1)}:T^{(0,1)}M\hookrightarrow TM\otimes\mathbb{C}.

The differential
du:TM\rightarrow TX
gives rise to a map

du\otimes\mathbb{C}:TM\otimes\mathbb{C}\rightarrow TX\otimes\mathbb{C}.

Define

\displaystyle\partial u=\Pi_{(1,0)}\circ du\otimes\mathbb{C}\circ\mbox{I}_{(1,0)}\in\Omega^{(1,0)}(E^{\prime})

\displaystyle\bar{\partial}u=\Pi_{(1,0)}\circ du\otimes\mathbb{C}\circ\mbox{I}_{(0,1)}\in\Omega^{(0,1)}(E^{\prime})

\displaystyle\partial\bar{u}=\Pi_{(0,1)}\circ du\otimes\mathbb{C}\circ\mbox{I}_{(1,0)}\in\Omega^{(1,0)}(E^{\prime\prime})

\displaystyle\bar{\partial}\bar{u}=\Pi_{(0,1)}\circ du\otimes\mathbb{C}\circ\mbox{I}_{(0,1)}\in\Omega^{(0,1)}(E^{\prime\prime}).

In local holomorphic coordinates of
M
and
X
,

\displaystyle\partial u=\partial u^{i}\frac{\partial}{\partial u^{i}}

\displaystyle\bar{\partial}u=\bar{\partial}u^{i}\frac{\partial}{\partial u^{i}}

\displaystyle\partial\bar{u}=\partial\bar{u}^{i}\frac{\partial}{\partial\bar{u}^{i}}

\displaystyle\bar{\partial}\bar{u}=\bar{\partial}\bar{u}^{i}\frac{\partial}{\partial\bar{u}^{i}}

\displaystyle\overline{\partial u}=\bar{\partial}\bar{u}\

\displaystyle\overline{\bar{\partial}u}=\partial\bar{u}.

Let
\partial_{E}
,
\bar{\partial}_{E}
denote the exterior derivatives on
E
induced by the Riemannian connection
\nabla
on
X
.
Since
\nabla
preserves
T^{(1,0)}X
and
T^{(0,1)}X
, they induce exterior derivatives on
E^{\prime}
and
E^{\prime\prime}
denoted by
\partial_{E^{\prime}}
,
\bar{\partial}_{E^{\prime}}
,
\partial_{E^{\prime\prime}}
,
\bar{\partial}_{E^{\prime\prime}}
.

Definition 4.4
.

The curvature operators of
E^{\prime}
and
E^{\prime\prime}
are
R_{E^{\prime}}=(\partial_{E^{\prime}}+\bar{\partial}_{E^{\prime}})^{2}
and
R_{E^{\prime\prime}}=(\partial_{E^{\prime\prime}}+\bar{\partial}_{E^{\prime\prime}})^{2}
respectively.

Definition 4.5
.

Let
\{e^{i}\}
be a local frame of
E
. For

\displaystyle\psi=\psi_{i}e^{i}\in\Omega^{p,q}(E)\ \mbox{and }\ \xi=\xi_{i}e^{i}\in\Omega^{p^{\prime},q^{\prime}}(E),

we set

\{\psi,\xi\}=\langle e^{i},e^{j}\rangle\psi_{i}\wedge\bar{\xi}_{j}\in\Omega^{p+q^{\prime},q+p^{\prime}}

where
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
is the Hermitian metric on
E
induced by identification
TX\simeq T^{(1,0)}X
.

Lemma 4.6
.

With
\{\cdot,\cdot\}
given as in Definition
6
,

\{\partial u,\partial u\}=-\{\bar{\partial}\bar{u},\bar{\partial}\bar{u}\},\ \ \{\partial\bar{u},\partial\bar{u}\}=-\{\bar{\partial}u,\bar{\partial}u\}.

for any smooth map
u:M\rightarrow X
between Kähler manifolds,

Proof.

In local holomorphic coordinates
(z^{\alpha})
on
M
and normal coordinates
(w^{i})
on
X
at
u(p)
,

\displaystyle\{\partial u,\partial u\}=\sum_{i}\frac{\partial{u^{i}}}{{\partial{z^{\beta}}}}\overline{\frac{\partial{u^{i}}}{\partial{z^{\alpha}}}}dz^{\beta}\wedge d\bar{z}^{\alpha}=-\sum_{i}\frac{\partial{\bar{u}^{i}}}{\partial{\bar{z}^{\alpha}}}\overline{\frac{\partial{\bar{u}^{i}}}{{\partial{\bar{z}^{\beta}}}}}d\bar{z}^{\alpha}\wedge dz^{\beta}=-\{\bar{\partial}\bar{u},\bar{\partial}\bar{u}\}

and

\displaystyle\{\partial\bar{u},\partial\bar{u}\}=\sum_{i}\frac{\partial\bar{u}^{i}}{{\partial{z^{\beta}}}}\overline{\frac{\partial\bar{u}^{i}}{\partial{z^{\alpha}}}}dz^{\beta}\wedge d\bar{z}^{\alpha}=-\sum_{i}\frac{\partial u^{i}}{\partial{\bar{z}^{\alpha}}}\overline{\frac{\partial u^{i}}{{\partial{\bar{z}^{\beta}}}}}d\bar{z}^{\alpha}\wedge dz^{\beta}=-\{\bar{\partial}u,\bar{\partial}u\}.

∎

Lemma 4.7
.

For any smooth map
u:M\rightarrow X
between Kähler manifolds, we have

\partial_{E^{\prime}}\bar{\partial}u=-\bar{\partial}_{E^{\prime}}\partial u,\ \ \partial_{E^{\prime\prime}}\bar{\partial}\bar{u}=-\bar{\partial}_{E^{\prime\prime}}\partial\bar{u}

and

\partial_{E^{\prime}}\partial u=0,\ \bar{\partial}_{E^{\prime}}\bar{\partial}u=0,\ \partial_{E^{\prime\prime}}\partial\bar{u}=0,\ \bar{\partial}_{E^{\prime\prime}}\bar{\partial}\bar{u}=0.

Proof.

We will prove the first equality, all the other being similar. In local holomorphic coordinates
(z^{\alpha})
on
M
and normal coordinates
(w^{i})
on
X
at
u(p)
,

\displaystyle\partial_{E^{\prime}}\bar{\partial}u=\frac{\partial^{2}u^{i}}{\partial z^{\alpha}\partial\bar{z}^{\beta}}dz^{\alpha}\wedge d\bar{z}^{\beta}\otimes\frac{\partial}{\partial{u^{i}}}=-\frac{\partial^{2}u^{i}}{\partial\bar{z}^{\beta}\partial z^{\alpha}}d\bar{z}^{\beta}\wedge dz^{\alpha}\otimes\frac{\partial}{\partial{u^{i}}}=-\bar{\partial}_{E^{\prime}}\partial u.

∎

We now state the well-known Siu-Bochner formula
[
Siu1
, Proposition 2]
. For the sake of completeness,
we provide a proof in the appendix to this chapter (cf. Section
6
).

Theorem 4.8

(Siu-Bochner formula)
.

For a harmonic map
u:M\rightarrow X
between Kähler manifolds,

\displaystyle\partial\bar{\partial}\{\bar{\partial}u,\bar{\partial}u\}\wedge\frac{\omega^{n-2}}{(n-2)!}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(4\left|\partial_{E^{\prime}}\bar{\partial}u\right|^{2}+Q\right)\frac{\omega^{n}}{n!}\ =\ \partial\bar{\partial}\{\bar{\partial}\bar{u},\bar{\partial}\bar{u}\}\wedge\frac{\omega^{n-2}}{(n-2)!}.

The following is the variation of the Siu’s Bochner Formula, inspired by the work of Mochizuki (cf.
[
M
, Proposition 21.42]
) for harmonic metrics.

Theorem 4.9
.

For a harmonic map
u:M\rightarrow X
between Kähler manifolds,

\displaystyle d\{\bar{\partial}_{E^{\prime}}\partial u,\bar{\partial}u-\partial u\}\wedge\frac{\omega^{n-2}}{(n-2)!}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(8\left|{\partial}_{E^{\prime}}\bar{\partial}u\right|^{2}+2Q\right)\wedge\frac{\omega^{n}}{n!}.

Proof.

They key observation is that, since
\partial\{\partial_{E^{\prime}}\bar{\partial}u,\bar{\partial}u\}\wedge\frac{\omega^{n-2}}{(n-2)!}
is an
(n+1,n-1)
-form and
\bar{\partial}\{\partial_{E^{\prime}}\bar{\partial}u,\partial u\}\wedge\frac{\omega^{n-2}}{(n-2)!}
is an
(n-1,n+1)
-form, these two forms are both identically equal to zero. Thus, by applying Lemma
4.6
and Lemma
4.7
,

(4.2)

\displaystyle\partial\{\partial_{E^{\prime}}\bar{\partial}u,\partial u-\bar{\partial}u\}\wedge\frac{\omega^{n-2}}{(n-2)!}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\partial\{\partial_{E^{\prime}}\bar{\partial}u,\partial u\}\wedge\frac{\omega^{n-2}}{(n-2)!}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\partial\{\bar{\partial}_{E^{\prime}}\partial u,\partial u\}\wedge\frac{\omega^{n-2}}{(n-2)!}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\partial\bar{\partial}\{\partial u,\partial u\}\wedge\frac{\omega^{n-2}}{(n-2)!}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\partial\bar{\partial}\{\bar{\partial}\bar{u},\bar{\partial}\bar{u}\}\wedge\frac{\omega^{n-2}}{(n-2)!},

(4.3)

\displaystyle\bar{\partial}\{\partial_{E^{\prime}}\bar{\partial}u,\partial u-\bar{\partial}u\}\wedge\frac{\omega^{n-2}}{(n-2)!}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\bar{\partial}\{\partial_{E^{\prime}}\bar{\partial}u,\bar{\partial}u\}\wedge\frac{\omega^{n-2}}{(n-2)!}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\bar{\partial}\partial\{\bar{\partial}u,\bar{\partial}u\}\wedge\frac{\omega^{n-2}}{(n-2)!}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\partial\bar{\partial}\{\bar{\partial}u,\bar{\partial}u\}\wedge\frac{\omega^{n-2}}{(n-2)!}.

Thus, we obtain

\displaystyle d\{\bar{\partial}_{E^{\prime}}\partial u,\bar{\partial}u-\partial u\}\wedge\frac{\omega^{n-2}}{(n-2)!}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle d\{\partial_{E^{\prime}}\bar{\partial}u,\partial u-\bar{\partial}u\}\wedge\frac{\omega^{n-2}}{(n-2)!}\ \ \mbox{(by Lemma~\ref{easycommute})}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\partial+\bar{\partial})\{\partial_{E^{\prime}}\bar{\partial}u,\partial u-\bar{\partial}u\}\wedge\frac{\omega^{n-2}}{(n-2)!}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\partial\bar{\partial}\{\bar{\partial}u,\bar{\partial}u\}+\partial\bar{\partial}\{\bar{\partial}\bar{u},\bar{\partial}\bar{u}\}\right)\wedge\frac{\omega^{n-2}}{(n-2)!}\ \ \mbox{(by (\ref{sb1}) and (\ref{sb2}))}.

Thus, the asserted identity follows from
Theorem
4.8
.
∎

5.
From a Kähler manifold to a Riemannian manifold

In
[
Sa
]
, Sampson introduced the following notion of negative curvature.

Definition 5.1
.

Let
X
be a Riemannian manifold and
R_{ijkl}
be its Riemann curvature tensor.
Then
X
is said to have
Hermitian-negative curvature
if

(5.1)

R_{ijkl}A^{i\bar{l}}A^{j\bar{k}}\leq 0

for any Hermitian semi-positive matrix
A=(A^{i\bar{l}})
.

Remark 5.2
.

It is well known that symmetric spaces of non-compact type have Hermitian-negative curvature. For an exposition, see
[
Lo
, Chapter 5]
.

Let
(M,g)
be Kähler manifold,
(X,h)
be a Riemannian manifold and
u:M\rightarrow X
be a smooth map.
Again consider the pullback vector bundle

(5.2)

E:=u^{-1}(TX\otimes\mathbb{C})

and denote by
\Omega^{p,q}(E)
the space of
E
-valued
(p,q)
-forms respectively.
If
(x^{i})
are local coordinates in
X
, then

\{\frac{\partial}{\partial u^{i}}:=\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{i}}\circ u\}

is a local frame of
E
.

Definition 5.3
.

Let

\displaystyle\mbox{I}_{(1,0)}:T^{(1,0)}M\hookrightarrow TM\otimes\mathbb{C},

\displaystyle\mbox{I}_{(0,1)}:T^{(0,1)}M\hookrightarrow TM\otimes\mathbb{C}.

be the inclusions and define

\displaystyle\partial u=du\circ\mbox{I}_{(1,0)}\in\Omega^{(1,0)}(E),

\displaystyle\bar{\partial}u=du\circ\mbox{I}_{(0,1)}\in\Omega^{(0,1)}(E).

In local holomorphic coordinates of
M
,

\displaystyle\partial u=\partial u^{i}\frac{\partial}{\partial u^{i}}

\displaystyle\bar{\partial}u=\bar{\partial}u^{i}\frac{\partial}{\partial u^{i}}.

Let
\partial_{E}
,
\bar{\partial}_{E}
denote the exterior derivatives on
E
induced by the Riemannian connection
\nabla
on
X
.

Definition 5.4
.

The curvature operator of
E
is
R_{E}=(\partial_{E}+\bar{\partial}_{E})^{2}
.

Definition 5.5
.

Let
\{e^{i}\}
be a local frame of
E
. For

\displaystyle\psi=\psi_{i}e^{i}\in\Omega^{p,q}(E)\ \mbox{and }\ \xi=\xi_{i}e^{i}\in\Omega^{p^{\prime},q^{\prime}}(E),

we set

\{\psi,\xi\}=\langle e^{i},e^{j}\rangle\psi_{i}\wedge\bar{\xi}_{j}\in\Omega^{p+q^{\prime},q+p^{\prime}}

where
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
is the pullback to
E
of the Hermitian metric defined by extending the Riemannian metric
h
of
X
.

Lemma 5.6
.

With
\{\cdot,\cdot\}
given as in Definition
6
,

\{\partial u,\partial u\}=-\{\bar{\partial}u,\bar{\partial}u\}.

for any smooth map
u:M\rightarrow X
.

Proof.

In local holomorphic coordinates
(z^{\alpha})
on
M
and normal coordinates
(x^{i})
on
X
at
u(p)
,

\displaystyle\{\partial u,\partial u\}=\sum_{i}\frac{\partial{u^{i}}}{{\partial{z^{\beta}}}}\overline{\frac{\partial{u^{i}}}{\partial{z^{\alpha}}}}dz^{\beta}\wedge d\bar{z}^{\alpha}=-\sum_{i}\frac{\partial{u^{i}}}{\partial{\bar{z}^{\alpha}}}\overline{\frac{\partial{u^{i}}}{{\partial{\bar{z}^{\beta}}}}}d\bar{z}^{\alpha}\wedge dz^{\beta}=-\{\bar{\partial}u,\bar{\partial}u\}

∎

Lemma 5.7
.

For any smooth map
u:M\rightarrow X
, we have

\partial_{E}\bar{\partial}u=-\bar{\partial}_{E}\partial u,\ \ \partial_{E}\partial u=0,\ \bar{\partial}_{E}\bar{\partial}u=0.

Proof.

We will prove the first equality, all the other being similar. In local holomorphic coordinates
(z^{\alpha})
on
M
and normal coordinates
(x^{i})
on
X
at
u(p)
,

\displaystyle\partial_{E^{\prime}}\bar{\partial}u=\frac{\partial^{2}u^{i}}{\partial z^{\alpha}\partial\bar{z}^{\beta}}dz^{\alpha}\wedge d\bar{z}^{\beta}\otimes\frac{\partial}{\partial{u^{i}}}=-\frac{\partial^{2}u^{i}}{\partial\bar{z}^{\beta}\partial z^{\alpha}}d\bar{z}^{\beta}\wedge dz^{\alpha}\otimes\frac{\partial}{\partial{u^{i}}}=-\bar{\partial}_{E^{\prime}}\partial u.

∎

The following version of Sampson’s Bochner formula is contained in
[
LY
, Lemma 6.10]
.

Theorem 5.8

(Sampson’s Bochner formula)
.

For a harmonic map
u:(M,g)\rightarrow(X,h)
from a Kähler manifold to a Riemannian manifold,

(5.3)

\displaystyle\partial\bar{\partial}\{\bar{\partial}u,\bar{\partial}u\}\wedge\frac{\omega^{n-2}}{(n-2)!}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(4\left|\partial_{E}\bar{\partial}u\right|^{2}+4Q_{0}\right)\frac{\omega^{n}}{n!}

where

Q_{0}=-2g^{\alpha\bar{\delta}}g^{\gamma\bar{\beta}}R_{ijkl}\frac{\partial u^{i}}{\partial z^{\alpha}}\frac{\partial u^{k}}{\partial\bar{z}^{\beta}}\frac{\partial u^{j}}{\partial z^{\gamma}}\frac{\partial u^{l}}{\partial\bar{z}^{\delta}}

in local coordinates
(z^{\alpha})
of
M
and
(x^{i})
of
X
.

By applying a similar proof as Theorem
4.9
, we obtain a generalization the following.

Theorem 5.9
.

For a harmonic map
u:M\rightarrow X
from a Kähler manifold to a Riemannian manifold,

\displaystyle d\{\bar{\partial}_{E}\partial u,\bar{\partial}u-\partial u\}\wedge\frac{\omega^{n-2}}{(n-2)!}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(8\left|{\partial}_{E}\bar{\partial}u\right|^{2}+8Q_{0}\right)\wedge\frac{\omega^{n}}{n!}.

Proof.

We apply a similar argument as in the proof of Theorem
4.9
. Again, we use the vanishing
\partial\{\partial_{E^{\prime}}\bar{\partial}u,\bar{\partial}u\}\wedge\frac{\omega^{n-2}}{(n-2)!}=0=\bar{\partial}\{\partial_{E^{\prime}}\bar{\partial}u,\partial u\}\wedge\frac{\omega^{n-2}}{(n-2)!}
to show

(5.4)

\displaystyle\partial\{\partial_{E^{\prime}}\bar{\partial}u,\partial u-\bar{\partial}u\}\wedge\frac{\omega^{n-2}}{(n-2)!}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\partial\{\partial_{E^{\prime}}\bar{\partial}u,\partial u\}\wedge\frac{\omega^{n-2}}{(n-2)!}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\partial\{\bar{\partial}_{E^{\prime}}\partial u,\partial u\}\wedge\frac{\omega^{n-2}}{(n-2)!}\ \ \ \mbox{(by Lemma~\ref{easycommute'})}.

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\partial\bar{\partial}\{\partial u,\partial u\}\wedge\frac{\omega^{n-2}}{(n-2)!}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\partial\bar{\partial}\{\bar{\partial}u,\bar{\partial}u\}\wedge\frac{\omega^{n-2}}{(n-2)!}\ \ \ \mbox{(by Lemma~\ref{form4'})},

(5.5)

\displaystyle\bar{\partial}\{\partial_{E^{\prime}}\bar{\partial}u,\partial u-\bar{\partial}u\}\wedge\frac{\omega^{n-2}}{(n-2)!}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\bar{\partial}\{\partial_{E^{\prime}}\bar{\partial}u,\bar{\partial}u\}\wedge\frac{\omega^{n-2}}{(n-2)!}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\bar{\partial}\partial\{\bar{\partial}u,\bar{\partial}u\}\wedge\frac{\omega^{n-2}}{(n-2)!}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\partial\bar{\partial}\{\bar{\partial}u,\bar{\partial}u\}\wedge\frac{\omega^{n-2}}{(n-2)!}.

Thus,

\displaystyle d\{\bar{\partial}_{E^{\prime}}\partial u,\bar{\partial}u-\partial u\}\wedge\frac{\omega^{n-2}}{(n-2)!}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle d\{\partial_{E^{\prime}}\bar{\partial}u,\partial u-\bar{\partial}u\}\wedge\frac{\omega^{n-2}}{(n-2)!}\ \ \mbox{(by Lemma~\ref{easycommute'})}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\partial+\bar{\partial})\{\partial_{E^{\prime}}\bar{\partial}u,\partial u-\bar{\partial}u\}\wedge\frac{\omega^{n-2}}{(n-2)!}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\partial\bar{\partial}\{\bar{\partial}u,\bar{\partial}u\}\wedge\frac{\omega^{n-2}}{(n-2)!}\ \ \mbox{(by (\ref{sb1'}) and (\ref{sb2'}))}.

Thus, the asserted identity follows from
Theorem
5.8
.
∎

6.
Appendix to Chapter
2

In this section, we provide a proof of Siu’s Bochner formula for the sake of completeness.
First, by a simple computation (cf.
[
LY
]
), for
\psi\in\Omega^{p,q}(E)
and
\xi\in\Omega^{p^{\prime},q^{\prime}}(E)
,

\displaystyle\partial\{\psi,\xi\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\{\partial_{E}\psi,\xi\}+(-1)^{p+q}\{\psi,\bar{\partial}_{E}\xi\}

(6.1)

\displaystyle\bar{\partial}\{\psi,\xi\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\{\bar{\partial}_{E}\psi,\xi\}+(-1)^{p+q}\{\psi,\partial_{E}\xi\}

\displaystyle\partial\bar{\partial}\{\psi,\xi\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\{\partial_{E}\bar{\partial}_{E}\psi,\xi\}+(-1)^{p+q+1}\{\bar{\partial}_{E}\psi,\bar{\partial}_{E}\xi\}

\displaystyle\ +(-1)^{p+q}\{\partial_{E}\psi,\partial_{E}\xi\}+\{\psi,\bar{\partial}_{E}\partial_{E}\xi\}.

Lemma 6.1
.

For any smooth map
u:M\rightarrow X
between Kähler manifolds, we have

\partial\bar{\partial}\{\bar{\partial}u,\bar{\partial}u\}\wedge\frac{\omega^{n-2}}{(n-2)!}=\left(-\{\partial_{E^{\prime}}\bar{\partial}u,\partial_{E^{\prime}}\bar{\partial}u\}+\{\bar{\partial}u,R_{E^{\prime}}(\bar{\partial}u)\}\right)\wedge\frac{\omega^{n-2}}{(n-2)!}

and

\partial\bar{\partial}\{\bar{\partial}\bar{u},\bar{\partial}\bar{u}\}\wedge\frac{\omega^{n-2}}{(n-2)!}=\left(-\{\partial_{E^{\prime\prime}}\bar{\partial}\bar{u},\partial_{E^{\prime\prime}}\bar{\partial}\bar{u}\}+\{\bar{\partial}\bar{u},R_{E^{\prime\prime}}(\bar{\partial}\bar{u})\}\right)\wedge\frac{\omega^{n-2}}{(n-2)!}.

Proof.

By setting
\psi=\xi=\bar{\partial}u\in\Omega^{0,1}(E^{\prime})
in (
6
) and repeatedly using the fact that
\bar{\partial}_{E^{\prime}}\bar{\partial}u=0
(cf. Lemma
4.7
),

\displaystyle\partial\bar{\partial}\{\bar{\partial}u,\bar{\partial}u\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\{\partial_{E^{\prime}}\bar{\partial}u,\partial_{E^{\prime}}\bar{\partial}u\}+\{\bar{\partial}u,\bar{\partial}_{E^{\prime}}\partial_{E^{\prime}}\bar{\partial}u\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\{\partial_{E^{\prime}}\bar{\partial}u,\partial_{E^{\prime}}\bar{\partial}u\}+\{\bar{\partial}u,(\bar{\partial}_{E^{\prime}}\partial_{E^{\prime}}+\partial_{E^{\prime}}\bar{\partial}_{E^{\prime}}+\bar{\partial}_{E^{\prime}}^{2})\bar{\partial}u\}.

Since
\{\bar{\partial}u,\partial_{E^{\prime}}^{2}\bar{\partial}u\}\wedge\frac{\omega^{n-2}}{(n-2)!}
is an

(n-1,n+1)
-form and hence zero for dimensional reasons, we can complete the square to obtain

\displaystyle\partial\bar{\partial}\{\bar{\partial}u,\bar{\partial}u\}\wedge\frac{\omega^{n-2}}{(n-2)!}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(-\{\partial_{E^{\prime}}\bar{\partial}u,\partial_{E^{\prime}}\bar{\partial}u\}+\{\bar{\partial}u,(\partial_{E^{\prime}}+\bar{\partial}_{E^{\prime}})^{2}\bar{\partial}u\}\right)\wedge\frac{\omega^{n-2}}{(n-2)!}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(-\{\partial_{E^{\prime}}\bar{\partial}u,\partial_{E^{\prime}}\bar{\partial}u\}+\{\bar{\partial}u,R_{E^{\prime}}(\bar{\partial}u)\}\right)\wedge\frac{\omega^{n-2}}{(n-2)!}

which proves the first equation.
The second equation follows by setting
\psi=\xi=\bar{\partial}\bar{u}\in\Omega^{0,1}(E^{\prime\prime})
in (
6
) and following exactly the same computation.
∎

Lemma 6.2
.

For any harmonic map
u:M\rightarrow X
between Kähler manifolds, we have

\displaystyle-\{\partial_{E^{\prime}}\bar{\partial}u,\partial_{E^{\prime}}\bar{\partial}u\}\wedge\frac{\omega^{n-2}}{(n-2)!}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 4\left|\partial_{E^{\prime}}\bar{\partial}u\right|^{2}\frac{\omega^{n}}{n!}

\displaystyle-\{\partial_{E^{\prime\prime}}\bar{\partial}\bar{u},\partial_{E^{\prime\prime}}\bar{\partial}\bar{u}\}\wedge\frac{\omega^{n-2}}{(n-2)!}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 4\left|\partial_{E^{\prime\prime}}\bar{\partial}\bar{u}\right|^{2}\frac{\omega^{n}}{n!}.

Proof.

From a straightforward computation (cf.
[
LY
, Lemma 6.2]
),

-\{\phi,\phi\}\wedge\frac{\omega^{n-2}}{(n-2)!}=4(|\phi|^{2}-|\mbox{Tr}_{\omega}\phi|^{2})\frac{\omega^{n}}{n!},\ \ \ \forall\phi\in\Omega^{1,1}(E).

We apply this identity with
\phi=\partial_{E^{\prime}}\bar{\partial}u
(resp.
\phi=\partial_{E^{\prime\prime}}\bar{\partial}\bar{u}
). Since
u
is harmonic,
\mbox{Tr}_{\omega}\partial_{E^{\prime}}\bar{\partial}u=0
and
\mbox{Tr}_{\omega}\partial_{E^{\prime\prime}}\bar{\partial}\bar{u}=0
by
[
LY
, Corollary 3.12]
and the Kähler identities.
∎

Lemma 6.3
.

For any smooth map
u:M\rightarrow X
between Kähler manifolds, we have

\{\bar{\partial}u,R_{E^{\prime}}(\bar{\partial}u)\}=-R_{i\bar{j}l\bar{k}}\partial u^{l}\wedge\bar{\partial}\bar{u}^{k}\wedge\bar{\partial}u^{i}\wedge\partial\bar{u}^{j}=\{\bar{\partial}\bar{u},R_{E^{\prime\prime}}(\bar{\partial}\bar{u})\}.

Proof.

We compute

(6.2)

\displaystyle\{\bar{\partial}u,R_{E^{\prime}}(\bar{\partial}u)\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\{\bar{\partial}u^{i}\frac{\partial}{\partial u^{i}},R_{E^{\prime}}(\bar{\partial}u^{j}\frac{\partial}{\partial u^{j}})\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\{\bar{\partial}u^{i}\frac{\partial}{\partial u^{i}},\bar{\partial}u^{j}\wedge R_{E^{\prime}}(\frac{\partial}{\partial u^{j}})\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\{\bar{\partial}u^{i}\frac{\partial}{\partial u^{i}},\bar{\partial}u^{j}\wedge R^{s}_{jk\bar{l}}\partial u^{k}\wedge\overline{\partial u^{l}}\frac{\partial}{\partial u^{s}}\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bar{\partial}u^{i}\wedge\partial\bar{u}^{j}\wedge G_{i\bar{s}}\overline{R^{s}_{jk\bar{l}}\partial u^{k}\wedge\overline{\partial u^{l}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bar{\partial}u^{i}\wedge\partial\bar{u}^{j}\wedge\overline{G_{s\bar{i}}R^{s}_{jk\bar{l}}\partial u^{k}\wedge\overline{\partial u^{l}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bar{\partial}u^{i}\wedge\partial\bar{u}^{j}\wedge\overline{R_{j\bar{i}k\bar{l}}\partial u^{k}\wedge\overline{\partial u^{l}}}\ \mbox{(by \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{koba}{}{}]}, (1.7.10))}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-R_{i\bar{j}l\bar{k}}\partial u^{l}\wedge\bar{\partial}\bar{u}^{k}\wedge\bar{\partial}u^{i}\wedge\partial\bar{u}^{j}\ \mbox{(by \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{koba}{}{}]}, (1.7.21))}

which proves the first equality.
Similarly as in (
6.2
),

(6.3)

\displaystyle\{\bar{\partial}\bar{u},R_{E^{\prime\prime}}(\bar{\partial}\bar{u})\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\{\bar{\partial}\bar{u}^{i}\frac{\partial}{\partial\bar{u}^{i}},R_{E^{\prime\prime}}(\bar{\partial}\bar{u}^{j}\frac{\partial}{\partial\bar{u}^{j}})\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\{\bar{\partial}\bar{u}^{i}\frac{\partial}{\partial\bar{u}^{i}},\bar{\partial}\bar{u}^{j}\wedge R_{E^{\prime\prime}}(\frac{\partial}{\partial\bar{u}^{j}})\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\{\bar{\partial}\bar{u}^{i}\frac{\partial}{\partial\bar{u}^{i}},\bar{\partial}\bar{u}^{j}\wedge\overline{R^{s}_{jk\bar{l}}}\partial\bar{u}^{k}\wedge\bar{\partial}u^{l}\frac{\partial}{\partial\bar{u}^{s}}\}\ \ \ \ \mbox{(by \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{koba}{}{}, (1.5.10)]})}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bar{\partial}\bar{u}^{i}\wedge\partial u^{j}\wedge G_{s\bar{i}}R^{s}_{jk\bar{l}}\bar{\partial}u^{k}\wedge\partial\bar{u}^{l}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bar{\partial}\bar{u}^{i}\wedge\partial u^{j}\wedge\ R_{j\bar{i}k\bar{l}}\bar{\partial}u^{k}\wedge\partial\bar{u}^{l}\ \mbox{(by \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{koba}{}{}, (1.7.10)]})}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-R_{j\bar{i}k\bar{l}}\partial u^{j}\wedge\bar{\partial}\bar{u}^{i}\wedge\bar{\partial}u^{k}\wedge\partial\bar{u}^{l}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-R_{k\bar{l}j\bar{i}}\partial u^{j}\wedge\bar{\partial}\bar{u}^{i}\wedge\bar{\partial}u^{k}\wedge\partial\bar{u}^{l}\ \mbox{(by \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{koba}{}{}, (1.7.22)]})}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-R_{i\bar{j}l\bar{k}}\partial u^{l}\wedge\bar{\partial}\bar{u}^{k}\wedge\bar{\partial}u^{i}\wedge\partial\bar{u}^{j}.

This proves the second equality.
∎

Lemma 6.4
.

For any harmonic map
u:M\rightarrow X
between Kähler manifolds, we have

\left|\partial_{E^{\prime\prime}}\bar{\partial}\bar{u}\right|^{2}=\left|\bar{\partial}_{E^{\prime\prime}}\partial\bar{u}\right|^{2}=\left|\partial_{E^{\prime}}\bar{\partial}u\right|^{2}.

Proof.

The left equality follows from Lemma
4.7
and the right from the fact that conjugation is an isometry.
∎

Proof of Siu’s Bochner formula, Theorem
4.8
.

Combine Lemma
4.3
, Lemma
6.1
, Lemma
6.2
, Lemma
6.3
and Lemma
6.4
.

\Box

Chapter 3
Harmonic maps from Riemann surfaces

In this chapter, we present some results about harmonic section with possibly infinite energy from punctured Riemann surfaces. The target space
\tilde{X}
is an NPC space. For smooth targets, the existence results are due to
[
Loh
]
and
[
Wo
]
.
We will also prove new uniqueness theorems for these harmonic maps. In the case when the harmonic map is of finite energy, uniqueness of harmonic maps follows from previous work (cf.
[
Me
]
,
[
DM5
]
) of the authors. Additionally, we give precise estimates of these harmonic maps near the punctures. These results are used to find pluriharmonic maps from smooth quasi-projective varieties of arbitrary dimensions in Chapter
5
.

Throughout this chapter, we use the following notation:

•

\bar{\mathcal{R}}
is a compact Riemann surface

•

\mathcal{R}=\bar{\mathcal{R}}\backslash\{p^{1},\dots,p^{n}\}
is a punctured Riemann surface

•

\Pi:\tilde{\mathcal{R}}\rightarrow\mathcal{R}
is the universal covering map

•

\rho:\pi_{1}(\mathcal{R})\rightarrow\mathsf{Isom}(\tilde{X})
is a homomorphism.

For each
j=1,\dots,n
,

•

\lambda^{j}\in\pi_{1}(\mathcal{R})
is the element associated to the loop around the puncture
p^{j}

•

I^{j}:=\rho(\lambda^{j})\in\mathsf{Isom}(\tilde{X})
.

The main results of this chapter are the following two theorems.

Theorem 6.5

(Existence)
.

Assume that
\tilde{X}
is an NPC space.
If

(A)

the action of
\rho(\pi_{1}(\mathcal{R}))
is proper (cf. Definition
