1.10
), and

(B)

I^{j}
has exponential decay (cf. Definition
2.1
) for
j=1,\dots,n
,

then there exists a harmonic section
u:\mathcal{R}\rightarrow\tilde{\mathcal{R}}\times_{\rho}\tilde{X}
with logarithmic energy growth (cf. Definition
6.9
below).

Theorem 6.6

(Uniqueness)
.

The section
u
of Theorem
6.5
is the unique harmonic section with logarithmic energy growth in the following cases:

(i)

\tilde{X}
is a negatively curved space as in Definition
1.6

(ii)

\tilde{X}
is an irreducible symmetric space of non-compact type or
is an irreducible locally finite Euclidean building such that the action of
\rho(\pi_{1}(\mathcal{R}))
does not fix an unbounded closed convex strict subset of
\tilde{X}
.

We explain some of the terminology contained Theorem
6.5
. Loosely speaking,
sub-logarithmic growth
means that, near the punctures of the Riemann surface, the section grows at most logarithmically, and
logarithmic energy growth
means that the energy of the section grows like a logarithmic function.

In order to give a precise definition, consider the universal cover
\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{S}^{1}
with the free group
2\pi\mathbb{Z}\simeq\pi_{1}(\mathbb{S}^{1})
acting on
\mathbb{R}
by translation.

Definition 6.7
.

For a homomorphism
\rho:2\pi\mathbb{Z}\simeq\pi_{1}(\mathbb{S}^{1})\rightarrow\tilde{X}
,
let
\mathbb{R}\times_{\rho}\tilde{X}\rightarrow\mathbb{S}^{1}
be the flat
\tilde{X}
-fiber bundle defined by
\rho
(cf. Subsection
1.4
).
Define
\mathsf{E}_{\rho}
to be the infimum of the (vertical) energies of sections
\mathbb{S}^{1}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}\times_{\rho}\tilde{X}
(cf. (
1.3
)).

Remark 6.8
.

If
\Delta_{I}
is the translation length of
I=\rho([\mathbb{S}^{1}])
, then

\mathsf{E}_{\rho}=\frac{\Delta^{2}_{I}}{2\pi}.

Next, we will

(6.4)

fix a conformal disk
\mathbb{D}^{j}\subset\bar{\mathcal{R}}
centered at each puncture
p^{j}

such that
\mathbb{D}^{i}\cap\mathbb{D}^{j}=\emptyset
for
i\neq j
. Furthermore, we use the following notation:

•

\mathbb{D}^{j*}=\mathbb{D}^{j}\backslash\{0\}

•

\mathbb{D}^{j}_{r}=\{z\in\mathbb{D}^{j}:|z|<r\}

•

\mathcal{R}_{r}=\mathcal{R}\backslash\bigcup_{j=1}^{n}\mathbb{D}^{j}_{r}

•

2\pi\mathbb{Z}\simeq\langle\lambda^{j}\rangle
is the free group generated by
\lambda^{j}

•

\rho^{j}:\langle\lambda^{j}\rangle\rightarrow\mathsf{Isom}(\tilde{X})
be the restriction of
\rho:\pi_{1}(\mathcal{R})\rightarrow\mathsf{Isom}(\tilde{X})
.

Fix
P_{0}\in\tilde{X}
and a fundamental domain
F
of
\tilde{\mathcal{R}}
. Let
f_{0}
be the section of the fiber bundle
\tilde{\mathcal{R}}\times_{\rho}\tilde{X}\rightarrow\mathcal{R}
such that, for any
p\in\mathcal{R}\cap\Pi(F)
,
f_{0}(p)=[(\tilde{p},P_{0})]
where
\tilde{p}=\Pi^{-1}(p)\cap F
. (Note that
\Pi(F)
is of full measure in
\mathcal{R}
.) Let
p^{j}\in\mathcal{R}
be a puncture and define

(6.5)

\delta_{j}:\mathbb{D}^{*j}\rightarrow[0,\infty),\ \ \ \ \delta_{j}(z)=\essinf_{\{z\in\mathbb{D}^{*j}\}}d(f(z),f_{0}(z)).

Definition 6.9
.

We say a section
f:\mathcal{R}\rightarrow\tilde{\mathcal{R}}\times_{\rho}\tilde{X}
has
sub-logarithmic growth
if for any
j=1,\dots,n

\lim_{|z|\rightarrow 0}\delta_{j}(z)+\epsilon\log|z|=-\infty\mbox{ in }\mathbb{D}^{j*}.

By the triangle inequality, this definition is independent of the choice of
P_{0}\in\tilde{X}
.
We say
f
has
logarithmic energy growth
if there exists
\rho_{0}>0
such that

(6.6)

\sum_{j=1}^{n}\mathsf{E}_{\rho^{j}}\log\frac{1}{r}\leq E^{f}[\mathcal{R}_{r}]\leq\sum_{j=1}^{n}\mathsf{E}_{\rho^{j}}\log\frac{1}{r}+C,\ \ \ 0<r<r_{0}<\rho_{0}

for some constant
C>0
.

7.
Infinite energy harmonic maps from a punctured disk

Before we consider the domain to be a general punctured Riemann surface, we first start by studying harmonic maps from a punctured disk
\bar{\mathbb{D}}^{*}
to an NPC space
\tilde{X}
. Identify the boundary of the disk with the circle; i.e.

\partial\mathbb{D}=\mathbb{S}^{1},

This induces a natural identification

2\pi\mathbb{Z}\simeq\pi_{1}(\mathbb{S}^{1})\simeq\pi_{1}(\bar{\mathbb{D}}^{*}).

The main result of this section is the following.

Theorem 7.1

(Existence and Uniqueness of the Dirichlet solution on
\mathbb{D}^{*}
)
.

Assume the following:

•

\rho:2\pi\mathbb{Z}\simeq\pi_{1}(\mathbb{S}^{1})\simeq\pi_{1}(\bar{\mathbb{D}}^{*})\rightarrow\mathsf{Isom}(\tilde{X})

is a homomorphism

•

k:\bar{\mathbb{D}}^{*}\rightarrow\widetilde{\bar{\mathbb{D}}^{*}}\times_{\rho}\tilde{X}
is a locally Lipschitz section

•

I:=\rho([\mathbb{S}^{1}])
has exponential decay to its translation length

Then there exists a harmonic section

u:\bar{\mathbb{D}}^{*}\rightarrow\widetilde{\bar{\mathbb{D}}^{*}}\times_{\rho}\tilde{X}\mbox{ with }u|_{\mathbb{S}^{1}}=k|_{\mathbb{S}^{1}}.

Furthermore,
there exists a constant
C>0
that depends only on
\mathsf{E}_{\rho}
of Definition
6.7
,
a
,
b
from Definition
2.1
and the section
k
satisfying the following properties:

(i)

\displaystyle{\mathsf{E}_{\rho}\log\frac{1}{r}\leq E^{u}[\mathbb{D}_{r,1}]\leq\mathsf{E}_{\rho}\log\frac{1}{r}+C,\ \ \ 0<r\leq 1}

(ii)

\displaystyle{\left|\frac{\partial u}{\partial r}\right|^{2}\leq\frac{C}{r^{2}(-\log r)}\ \ \mbox{ and }\ \ \left(\left|\frac{\partial u}{\partial\theta}\right|^{2}-\frac{\mathsf{E}_{\rho}}{2\pi}\right)\leq\frac{C}{-\log r}}
in
\mathbb{D}_{\frac{1}{2}}^{*}

(iii)

u
has sub-logarithmic growth.

Moreover,
u
is the only harmonic section satisfying
u|_{\partial\mathbb{D}}=k|_{\partial\mathbb{D}}
and property (iii).

Proof.

Combine Lemma
7.5
, Lemma
7.7
, Lemma
7.9
and Lemma
7.10
below.
∎

The purpose for the rest of this subsection is to prove the lemmas that comprise the proof of Theorem
7.1
.
We start with the following preliminary lemma about subharmonic functions.

Lemma 7.2
.

For
r>0
, let
\nu:\mathbb{D}_{r}^{*}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
be a subharmonic function that extends as a continuous function to
\bar{\mathbb{D}}_{r}^{*}=\overline{\mathbb{D}_{r}}\backslash\{0\}
. If we assume that

(7.1)

\lim_{z\rightarrow 0}\ \nu(z)+\epsilon\log|z|=-\infty,\ \ \forall\epsilon>0,

then

\sup_{z\in\mathbb{D}_{r}^{*}}\nu(z)\leq\max_{\zeta\in\partial\mathbb{D}_{r}}\nu(\zeta).

In particular,
\nu
extends to a subharmonic function on
\mathbb{D}_{r}
(cf.
[
HK
]
).

Proof.

Let
h:\mathbb{D}_{r}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
be the unique Dirichlet solution with
h|_{\partial\mathbb{D}_{r}}=\nu|_{\partial\mathbb{D}_{r}}
. By the maximum principle,
h
is non-negative and bounded on
\mathbb{D}_{r}
. The function

f_{\epsilon}(z)=\nu(z)-h(z)+\epsilon(\log|z|-\log r)

is subharmonic on
\mathbb{D}_{r}^{*}
with

f_{\epsilon}|_{\partial\mathbb{D}_{r}}\equiv 0
on
\partial\mathbb{D}_{r}
.
Furthermore, since
h
is a bounded function and
\nu
satisfies (
7.1
),

\lim_{z\rightarrow 0}f_{\epsilon}(z)=-\infty.

By the maximum principle,
f_{\epsilon}\leq 0
. By letting
\epsilon\rightarrow 0
, we conclude
\nu(z)-h(z)\leq 0
for all
z\in\mathbb{D}_{r}^{*}
.
Thus,

\sup_{z\in\mathbb{D}_{r}^{*}}\nu(z)\leq\sup_{z\in\mathbb{D}_{r}}h(z)\leq\max_{\zeta\in\partial\mathbb{D}_{r}}h(\zeta)=\max_{\zeta\in\partial\mathbb{D}_{r}}\nu(\zeta).

∎

Lemma 7.3
.

If
\rho
and
I
are as in Theorem
7.1
, then for any section
f:\mathbb{D}_{r,r_{0}}\rightarrow\widetilde{\bar{\mathbb{D}}^{*}}\times_{\rho}\tilde{X}
,

\mathsf{E}_{\rho}\log\frac{r_{0}}{r}\leq\int_{\mathbb{D}_{r,r_{0}}}\frac{1}{r^{2}}\left|\frac{\partial f}{\partial\theta}\right|^{2}rdr\wedge d\theta\leq E^{f}[\mathbb{D}_{r,r_{0}}],\ \ 0<r<r_{0}<1.

Proof.

By the definition of
\mathsf{E}_{I}
, any section
c:\mathbb{S}^{1}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}\times_{\rho}\tilde{X}
satisfies

\mathsf{E}_{\rho}\leq\int_{0}^{2\pi}\left|\frac{\partial c}{\partial\theta}\right|^{2}d\theta.

After identifying
\mathbb{S}^{1}
with
\partial\mathbb{D}_{r}
, we can view the restriction
f|_{\partial\mathbb{D}_{r}}
as a section
\mathbb{S}^{1}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}\times_{\rho}\tilde{X}
.
Thus,

\displaystyle\mathsf{E}_{\rho}\log\frac{r_{0}}{r}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathsf{E}_{\rho}\int_{r}^{r_{0}}\frac{1}{r^{2}}rdr\wedge d\theta

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{2\pi}\int_{r}^{r_{0}}\frac{1}{r^{2}}\left|\frac{\partial f}{\partial\theta}(r,\theta)\right|^{2}rdr\wedge d\theta

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle E^{f}[\mathbb{D}_{r,r_{0}}].

∎

Lemma 7.4
.

If
\rho
,
I
and
k:\bar{\mathbb{D}}^{*}\rightarrow\widetilde{\bar{\mathbb{D}}^{*}}\times_{\rho}\tilde{X}
are as in Theorem
7.1
, then there exists a constant
C>0
and a Lipschitz section
v:\bar{\mathbb{D}}^{*}\rightarrow\widetilde{\bar{\mathbb{D}}^{*}}\times_{\rho}\tilde{X}
with
v|_{\partial\mathbb{D}}=k|_{\partial\mathbb{D}}
such that

(7.2)

\mathsf{E}_{\rho}\log\frac{r_{0}}{r}\leq E^{v}[\mathbb{D}_{r,r_{0}}]\leq\mathsf{E}_{\rho}\log\frac{r_{0}}{r}+C,\ \ \ 0<r<r_{0}\leq\frac{1}{2}.

Moreover, the Lipschitz constant of
v
and
C
are dependent only on
\mathsf{E}_{\rho}
,
a
,
b
from Definition
2.1
and
k
.

Proof.

We will first construct an one-parameter family of sections
\gamma_{s}:\mathbb{S}^{1}\mapsto\mathbb{R}\times_{\rho}\tilde{X}
as follows:

•

If
I
is semisimple (i.e. elliptic or hyperbolic), let
P_{\star}\in\tilde{X}
be a point where the infimum
\Delta_{I}
is attained. For any
s\in[0,\infty)
, define

\tilde{\gamma}_{s}:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\tilde{X}
to be the
\rho
-equivariant geodesic (a constant map if
I
is elliptic) such that
\tilde{\gamma}_{s}(0)=P_{\star}
and
\tilde{\gamma}_{s}(2\pi)=I(P_{\star})
and let
\gamma_{s}:\mathbb{S}^{1}\rightarrow\tilde{\mathbb{S}}^{1}\times_{\rho}\tilde{X}
be the associated section.

•

If
I
is parabolic, then
let

c:[0,\infty)\rightarrow\tilde{X}
be a geodesic ray defined in Definition
2.1
. Define the
\rho
-equivariant curve

\tilde{\gamma}_{s}:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\tilde{X}
such that
\tilde{\gamma}_{s}|_{[0,2\pi]}
is the geodesic from
c(s)
and
I(c(s))
and let
\gamma_{s}:\mathbb{S}^{1}\rightarrow\tilde{\mathbb{S}}^{1}\times_{\rho}\tilde{X}
be the associated section.

We note that by the quadrilateral comparison of NPC spaces, for
\theta\in[0,2\pi]
,

d(\tilde{\gamma}_{s_{1}}(\theta),\tilde{\gamma}_{s_{2}}(\theta))\leq(1-\frac{\theta}{2\pi})d(c(s_{1}),c(s_{2}))+\frac{\theta}{2\pi}d(I\circ c(s_{1})),I\circ c(s_{2})))=|s_{1}-s_{2}|.

Thus

(7.3)

\left|\frac{d}{ds}(\tilde{\gamma}_{s}(\theta))\right|\leq 1,\ \ \ \forall s\in(0,\infty),\ \theta\in\mathbb{S}^{1}.

Since
I\in\mathsf{Isom}(\tilde{X})
has exponential decay of Definition
2.1
, we have

(7.4)

\int_{\mathbb{S}^{1}}\left|\frac{\partial\gamma_{s}}{\partial\theta}\right|^{2}d\tau\leq\mathsf{E}_{\rho}(1+2\pi be^{-as}).

Define a
\rho
-equivariant map
\tilde{v}:\widetilde{\bar{\mathbb{D}}^{*}}\rightarrow\tilde{X}
as follows: First, for
(r,t)\in\widetilde{\bar{\mathbb{D}}^{*}}
, let

\tilde{v}(r,t):=\tilde{\gamma}_{(-\log r-\log 2)^{\frac{1}{3}}}(t)\mbox{ for }r\in(0,\frac{1}{2}]\ \mbox{ and }\ \tilde{v}(1,t):=\tilde{k}(1,t)

Next, for each
t\in\mathbb{R}
, let

r\mapsto\tilde{v}(r,t)\mbox{ for }r\in[\frac{1}{2},1]

to be the arclength parameterization of the geodesic between
\tilde{v}(\frac{1}{2},t)
and
\tilde{k}(\frac{1}{2},t)
. Finally, let

v:\bar{\mathbb{D}}^{*}\rightarrow\widetilde{\bar{\mathbb{D}}^{*}}\times_{\rho}\tilde{X}
be the section associated to the
\rho
-equivariant map
\tilde{v}
.

For
0<r<r_{0}<\frac{1}{2}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathsf{E}_{\rho}\log\frac{r_{0}}{r}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{2\pi}\int_{r}^{r_{0}}\frac{1}{r^{2}}\left|\frac{\partial v}{\partial\theta}\right|^{2}rdr\wedge d\theta\ \ \ \ \ \mbox{(by Lemma~\ref{lowerbd})}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{2\pi}\int_{r}^{r_{0}}\frac{1}{r^{2}}\left|\frac{\partial(\gamma_{(-\log r-\log 2)^{\frac{1}{3}}})}{\partial\theta}\right|^{2}rdr\wedge d\theta

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{2\pi}\int_{-\log r_{0}}^{-\log r}\left|\frac{\partial(\gamma_{(t-\log 2)^{\frac{1}{3}}})}{\partial\theta}\right|^{2}dtd\theta

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\int_{-\log r_{0}}^{-\log r}(\mathsf{E}_{\rho}+be^{-a(t-\log 2)^{\frac{1}{3}}})dt\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \mbox{(by (\ref{gammatbd}))}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathsf{E}_{\rho}\log\frac{r_{0}}{r}+C.

By (
7.3
),

\displaystyle\left|\frac{\partial v}{\partial r}\right|^{2}(r,\theta)=\left|\frac{\partial}{\partial s}\Big|_{s=(-\log r-\log 2)^{\frac{1}{3}}}\gamma_{s}(\theta)\right|^{2}\left|\frac{\partial((-\log r-\log 2)^{\frac{1}{3}})}{\partial r}\right|^{2}\leq\frac{1}{r^{2}(-\log r-\log 2)^{\frac{4}{3}}},

and thus

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{2\pi}\int_{r}^{r_{0}}\left|\frac{\partial v}{\partial r}\right|^{2}rdr\wedge d\theta

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{2\pi}\int_{r}^{r_{0}}\frac{1}{r(-\log r-\log 2)^{\frac{4}{3}}}dr\wedge d\theta\leq C.

∎

Lemma 7.5

(Existence and property (i) of Theorem
7.1
)
.

If
\tilde{X}
,
\rho
,
\mathsf{E}_{\rho}
and
k
as in Theorem
7.1
,
then there exists a harmonic section
u:\mathbb{D}^{*}\rightarrow\widetilde{\mathbb{D}^{*}}\times_{\rho}\tilde{X}
with
u|_{\partial\mathbb{D}}=k|_{\partial\mathbb{D}}
and a constant
C>0
that depends only on
\mathsf{E}_{\rho}
,
a
,
b
from Definition
2.1
and the section
k
such that

\mathsf{E}_{\rho}\log\frac{1}{r}\leq E^{u}[\mathbb{D}_{r,1}]\leq\mathsf{E}_{\rho}\log\frac{1}{r}+C,\ \ \ 0<r\leq 1.

Proof.

Let
v
be as in Lemma
7.4
. By
[
KS1
, Theorem 2.7.2]
, there exists a unique harmonic section

u_{r}:\mathbb{D}_{r,1}\rightarrow\widetilde{\mathbb{D}_{r,1}}\times_{\rho}\tilde{X}\mbox{ with }u_{r}|_{\partial\mathbb{D}_{r,1}}=v|_{\partial\mathbb{D}_{r,1}}.

We have that

\displaystyle\mathsf{E}_{\rho}\log\frac{r_{0}}{r}+E^{u_{r}}[\mathbb{D}_{r_{0},1}]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle E^{u_{r}}[\mathbb{D}_{r,r_{0}}]+E^{u_{r}}[\mathbb{D}_{r_{0},1}]\ \ \ \ \ \mbox{(by Lemma~\ref{lowerbd})}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle E^{u_{r}}[\mathbb{D}_{r,1}]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle E^{v}[\mathbb{D}_{r,1}]\ \ (\mbox{since $u_{r}$ is minimizing})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle E^{v}[\mathbb{D}_{r,r_{0}}]+E^{v}[\mathbb{D}_{r_{0},1}]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\mathsf{E}_{\rho}\log\frac{r_{0}}{r}+C+E^{v}[\mathbb{D}_{r_{0},1}]\ \ \ \ \ \mbox{(by (\ref{glog}))}.

Therefore,

(7.5)

E^{u_{r}}[\mathbb{D}_{r_{0},1}]\leq C+E^{v}[\mathbb{D}_{r_{0},1}].

Note that the right hand side of the above is independent of
r\in(0,\frac{1}{2}]
.
Thus,
\{u_{r}|_{\mathbb{D}_{2r_{0},1}}\}
is an equicontinuous family of sections (cf.
[
KS1
, Theorem 2.4.6]
). Consequently, there exists a sequence
r_{i}\rightarrow 0
and a harmonic section

(7.6)

u:\bar{\mathbb{D}}^{*}\rightarrow\widetilde{\bar{\mathbb{D}}^{*}}\times_{\rho}\tilde{X}\mbox{ with }u|_{\partial\mathbb{D}}=k|_{\partial\mathbb{D}}.

such that the sequence
\{u_{r_{i}}\}
converges uniformly on every on compact subset of
\mathbb{D}^{*}
to
u
.

To prove property (i), we note that for
0<r<r_{1}<1
,

\displaystyle E^{u_{r}}[\mathbb{D}_{r,r_{1}}]+E^{u}[\mathbb{D}_{r_{1},1}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle E^{u_{r}}[\mathbb{D}_{r,1}]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle E^{v}[\mathbb{D}_{r,1}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle E^{v}[\mathbb{D}_{r,r_{1}}]+E^{v}[\mathbb{D}_{r_{1},1}]

which, combined with Lemma
7.3
and (
7.2
), imply that

E^{u_{r}}[\mathbb{D}_{r_{1},1}]\leq E^{v}[\mathbb{D}_{r_{1},1}]+C.

Letting
r\rightarrow 0
and applying the lower semicontinuity of energy
[
KS1
, Theorem 1.6.1]
, we obtain

E^{u}[\mathbb{D}_{r_{1},1}]\leq E^{v}[\mathbb{D}_{r_{1},1}]+C.

Applying Lemma
7.3
and (
7.2
), we obtain

\mathsf{E}_{\rho}\log\frac{1}{r_{1}}\leq E^{u}[\mathbb{D}_{r_{1},1}]\leq E^{v}[\mathbb{D}_{r_{1},1}]+C\leq\mathsf{E}_{\rho}\log\frac{1}{r_{1}}+C.

∎

Lemma 7.6
.

If
u:\bar{\mathbb{D}}^{*}\rightarrow\widetilde{\bar{\mathbb{D}}^{*}}\times_{\rho}\tilde{X}
is a harmonic section with logarithmic energy growth,
then

\int_{\mathbb{D}^{*}}\left|\frac{\partial u}{\partial r}\right|^{2}rdr\wedge d\theta\leq C\ \mbox{ and }\ \int_{\mathbb{D}^{*}}\frac{1}{r^{2}}\left(\left|\frac{\partial u}{\partial\theta}\right|^{2}-\frac{\mathsf{E}_{\rho}}{2\pi}\right)rdr\wedge d\theta\leq C

where
C
that depends
C_{0}
from (
6.6
).

Proof.

Follows immediately from the inequality (
6.6
) in the Definition
6.9
.
∎

Lemma 7.7

(Property (ii) of Theorem
7.1
)
.

If
u:\bar{\mathbb{D}}^{*}\rightarrow\widetilde{\bar{\mathbb{D}}^{*}}\times_{\rho}\tilde{X}
is a harmonic section with logarithmic energy growth,
then

(7.7)

\displaystyle\left(\left|\frac{\partial u}{\partial\theta}\right|^{2}-\frac{\mathsf{E}_{\rho}}{2\pi}\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{C}{(-\log r)}\ \text{ in }\,\mathbb{D}_{\frac{1}{4}}^{*}

(7.8)

\displaystyle\lim_{r\rightarrow 0}(-\log r)\left(\left|\frac{\partial u}{\partial\theta}\right|^{2}(r,\theta)-\frac{\mathsf{E}_{\rho}}{2\pi}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0

(7.9)

\displaystyle\left|\frac{\partial u}{\partial r}\right|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{C}{r^{2}(-\log r)}\ \mbox{ in }\,\mathbb{D}_{\frac{1}{4}}^{*}

(7.10)

\displaystyle\lim_{r\rightarrow 0}(-\log r)r^{2}\left|\frac{\partial u}{\partial r}\right|^{2}(r,\theta)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0.

where
C
that depends
C_{0}
from (
6.6
).

Proof.

Consider the cylinder

{\mathcal{C}}=(0,\infty)\times\mathbb{S}^{1}

and let

(7.11)

\Phi:{\mathcal{C}}\rightarrow\mathbb{D}^{*},\ \ \ \ (t,\psi)=(r=e^{-t},\theta=\psi).

Since
\Phi
is a conformal map,
u\circ\Phi
is harmonic. Thus, the directional energy density functions
\left|\frac{\partial(u\circ\Phi)}{\partial t}\right|^{2}
and
\left|\frac{\partial(u\circ\Phi)}{\partial\psi}\right|^{2}
are subharmonic by
[
KS1
, Remark 2.4.3]
.
Furthermore,

\left|\frac{\partial(u\circ\Phi)}{\partial t}\right|^{2}
and
\left|\frac{\partial(u\circ\Phi)}{\partial\psi}\right|^{2}-\frac{\mathsf{E}_{\rho}}{2\pi}
are integrable in
{\mathcal{C}}=(0,\infty)\times\mathbb{S}^{1}
by Lemma
15.2
and the chain rule.

The subharmonicity of the directional energy density functions implies

0\leq\int_{(t_{1},t_{2})\times\mathbb{S}^{1}}\triangle\varphi\left|\frac{\partial(u\circ\Phi)}{\partial\psi}\right|^{2}dtd\psi,\ \ \forall\varphi\in C_{c}^{\infty}((t_{1},t_{2})\times\mathbb{S}^{1}),\ \varphi\geq 0.

Letting
\varphi
approximate the characteristic function of
(t_{1},t_{2})\times\mathbb{S}^{1}
, we obtain

0\leq\frac{d}{dt}\Big|_{t=t_{2}}\left(\int_{\{t\}\times\mathbb{S}^{1}}\left|\frac{\partial(u\circ\Phi)}{\partial\psi}\right|^{2}d\psi\right)-\frac{d}{dt}\Big|_{t=t_{1}}\left(\int_{\{t\}\times\mathbb{S}^{1}}\left|\frac{\partial(u\circ\Phi)}{\partial\psi}\right|^{2}d\psi\right).

In other words,

F(t):=\int_{\{t\}\times\mathbb{S}^{1}}\left|\frac{\partial(u\circ\Phi)}{\partial\psi}\right|^{2}-\frac{\mathsf{E}_{\rho}}{2\pi}\ d\psi,\ \ \ t\in(0,\infty)

is a convex function. By Lemma
15.2
,
\int_{0}^{\infty}F(t)dt<\infty
.
Since
F(t)
is convex and integrable,

F(t)
is a decreasing. Thus, we obtain

(7.12)

tF(t)\leq 2\int_{\frac{t}{2}}^{t}F(\tau)\ d\tau\leq 2\int_{\frac{t}{2}}^{\infty}F(\tau)\ d\tau.

With
\mathcal{B}_{1}(t_{0},\psi_{0})
denoting the unit disk centered at
(t_{0},\psi_{0})\in\mathcal{C}
, the mean value inequality implies

\displaystyle t_{0}\left(\left|\frac{\partial(u\circ\Phi)}{\partial\psi}\right|^{2}-\frac{\mathsf{E}_{\rho}}{2\pi}\right)(t_{0},\psi_{0})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{t_{0}}{\pi}\int_{\mathcal{B}_{1}(t_{0},\psi_{0})}\left(\left|\frac{\partial(u\circ\Phi)}{\partial\psi}\right|^{2}-\frac{\mathsf{E}_{\rho}}{2\pi}\right)dtd\psi

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{t_{0}}{\pi}\int_{(t_{0}-1,t_{0}+1)\times\mathbb{S}^{1}}\left(\left|\frac{\partial(u\circ\Phi)}{\partial\psi}\right|^{2}-\frac{\mathsf{E}_{\rho}}{2\pi}\right)dtd\psi

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\pi}\frac{t_{0}}{t_{0}-1}\int_{(t_{0}-1,t_{0}+1)\times\mathbb{S}^{1}}t\left(\left|\frac{\partial(u\circ\Phi)}{\partial\psi}\right|^{2}-\frac{\mathsf{E}_{\rho}}{2\pi}\right)dtd\psi

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\pi}\frac{t_{0}}{t_{0}-1}\int_{t_{0}-1}^{t_{0}+1}tF(t)dt

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\pi}\frac{t_{0}}{t_{0}-1}\int_{t_{0}-1}^{t_{0}+1}2\int_{\frac{t}{2}}^{\infty}F(\tau)\ d\tau dt\ \ \ \mbox{(by (\ref{bbbb}))}.

Since
F(\tau)\geq 0
,

\int_{\frac{t}{2}}^{\infty}F(\tau)\ d\tau\leq\int_{\frac{t_{0}-1}{2}}^{\infty}F(\tau)\ d\tau,\ \ \ t\in(t_{0}-1,t_{0}+1).

Combining the above two inequalities and assuming
t_{0}\geq 2
(and thus
\frac{t_{0}}{t_{0}-1}\leq 2
),

(7.13)

\displaystyle t_{0}\left(\left|\frac{\partial(u\circ\Phi)}{\partial\psi}\right|^{2}(t_{0},\psi_{0})-\frac{\mathsf{E}_{\rho}}{2\pi}\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{4}{\pi}\int_{\frac{t_{0}-1}{2}}^{\infty}F(\tau)\ d\tau.

Estimates (
7.7
) and (
7.8
) both follow from (
7.13
) by the chain rule and the fact that
F(\tau)
is integrable on
(0,\infty)
with integral bound given by Lemma
15.2
.

Similar argument for

G(t):=\int_{\{t\}\times\mathbb{S}^{1}}\left|\frac{\partial(u\circ\Phi)}{\partial t}\right|^{2}d\psi,\ \ \ t\in(0,\infty)

proves (
7.9
) and (
7.10
).
∎

Lemma 7.8
.

Let
u:\bar{\mathbb{D}}^{*}\rightarrow\widetilde{\bar{\mathbb{D}}^{*}}\times_{\rho}\tilde{X}
be a harmonic section with logarithmic energy growth. For any
\epsilon>0
, there exists
\rho_{0}>0
such that

d^{2}(u(r_{1}e^{i\theta}),u(r_{0}e^{i\theta}))\leq-\epsilon\log r_{1},\ \ \ \forall 0<r_{1}<r_{0}\leq\rho_{0},\,0<\theta<2\pi.

Proof.

Fix
\epsilon>0
. The convergence of

(-\log r)r^{2}\left|\frac{\partial u}{\partial r}\right|^{2}(r,\theta)
to 0 as
r\rightarrow 0
(cf. (
7.10
)) implies that there exists
\rho_{0}>0
such that

\left|\frac{\partial u}{\partial r}\right|\leq\frac{\sqrt{\epsilon}}{2r(-\log r)^{\frac{1}{2}}},\ \ r\in(0,\rho_{0}].

Thus, we have for
0<r_{1}<r_{0}\leq\rho_{0}
, and noting
\rho
-equivariance of
\tilde{u}
, we have

\displaystyle d^{2}(u(r_{1}e^{i\theta}),u(r_{0}e^{i\theta}))

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\left(\int_{r_{0}}^{r_{1}}\frac{d}{dr}d(u(re^{i\theta}),u(r_{0}e^{i\theta}))dr\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\left(\int_{r_{1}}^{r_{0}}\left|\frac{\partial u}{\partial r}\right|(re^{i\theta})dr\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{\epsilon}{4}\left(\int_{r_{1}}^{r_{0}}\frac{dr}{r(-\log r)^{\frac{1}{2}}}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\epsilon\left((-\log r_{1})^{\frac{1}{2}}-(-\log r_{0})^{\frac{1}{2}}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle-\epsilon\log r_{1}.

∎

Lemma 7.9

(Property (iii) of Theorem
7.1
)
.

If
u:\bar{\mathbb{D}}^{*}\rightarrow\widetilde{\bar{\mathbb{D}}^{*}}\times_{\rho}\tilde{X}
is a harmonic section with logarithmic energy growth,
then
u
has sub-logarithmic growth.

Proof.

By Lemma
7.8
, for
\epsilon>0
, there exists

r_{0}>0
sufficiently small such that

d^{2}(u(re^{i\theta}),u(r_{0}e^{i\theta}))\leq-\frac{\epsilon}{4}\log r,\ \ \ r\in(0,r_{0}).

Set
P_{0}=\tilde{u}(r_{0}e^{i\theta_{0}})\in\tilde{X}
and define
f_{0}(p)=[(\tilde{p},P_{0})]
as in Definition
6.9
. For
z=re^{i\theta}
,

\displaystyle d^{2}(u(z),f_{0}(z))+\epsilon\log|z|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle d^{2}(u(re^{i\theta}),u(r_{0}e^{i\theta_{0}}))+\epsilon\log r

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 2d^{2}(u(re^{\char 16\relax\theta}),u(r_{0}e^{i\theta}))+2d^{2}(u(r_{0}e^{i\theta}),u(r_{0}e^{i\theta_{0}}))+\epsilon\log r

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{\epsilon}{2}\log r+d^{2}(u(r_{0}e^{i\theta}),u(r_{0}e^{i\theta_{0}})).

Now let
r\rightarrow 0
.
∎

Lemma 7.10

(Uniqueness for sub-logarithmic growth maps)
.

If
u,v:\bar{\mathbb{D}}^{*}\rightarrow\widetilde{\bar{\mathbb{D}}^{*}}\times_{\rho}\tilde{X}
are harmonic sections with sub-logarithmic growth with
u=v
on
\partial\mathbb{D}
, then
u=v
on
\mathbb{D}^{*}
.

Proof.

The function
d^{2}(u,v)
(as defined by (
1.4
))
is a continuous subharmonic function (c.f.
[
KS1
, Remark 2.4.3]
). Furthermore,
d^{2}(u,v)\equiv 0
on
\partial\mathbb{D}
. Since
u
,
v
both have sub-logarithmic growth, the triangle inequality implies

\lim_{|z|\rightarrow 0}d^{2}(u(z),v(z))+\epsilon\log|z|=-\infty.

Thus, we can apply Lemma
7.2
to conclude
d^{2}(u(z),v(z))\equiv 0
on
\mathbb{D}^{*}
which implies
u\equiv v
.
∎

Corollary 7.11
.

Any harmonic section
v:\bar{\mathbb{D}}^{*}\rightarrow\widetilde{\bar{\mathbb{D}}^{*}}\times_{\rho}\tilde{X}

with sub-logarithmic growth satisfies properties (i), (ii) and (iii) of Theorem
7.1
.

Proof.

By the uniqueness assertion of Lemma
7.10
,
v
must be the harmonic section
u
constructed in Theorem
7.1
.
∎

8.
Proof of existence, Theorem
6.5

In this section, we prove existence of an equivariant harmonic map from a punctured Riemann surface
\mathcal{R}
.
We use the following notation:

•

k:\mathcal{R}\rightarrow\tilde{\mathcal{R}}\times_{\rho}\tilde{X}
is a locally Lipschitz section (cf.
[
KS1
, Proposition 2.6.1]
)

•

k^{j}:=k|_{\bar{\mathbb{D}}^{j*}}

•

\langle\lambda^{j}\rangle
is the subgroup of
\pi_{1}(\mathcal{R})
generated by a loop
\lambda^{j}
around the puncture
p^{j}

•

\rho^{j}:\langle\lambda^{j}\rangle\rightarrow\mathsf{Isom}(\tilde{X})
is the restriction of
\rho
to
\langle\lambda^{j}\rangle

•

I^{j}=\rho(\lambda^{j})

•

\mathsf{E}_{j}:=\mathsf{E}_{\rho^{j}}
as in Definition
6.7
where we identify
[\lambda^{j}]
with
[\mathbb{S}^{1}]

Applying Lemma
7.4
with
\rho=\rho^{j}
,
I=I^{j}
and
k=k^{j}
yields a prototype section

v^{j}:\mathbb{D}^{*}\rightarrow\widetilde{\mathbb{D}^{*}}\times_{\rho^{j}}\tilde{X}\subset\widetilde{{\mathcal{R}}}\times_{\rho^{j}}\tilde{X}.

The composition of
v^{j}
and the quotient map

\widetilde{{\mathcal{R}}}\times_{\rho^{j}}\tilde{X}\rightarrow\widetilde{{\mathcal{R}}}\times_{\rho}\tilde{X}
defines
a section
of
\tilde{\mathcal{R}}\times_{\rho}\tilde{X}\rightarrow\mathcal{R}
over
{\mathbb{D}}^{*j}
which we call again
v^{j}
.
We extend these local sections
v^{j}:{\mathbb{D}}^{*j}\rightarrow\tilde{\mathcal{R}}\times_{\rho}\tilde{X}
for
j=1,\dots,n
to define a smooth section
v:{\mathcal{R}}\rightarrow\tilde{\mathcal{R}}\times_{\rho}\tilde{X}
.
By the construction and Lemma
7.4
,

(8.1)

\sum_{j=1}^{n}\mathsf{E}_{j}\log\frac{1}{r}\leq E^{v}[\mathcal{R}_{r}]\leq\sum_{j=1}^{n}\mathsf{E}_{j}\log\frac{1}{r}+C,\ \ \ 0<r\leq 1.

Definition 8.1
.

The locally Lipschitz section

(8.2)

v:\mathcal{R}\rightarrow\tilde{\mathcal{R}}\times_{\rho}\tilde{X}

constructed above is called the
prototype section
of the fiber bundle
\tilde{\mathcal{R}}\times_{\rho}\tilde{X}\rightarrow\mathcal{R}
.
The associated
\rho
-equivariant map
\tilde{v}:\tilde{M}\rightarrow\tilde{X}
is called the
prototype map
.

Define

u_{\mathcal{R}_{r}}:\mathcal{R}_{r}\rightarrow\tilde{\mathcal{R}}\times_{\rho}\tilde{X}

to be the unique harmonic section with boundary values equal to that of
v|_{\partial\mathcal{R}_{r}}
(cf.
[
KS1
, Theorem 2.7.2]
). Fix
r_{0}\in(0,\frac{1}{2}]
and let
r\in(0,r_{0})
.
By Lemma
7.3
,

\mathsf{E}_{j}\log\frac{r_{0}}{r}\leq\int_{\mathbb{D}_{r,r_{0}}}\frac{1}{r^{2}}\left|\frac{\partial u_{\mathcal{R}_{r}}}{\partial\theta}\right|^{2}rdr\wedge d\theta\leq E^{u_{\mathcal{R}_{r}}}[\mathbb{D}^{j}_{r,r_{0}}].

Thus, by (
8.1
),

E^{v}[\bigcup_{j=1}^{n}\mathbb{D}^{j}_{r,r_{0}}]\leq E^{u_{\mathcal{R}_{r}}}[\bigcup_{j=1}^{n}\mathbb{D}^{j}_{r,r_{0}}]+C

which implies

\displaystyle E^{u_{\mathcal{R}_{r}}}[\bigcup_{j=1}^{n}\mathbb{D}^{j}_{r,r_{0}}]+E^{u_{\mathcal{R}_{r}}}[\mathcal{R}_{r_{0}}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle E^{u_{\mathcal{R}_{r}}}[\mathcal{R}_{r}]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle E^{v}[\mathcal{R}_{r}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle E^{v}[\bigcup_{j=1}^{n}\mathbb{D}^{j}_{r,r_{0}}]+E^{v}[\mathcal{R}_{r_{0}}]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle E^{u_{\mathcal{R}_{r}}}[\bigcup_{j=1}^{n}\mathbb{D}^{j}_{r,r_{0}}]+C+E^{v}[\mathcal{R}_{r_{0}}].

In other words,

(8.3)

E^{u_{\mathcal{R}_{r}}}[\mathcal{R}_{r_{0}}]\leq C+E^{v}[\mathcal{R}_{r_{0}}],\ \ \ \forall r\in(0,r_{0}).

The right hand side of the inequality (
8.3
) is independent of the parameter
r
; i.e. once we fix
r_{0}
, the quantity
E^{u_{\mathcal{R}_{r}}}[\mathcal{R}_{r_{0}}]
is uniformly bounded for all
r\in(0,r_{0})
. This implies a uniform Lipschitz bound, say
L
, of
u_{\mathcal{R}_{r}}
for
r\in(0,r_{0})
in
\mathcal{R}_{2r_{0}}
(cf.
[
KS1
, Theorem 2.4.6]
).

Let
\{\mu_{1},\dots,\mu_{N}\}
be a set of generators of
\pi_{1}(\mathcal{R})
and
\tilde{u}_{\mathcal{R}_{r}}
,
\tilde{v}
be the
\rho
-equivariant maps associated to sections
u_{\mathcal{R}_{r}}
,
v
respectively.
Thus,

d(\tilde{u}_{\mathcal{R}_{r}}(\mu_{i}p),\tilde{u}_{\mathcal{R}_{r}}(p))\leq Ld_{\tilde{M}}(\mu_{i}p,p),\ \ \forall p\in\tilde{\mathcal{R}}_{2r_{0}},\ i=1,\dots,N\ \ r\in(0,r_{0}).

If we let

c=L\sup\{d_{\tilde{M}}(\mu_{i}p,p):i=1,\dots,N,\ p\in\tilde{\mathcal{R}}_{2r_{0}}\},

then by equivariance

d(\rho(\mu_{i})\tilde{u}_{\mathcal{R}_{r}}(p),\tilde{u}_{\mathcal{R}_{r}}(p)))\leq c,\ \ \ p\in\tilde{\mathcal{R}}_{2r_{0}},\ i=1,\dots,N,\ \ r\in(0,r_{0}).

In other words,
\delta(\tilde{u}_{\mathcal{R}_{r}}(p))\leq c
for all
p\in\tilde{\mathcal{R}}_{2r_{0}}
and
r\in(0,r_{0})
.
By the properness of
\rho
, there exists
P_{0}\in\tilde{X}
and
R_{0}>0
such that

\{\tilde{u}_{\mathcal{R}_{r}}(p):\ p\in\tilde{\mathcal{R}}_{2r_{0}},\ r\in(0,r_{0})\}\subset B_{R_{0}}(P_{0}).

Thus, by taking a compact exhaustion, applying Arzela-Ascoli and the usual diagonalization argument, there exists a subsequence of
\tilde{u}_{\mathcal{R}_{r}}
converges locally uniformly to a
\rho
-equivariant harmonic map
\tilde{u}:\tilde{\mathcal{R}}\rightarrow\tilde{X}
. Let
u:\mathcal{R}\rightarrow\tilde{\mathcal{R}}\times_{\rho}\tilde{X}
be the associated harmonic section.
The lower semicontinuity of energy (cf.
[
KS1
, Theorem 1.6.1]
) and (
8.3
) imply

E^{u}[\mathcal{R}_{r_{0}}]\leq\sum_{j=1}^{n}\mathsf{E}_{j}\log\frac{1}{r_{0}}+C,\ \ \forall r_{0}\in(0,\frac{1}{2}]

which, along with Lemma
7.3
, proves that
u
has logarithmic energy growth (cf. Definition
6.9
). The fact that
u
has sub-logarithmic growth follows from Lemma
7.9
. This completes the proof of Theorem
6.5
.

9.
Proof of uniqueness, Theorem
6.6

In this section, we prove the uniqueness of a harmonic map from a punctured Riemann surface
\mathcal{R}
(cf. Theorem
6.6
).
First note that by Lemma
7.3
, for
0<r<r_{0}<1
and any section
f:\bar{\mathbb{D}}^{*}\rightarrow\widetilde{\bar{\mathbb{D}}^{*}}\times_{\rho}\tilde{X}
,

(9.1)

\sum_{j=1}^{n}\mathsf{E}_{j}\log\frac{r_{0}}{r}\leq E^{f}[\bigcup_{j=1}^{n}\mathbb{D}^{j}_{r,r_{0}}].

Definition 9.1
.

Let

E^{f}(r)=E^{f}[\mathcal{R}_{r}]-\sum_{j=1}^{n}\mathsf{E}_{j}\log\frac{1}{r}.

Note that by (
9.1
),
r\mapsto E^{f}(r)
is an increasing function.
Define the
modified energy
of
f
as

E^{f}(0)=\lim_{r\rightarrow 0}E^{f}(r).

Let
\mathcal{L}_{\rho}
be the set of all sections
\mathcal{R}\rightarrow\tilde{\mathcal{R}}\times_{\rho}\tilde{X}
such that
E^{f}(0)<\infty
. Let
\mathcal{H}_{\rho}\subset\mathcal{L}_{\rho}
denote the set of all harmonic sections
u:\mathcal{R}\rightarrow\tilde{\mathcal{R}}\times_{\rho}\tilde{X}
of sub-logarithmic growth such that the associated
\rho
-equivariant map
u
that is not identically constant or does not map into a geodesic.

The goal is to prove that
\mathcal{H}_{\rho}
contains only one element. We first prove the following series of preliminary lemmas.

Lemma 9.2
.

If
u\in{\mathcal{H}}_{\rho}
and
\mathbb{D}^{j}\subset\bar{\mathcal{R}}
is the fixed conformal disk at the puncture
p^{j}
(cf. (
6.4
)), then the restriction map
u|_{\mathbb{D}^{j*}}
satisfies the properties (i), (ii) and (iii) of Theorem
7.1
.

Proof.

This follows immediately from Corollary
7.11
.
∎

Lemma 9.3
.

If
u_{0},u_{1}\in{\mathcal{H}}_{\rho}
, then

d^{2}(u_{0},u_{1})=c

for some constant
c
.

Proof.

We prove Lemma
9.3
by showing that
d^{2}(u_{0},u_{1})
extends as a subharmonic function to
\bar{\mathcal{R}}
. Since
\bar{\mathcal{R}}
is compact, this implies
d^{2}(u_{0},u_{1})
is constant. Indeed, let
\mathbb{D}\subset\mathcal{R}
be a holomorphic disk. By
[
KS1
, Lemma 2.4.2 and Remark 2.4.3]
, the function
d^{2}(u_{0},u_{1})
is subharmonic in
\mathbb{D}
. Since this statement is true for any holomorphic disk
\mathbb{D}\subset\mathcal{R}
, we conclude that
d^{2}(u_{0},u_{1})
is subharmonic in
\mathcal{R}
.

Next, consider the fixed conformal disk
\mathbb{D}^{j}\subset\bar{\mathcal{R}}
centered at
p^{j}\in{\mathcal{P}}
(cf. (
6.4
)).
Since both
u_{0}
and
u_{1}
have sub-logarithmic growth,

d(u_{0}(z),u_{1}(z))+\epsilon\log|z|\rightarrow-\infty
as
z\rightarrow 0
in
\mathbb{D}^{j}
by the triangle inequality. Thus, by Lemma
7.2
,

d^{2}(u_{0},u_{1})
is bounded in
\mathbb{D}^{j*}

and extends as a subharmonic function on
\mathbb{D}^{j}
. Hence, we conclude that
d^{2}(u_{0},u_{1})
extends as a subharmonic function on
\bar{\mathcal{R}}
, thereby proving Lemma
9.3
.
∎

For
u_{0},u_{1}\in{\mathcal{H}}_{\rho}
, let
\tilde{u}_{0}
,
\tilde{u}_{1}
be the associated
\rho
-equivariant maps. For
s\in[0,1]
, define
u_{s}:\mathcal{R}\rightarrow\tilde{\mathcal{R}}\times_{\rho}\tilde{X}
to be the associated section of the
\rho
-equivariant map

(9.2)

\tilde{u}_{s}:\tilde{\mathcal{R}}\rightarrow\tilde{X},\ \ \ \tilde{u}_{s}(z)=(1-s)\tilde{u}_{0}(z)+s\tilde{u}_{1}(z)

where the sum on the right hand side above denotes geodesic interpolation (cf. Notation
1.3
).
Since
\tilde{u}_{0}
and
\tilde{u}_{1}
are
\rho
-equivariant,
\tilde{u}_{s}
is also
\rho
-equivariant.

From Lemma
9.2
, Lemma
9.3
, the convexity of the distance function and the convexity of energy (cf.
[
KS1
, (2.2vi)]
), it follows that
u_{s}|_{\mathbb{D}^{j*}}
also satisfies the properties
(i)
,
(ii)
and
(iii)
of Theorem
7.1
for all
s\in[0,1]
; i.e.

•

\displaystyle{E^{u_{s}}[\mathbb{D}^{j}_{r,r_{0}}]\leq C+\mathsf{E}_{j}\log\frac{r_{0}}{r}}

•

\displaystyle{\lim_{r\rightarrow 0}E^{u_{s}|_{\partial\mathbb{D}^{j}_{r}}}[\mathbb{S}^{1}]=\mathsf{E}_{j},}\vskip 1.42262pt

•

u_{s}
has sub-logarithmic growth.

Lemma 9.4
.

Let
u_{0},u_{1}\in{\mathcal{H}}_{\rho}
and
\epsilon>0
. For any
\rho_{0}\in(0,1)
, there exists
r_{0}\in(0,\rho_{0})
such that

\frac{1}{-\log r_{0}}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\int_{0}^{2\pi}d^{2}(u_{0}|_{\partial\mathbb{D}^{j}_{r_{0}}},u_{s}|_{\partial\mathbb{D}^{j}_{r_{0}^{2}}})d\theta<\epsilon,\ \ \ \forall s\in[0,1].

Proof.

It suffices to prove Lemma
9.4
for
s=1
. Let
\rho_{0}>0
be given. First, Lemma
7.8
asserts that there exists
\rho_{1}\in(0,\rho_{0})
such that

\frac{1}{-\log r_{0}}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\int_{0}^{2\pi}d^{2}(u_{1}|_{\partial\mathbb{D}^{j}_{r_{0}}},u_{1}|_{\partial\mathbb{D}^{j}_{r_{0}^{2}}})d\theta<\frac{\epsilon}{4},\ \ \ \ \forall r_{0}\in(0,\rho_{1}).

For
c>0
as in Lemma
9.3
, choose
r_{0}\in(0,\rho_{1})
such that

\frac{2\pi nc}{-\log r_{0}}<\frac{\epsilon}{4}.

Then

\frac{1}{-\log r_{0}}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\int_{0}^{2\pi}d^{2}(u_{0}|_{\partial\mathbb{D}^{j}_{r_{0}}},u_{1}|_{\partial\mathbb{D}^{j}_{r_{0}}})d\theta=\frac{2\pi nc}{-\log r_{0}}<\frac{\epsilon}{4}.

The inequality of Lemma
9.4
for
s=1
follows from the above two inequalities and the triangle inequality.
∎

Lemma 9.5
.

Let
u_{0},u_{1}\in{\mathcal{H}}_{\rho}
and
s\in[0,1]
. For
\epsilon>0
, there exists
\rho_{0}>0
sufficiently small such that

-\log r_{0}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\left(E^{u_{0}|_{\partial\mathbb{D}^{j}_{r_{0}}}}[\mathbb{S}^{1}]+E^{u_{s}|_{\partial\mathbb{D}^{j}_{r_{0}^{2}}}}[\mathbb{S}^{1}]\right)<-2\log r_{0}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\mathsf{E}_{j}+\epsilon,\ \ \ 0<r_{0}<\rho_{0}.

Proof.

For
s=1
, Lemma
9.5
follows from Lemma
7.7
. The general case of
s\in[0,1]
follows immediately by convexity of energy (cf.
[
KS1
, (2.2vi)]
).
∎

Lemma 9.6
.

For
u_{0},u_{1}\in{\mathcal{H}}_{\rho}
and
s\in[0,1]
, we have

E^{u_{0}}(0)=E^{u_{s}}(0).

Proof.

It suffices to prove Lemma
9.6
for
s=1
. Assume on the contrary that
E^{u_{0}}(0)\neq E^{u_{1}}(0)
. By changing the role of
u_{0}
and
u_{1}
if necessary, we can assume
E^{u_{0}}(0)<E^{u_{1}}(0)
. Let
\rho_{0}>0
smaller of the
\rho_{0}
in Lemma
9.4
and Lemma
9.5
. Choose
\epsilon>0
and
r_{0}\in(0,\rho_{0}]
such that

E^{u_{0}}(r_{0})<E^{u_{1}}(r_{0})-2\epsilon

which implies (cf. Definition
9.1
)

(9.3)

E^{u_{0}}[\mathcal{R}_{r_{0}}]<E^{u_{1}}[\mathcal{R}_{r_{0}}]-2\epsilon.

Next fix
r_{1}\in(0,r_{0})
. Let
\tilde{u}_{0}
,
\tilde{u}_{1}:\tilde{\mathcal{R}}\rightarrow\tilde{X}
be the
\rho
-equivariant maps associated to sections
u_{0}
,
u_{1}
.
For the fixed conformal disk
\mathbb{D}^{j}\subset\bar{\mathcal{R}}
centered at the puncture
p^{j}
(cf. (
6.4
)), let
\tilde{\mathbb{D}}^{j}_{r_{1},r_{0}}\subset\tilde{\mathcal{R}}
the lift of
\mathbb{D}^{j}_{r_{1},r_{0}}\subset\mathcal{R}
.
We define a “bridge” between map
\tilde{u}_{0}
and
\tilde{u}_{1}
by setting

\tilde{b}:\bigcup_{j=1}^{n}\tilde{\mathbb{D}}^{j}_{r_{1},r_{0}}\rightarrow\tilde{X}

to be the geodesic interpolation
(1-t)U_{0}(\theta)+tU_{1}(\theta)
where

t=\frac{\log|z|-\log r_{0}}{\log r_{1}-\log r_{0}},\ \ U_{0}(\theta)=\tilde{u}_{0}(r_{0},\theta)\ \text{ and }\ U_{1}(\theta)=\tilde{u}_{1}(r_{1},\theta).

In other words,

\tilde{b}(r,\theta)=\frac{\log r_{1}-\log r}{\log r_{1}-\log r_{0}}\tilde{u}_{0}(r_{0},\theta)+\frac{\log r-\log r_{0}}{\log r_{1}-\log r_{0}}\tilde{u}_{1}(r_{1},\theta)

for
(r,\theta)\in\tilde{\mathbb{D}}^{j}_{r_{1},r_{0}}
,
j=1,\dots,n
.
Let

b:\bigcup_{j=1}^{n}\mathbb{D}^{j}_{r_{1},r_{0}}\rightarrow\tilde{\mathcal{R}}\times_{\rho}\tilde{X}

be the local section associated with
\tilde{b}
.

The NPC condition implies (by an argument analogous to the proof the bridge lemma
[
KS2
, Lemma 3.12]
)

\displaystyle E^{b}[\bigcup_{j=1}^{n}\mathbb{D}^{j}_{r_{1},r_{0}}]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\log\frac{r_{0}}{r_{1}}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\left(E^{u_{0}|_{\partial\mathbb{D}^{j}_{r_{0}}}}[\mathbb{S}^{1}]+E^{u_{1}|_{\partial\mathbb{D}^{j}_{r_{1}}}}[\mathbb{S}^{1}]\right)

\displaystyle\ +\frac{1}{\log\frac{r_{0}}{r_{1}}}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\int_{0}^{2\pi}d^{2}(u_{0}|_{\partial\mathbb{D}^{j}_{r_{0}}},u_{1}|_{\partial\mathbb{D}^{j}_{r_{1}}})d\theta.

Choose
r_{1}=r_{0}^{2}
(cf. Lemma
9.4
and Lemma
9.5
) to obtain

(9.4)

E^{b}[\bigcup_{j=1}^{n}\mathbb{D}^{j}_{r_{0}^{2},r_{0}}]<-\log r_{0}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\mathsf{E}_{j}+2\epsilon.

Since the section

h:\mathcal{R}_{r_{0}^{2}}\rightarrow\tilde{\mathcal{R}}\times_{\rho}\tilde{X}

defined by setting

h=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}u_{0}&\mbox{ in }\mathcal{R}_{r_{0}}\\
b&\mbox{ in }\displaystyle{\bigcup_{j=1}^{n}\mathbb{D}^{j}_{r_{0}^{2},r_{0}}}\end{array}\right.

is a competitor for
u_{1}
, we have

\displaystyle E^{u_{1}}[\mathcal{R}_{r_{0}^{2}}]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle E^{h}[\mathcal{R}_{r_{0}^{2}}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle E^{u_{0}}[\mathcal{R}_{r_{0}}]+E^{b}[\bigcup_{j=1}^{n}\mathbb{D}^{j}_{r_{0}^{2},r_{0}}]

\displaystyle<

\displaystyle E^{u_{1}}[\mathcal{R}_{r_{0}}]-2\epsilon+E^{b}[\bigcup_{j=1}^{n}\mathbb{D}^{j}_{r_{0}^{2},r_{0}}]\ \ \ \ \ \ \mbox{(by (\ref{ococ}))}

\displaystyle<

\displaystyle E^{u_{1}}[\mathcal{R}_{r_{0}}]-\log r_{0}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\frac{\Delta_{I^{j}}^{2}}{2\pi}\ \ \ \ \ \ \mbox{(by (\ref{acac}))}.

Thus,

E^{u_{1}}[\bigcup_{j=1}^{n}\mathbb{D}^{j}_{r_{0}^{2},r_{0}}]<-\log r_{0}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\mathsf{E}_{j}.

This contradicts (
9.1
) and proves Lemma
9.6
.
∎

Lemma 9.7
.

For
u_{0},u_{1}\in\mathcal{H}_{\rho}
, there exists a constant
c
such that

(9.5)

\displaystyle d(u_{s}(p),u_{1}(p))

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle cs,\forall p\in\mathcal{R}

(9.6)

\displaystyle|(u_{s})_{*}(V)|^{2}(p)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle|(u_{0})_{*}(V)|^{2}(p),\ \ \mbox{for a.e.~}s\in[0,1],\ p\in\mathcal{R},V\in T_{p}\tilde{M}.

Proof.

By the convexity of energy (cf.
[
KS1
, (2.2vi)]
),

E^{u_{s}}[\mathcal{R}_{r}]\leq(1-s)E^{u_{0}}[\mathcal{R}_{r}]+sE^{u_{1}}[\mathcal{R}_{r}]-s(1-s)\int_{\mathcal{R}_{r}}|\nabla d(u_{0},u_{1})|^{2}d\mbox{vol}_{M}

for any
r>0
.

E^{u_{s}}(r)\leq(1-s)E^{u_{0}}(r)+sE^{u_{1}}(r)-s(1-s)\int_{\mathcal{R}_{r}}|\nabla d(u_{0},u_{1})|^{2}d\mbox{vol}_{M}.

Letting
r\rightarrow 0
and applying Lemma
9.6
, we conclude

(9.7)

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{R}}|\nabla d(u_{0},u_{1})|^{2}d\mbox{vol}_{M}

(9.8)

\displaystyle E^{u_{s}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle E^{u_{0}},\ \ \forall s\in[0,1]

First, (
9.7
) implies that
\nabla d(u_{0},u_{1})=0
a.e. in
M
which in turn implies
d(u_{0},u_{1})\equiv c
for some
c
. By the definition of the map
u_{s}
, (
9.5
) follows immediately.

Next, for
\{P,Q,R,S\}\subset\tilde{X}
, the quadrilateral comparison for NPC spaces implies

d^{2}(P_{t},Q_{t})\leq(1-t)d^{2}(P,Q)+td^{2}(R,S)

where
P_{t}=(1-t)P+tS
and
Q_{t}=(1-t)Q+tR
.
Applying the above inequality with
P=u_{0}(p)
,
Q=u_{1}(p)
,
S=u_{1}(\exp_{p}(tV))
and
Q=u_{0}(\exp_{t}(tV))
where
t>0
and
V\in T_{p}\tilde{M}
, dividing by
y
and letting
t\rightarrow 0
, we obtain (cf.
[
KS1
, Theorem 1.9.6]
)

|(u_{s})_{*}(V)|^{2}(p)\leq(1-s)|(u_{0})_{*}(V)|^{2}(p)+s|(u_{1})_{*}(V)|^{2}(p),\ \mbox{a.e.~$p\in\tilde{M},V\in T_{p}\tilde{M}$.}

Integrating the above over all unit vectors
V\in T_{p}\tilde{M}
and then over
p\in F
, we obtain

E^{u_{s}}\leq(1-s)E^{u_{0}}+sE^{u_{1}}.

Combining this with (
9.8
) implies (
9.6
).
∎

We assume there exists
u_{0},u_{1}\in\mathcal{H}_{\rho}
such that
u_{0}\not\equiv u_{1}
and treat the two cases (i) and (ii) of Theorem
6.6
separately.

•

\tilde{X}
is a negatively curved space as in Definition
1.6
:
For
p\in\tilde{\mathcal{R}}
, choose
\kappa>0
and
R>0
such that
B_{R}(u_{0}(p))
is a CAT(-
\kappa
) space. Let
\mathcal{U}
be a sufficiently small neighborhood of
p
and
s_{0}\in[0,1]
sufficiently small such that
\tilde{u}_{s}(\mathcal{U})\subset B_{R}(u(p))
for
s\in[0,s_{0}]
. Thus, if
\tilde{u}_{s_{0}}(p)\neq\tilde{u}_{0}(p)
, then applying
[
Me
, Section 5]
implies that the image under
u_{0}
of a sufficiently small neighborhood of
p
is contained in an image
\sigma(\mathbb{R})
of a geodesic line. (If
\tilde{X}
is a smooth manifold, this follows by Hartman
[
Ha
]
). If
\tilde{u}_{0}\not\equiv\tilde{u}_{1}
, then
\tilde{u}_{s}(p)\neq\tilde{u}_{0}(p)
for all
p\in\tilde{\mathcal{R}}
and
s\in(0,s_{0}]
by (
9.5
). Thus, we conclude that
\tilde{u}_{0}(\tilde{\mathcal{R}})
is contained in
\sigma(\mathbb{R})
. Consequently,
\rho(\pi_{1}(\tilde{\mathcal{R}}))
fixes
\sigma(\mathbb{R})
which contradicts the fact that
\rho(\pi_{1}(\mathcal{R}))
is satisfies assumption (B).

•

\tilde{X}
is an irreducible symmetric space of non-compact type or locally finite Euclidean buildings
:
For these two target spaces, Lemma
9.7
combined with
[
DM5
]
implies the uniqueness result.

Chapter 4
Harmonic maps from Kähler surfaces

In this chapter, we assume that
the complex dimension of
M
is 2.

In dimension 2, the intersection of two irreducible components of the normal crossings divisor consists of isolated points. Thus, the analysis around the juncture of a normal crossing divisor is much easier to understand than in higher dimensions.

Throughout this section, we assume:

•

M
is a smooth quasi-projective variety such that
M=\bar{M}\backslash\Sigma
where
\bar{M}
is a smooth projective variety of complex dimension
2
,
\Sigma
is a divisor with normal crossings and
M=\bar{M}\backslash\Sigma

•

\tilde{X}
is an NPC space

•

\rho:\pi_{1}(M)\rightarrow\mathsf{Isom}(\tilde{X})
is a proper homomorphism that does not fix an unbounded closed convex strict subset of
\tilde{X}
.

•

every element (resp. commuting pair) of
\mathsf{Isom}(\tilde{X})
has exponential decay (cf. Definition
2.1
and Definition
3.1
)

In other words,
M
and
\rho
are as in
Theorem
1
with the additional assumption that

\dim_{\mathbb{C}}M=2,

but the target space
\tilde{X}
is more general than in Theorem
1
. By relaxing the restriction on the target space, we include for example the case when
\tilde{X}
is a Euclidean building (cf. Theorem
4
) and the Weil-Petersson completion of Teichmüller space (cf. Theorem
5
).

The main theorems of Chapter
4
are:

Theorem 9.8
.

With the Poincaré-type Kähler metric
g
on
M
(cf. Section
11.1
), there exists a harmonic section

u:M\rightarrow\tilde{M}\times_{\rho}\tilde{X}

of logarithmic energy growth.

Theorem 9.9
.

If in addition
\tilde{X}
is as in Theorem
1
, the harmonic section
u:M\rightarrow\tilde{M}\times_{\rho}\tilde{X}
of Theorem
9.8

is pluriharmonic.

The main idea of the proof of Theorem
9.8
is to construct a
prototype section
which almost minimizes energy near infinity. This idea goes back to
[
Loh
]
and
[
JZ
]
. On the other hand, since we want include the case when the normal bundle of
\Sigma
is non-trivial and also the case when the divisor consists of more than one irreducible components, our argument closely follows the construction in Mochizuki
[
M
]
. We use the prototype section to construct a harmonic section. The idea is to consider the Dirichlet problem on a compactly supported domain with boundary values equal to
v
and then to take a compact exhaustion.

Throughout this Chapter, we let
\bar{M}
be a smooth projective variety of complex dimension
\geq 2
and
\Sigma
be a divisor with normal crossings such that

M=\bar{M}\backslash\Sigma.

Furthermore, let

\Sigma=\bigcup_{j=1}^{L}\Sigma_{j}

where
\{\Sigma_{j}\}
is the set of irreducible components of
\Sigma
.

Chapter
4
consists of the following:

In Section
10
, we study neighborhoods of the divisor of
\bar{M}
. To do so, we consider the following:

•

A neighborhood
\Omega\times\mathbb{D}_{\frac{1}{4}}
of a point in
\Sigma_{j}
away from the crossings

•

A neighborhood

\mathbb{D}_{\frac{1}{4}}\times\mathbb{D}_{\frac{1}{4}}
near the crossing
\Sigma_{j}\cap\Sigma_{i}
.

In Section
11
, we discuss a Poincaré-type metric
g
(cf. Definition
11.1
) due to Cornalba and Griffiths
[
CoGr
]
. This is a complete metric which puts the divisor
\Sigma
at infinity.

In Section
12
, we construct a
\rho
-equivariant
prototype section

v:M\rightarrow\tilde{M}\times_{\rho}\tilde{X}
with controlled growth near infinity. The crucial tool in this contruction is Theorem
7.1
(i.e. the Dirichlet solution on the punctured disk). This enables us to construct a fiber-wise harmonic map on the normal bundle of the divisor. This map defined near the divisor is then extended to all of
M
.

In Section
13
, we give precise estimates for energy growth of the prototype section near the divisor at infinity. These are important because they imply the estimates for the harmonic section.

In Section
14
, we use the prototype section
v:M\rightarrow\tilde{M}\times_{\rho}\tilde{X}
in order to construct a harmonic section
u:M\rightarrow\tilde{M}\times_{\rho}\tilde{X}
(cf. Theorem
9.8
). We end with some energy estimates of the harmonic section constructed.

In Section
15
, we show that the harmonic section is in fact pluriharmonic (cf. Theorem
9.9
). This is where we take advantage of the variation of the Bochner formulas proved in Chapter
2
.

10.
Neighborhoods near the divisor

We continue to denote by
\mathbb{D}
the unit disk in the complex plane. For clarity, we will also denote the unit disk by
\mathbb{D}_{z}
to indicate that
\mathbb{D}
is being parameterized by the complex variable
z=re^{i\theta}
. We use analogous notation
\mathbb{D}_{z,r}
,
\mathbb{D}_{z,r_{1},r_{2}}
, etc. that corresponding to the sets defined in Section
7
. We also use the notation
\mathbb{S}^{1}_{\theta}
to denote the circle
\mathbb{S}^{1}
parameterized by the real variable
\theta
and identify
\mathbb{S}^{1}_{\theta}
as the boundary of
{\mathbb{D}}_{z}
via the map
\theta\mapsto e^{i\theta}
.

Recall that
\bar{M}
is a compact Kähler surface with a divisor
\Sigma
containing only simple normal crossings,

M=\bar{M}\backslash\Sigma,

and
\{\Sigma_{j}:j=1,\cdots,L\}
are the irreducible components of
\Sigma
. Let
\sigma_{j}
be the canonical section of
{\mathcal{O}}(\Sigma_{j})
with zero set
\Sigma_{j}
.

To study a neighborhood of the juncture, let
P\in\Sigma_{i}\cap\Sigma_{j}
for some
i,j\in\{1,\dots,L\}
with
i\neq j
, and let
V_{P}
be a neighborhood of
P
containing no other crossings.
Choose holomorphic trivializations
e_{i}
(resp.
e_{j}
) of
\mathcal{O}(\Sigma_{i})
(resp.
\mathcal{O}(\Sigma_{j})
) on

V_{P}
and define
z^{1}
(resp.
z^{2}
) by setting

(10.1)

\sigma_{i}=z^{1}e_{i},\mbox{ (resp. }\sigma_{j}=z^{2}e_{j}).

For each
j=1,\dots,L
, let
h_{j}
be a Hermitian metric on
\mathcal{\mathcal{}}{\mathcal{O}}(\Sigma_{j})
such that
|e_{j}|_{h_{j}}=1
in
V_{P}
for any crossing
P
.
Let
h
be a Hermitian metric on
\bar{M}
,
not necessarily Kähler, such that the following holds:

(i)

The metric
h
is the Euclidean metric in a neighborhood
V_{P}
of every crossing
P
, i.e.

(10.2)

h|_{V_{P}}=dz^{1}d\bar{z}^{1}+dz^{2}d\bar{z}^{2}.

By rescaling
\sigma_{1}
and
\sigma_{2}
if necessary, we can assume without the loss of generality that

(10.3)

\bar{\mathbb{D}}_{z^{1}}\times\bar{\mathbb{D}}_{z^{2}}\subset V_{P}.

(ii)

The metric
h
induces the orthogonal decomposition
T\bar{M}|_{\Sigma_{j}}=T\Sigma_{j}\oplus N\Sigma_{j}
and under the natural isomorphism

(10.4)

N\Sigma_{j}\simeq\mathcal{\mathcal{}}O(\Sigma_{j})|_{\Sigma_{j}},

the restriction of
h
to
N\Sigma_{j}
is same as
h_{j}
.

For
r\in(0,1]
, we set

\displaystyle{\mathcal{D}}_{j,r}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\{\nu\in N\Sigma_{j}:|\nu|_{h_{j}}<r\},

\displaystyle{\mathcal{D}}_{j,r}^{*}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\{\nu\in N\Sigma_{j}:0<|\nu|_{h_{j}}<r\},

\displaystyle\bar{\mathcal{D}}_{j,r}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\{\nu\in N\Sigma_{j}:|\nu|_{h_{j}}\leq r\}

\displaystyle\bar{\mathcal{D}}_{j,r}^{*}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\{\nu\in\subset N\Sigma_{j}:0<|\nu|_{h_{j}}\leq r\},

\displaystyle{\mathcal{D}}_{r}=\bigcup_{j=1}^{L}{\mathcal{D}}_{j,r},\ \ {\mathcal{D}}_{r}^{*}=\bigcup_{j=1}^{L}{\mathcal{D}}_{j,r}^{*},\ \ \bar{\mathcal{D}}_{r}=\bigcup_{j=1}^{L}\bar{\mathcal{D}}_{j,r},\ \ \bar{\mathcal{D}}_{r}^{*}=\bigcup_{j=1}^{L}\mathcal{D}_{j,r}^{*}

(10.5)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{D}}_{r_{1},r_{2}}={\mathcal{D}}_{r_{2}}\backslash\bar{\mathcal{D}}_{r_{1}}\mbox{ for
$0<r_{1}<r_{2}\leq 1.$}

There exists
r>0
such that the restriction of the exponential map

\exp:N\Sigma_{j}\subset T\bar{M}|_{\Sigma_{j}}\rightarrow\bar{M}

defines diffeomorphism of

{\mathcal{D}}_{j,r}
to a neighborhood of
\Sigma_{j}
in
\bar{M}
. By rescaling
\sigma_{j}
if necessary, we may assume
r>1
.
In particular, we identify (
10.5
) for sufficiently small
r>0
as an open subset of
M
via the exponential map; i.e.

(10.6)

{\mathcal{D}}_{r_{1},r_{2}}:=\bigcup_{j}\{\exp\nu:\nu\in N\Sigma_{j},\ r_{1}<|\nu|_{h_{j}}<r_{2}\}\subset M.

Denote

\bar{\mathcal{D}}_{j}:=\bar{\mathcal{D}}_{j,1}\subset N\Sigma_{j}.

The restriction of the normal bundle
N\Sigma_{j}\rightarrow\Sigma_{j}
to
\bar{\mathcal{D}}_{j}
defines a disk bundle

(10.7)

\pi_{j}:\bar{\mathcal{D}}_{j}\rightarrow\Sigma_{j}.

We also identity
\bar{\mathcal{D}}_{j}
as a subset of
\bar{M}
; i.e.

(10.8)

\bar{\mathcal{D}}_{j}\simeq\exp(\bar{\mathcal{D}}_{j})\subset\bar{M}.

We denote by
J_{\bar{M}}
the holomorphic structure on
\bar{\mathcal{D}}_{j}
defined by pulling back the complex structure on
\bar{M}
via the exponential map.

We now consider a finite collection of sets near the divisor
of the following two types:

•

A set of type (A) admits a local unitary trivialization

(10.9)

\pi_{j}^{-1}(\Omega)\simeq\Omega\times\bar{\mathbb{D}}_{z^{2}},\ \

of
\pi_{j}:\bar{\mathcal{D}}_{j}\rightarrow\Sigma_{j}
where
\Omega\subset\Sigma_{j}
is a contractible open subset of
\Sigma_{j}
containing no crossings. We will use coordinates
(z^{1},z^{2})
where
z^{1}
is a holomorphic local coordinate in
\Omega
and
z^{2}
is the standard coordinate of
\bar{\mathbb{D}}
.
Although the coordinates
(z^{1},z^{2})
are holomorphic with respect to the product complex structure
J_{prod}
of
\Omega\times\bar{\mathbb{D}}_{z^{2}}
, they are
not holomorphic
with respect to the complex structure
J_{\bar{M}}
. However, by construction, we have that

J_{\bar{M}}=J_{prod}\ \ \mbox{on}\ \ T_{(z^{1},0)}(\Omega\times\bar{\mathbb{D}}_{z^{2}}).

•

A set of type (B) is as in (
10.3
); i.e.

(10.10)

\bar{\mathbb{D}}_{z^{1}}\times\bar{\mathbb{D}}_{z^{2}}\subset V_{P}

where
V_{P}
be an open set containing a single crossing
P\in\Sigma_{i}\cap\Sigma_{j}
(
i\neq j
).
By the property (i) of the hermitian metric
h
,

(z^{1},z^{2})
are holomorphic coordinates with respect to
J_{\bar{M}}
.
Furthermore, with the identification

\bar{\mathbb{D}}_{z^{1}}\simeq\bar{\mathbb{D}}_{z^{1}}\times\{0\}\subset\Sigma_{1}
(resp.
\bar{\mathbb{D}}_{z^{2}}\simeq\{0\}\times\bar{\mathbb{D}}_{z^{2}}\subset\Sigma_{2}
),

(10.11)

\pi_{j}^{-1}(\bar{\mathbb{D}}_{z^{1}})\simeq\bar{\mathbb{D}}_{z^{1}}\times\bar{\mathbb{D}}_{z^{2}}\ \ \ (\mbox{resp. }\pi_{i}^{-1}(\bar{\mathbb{D}}_{z^{2}})\simeq\bar{\mathbb{D}}_{z^{1}}\times\bar{\mathbb{D}}_{z^{2}})

is a local unitary trivialization of
\pi_{j}:\bar{\mathcal{D}}_{j}\rightarrow\Sigma_{j}
(resp.
\pi_{i}:\bar{\mathcal{D}}_{i}\rightarrow\Sigma_{i}
).

Definition 10.1
.

We will refer to the coordinates
(z^{1},z^{2})
discussed in (A) above as the
standard product coordinates
on a set
\Omega\times\bar{\mathbb{D}}_{z^{2}}
of type (A).

Definition 10.2
.

We will refer to the coordinates
(z^{1},z^{2})
discussed in (B) above as the
standard product coordinates
and the
holomorphic coordinates
on a set
\bar{\mathbb{D}}_{z^{1}}^{*}\times\bar{\mathbb{D}}_{z^{2}}^{*}
of type (B).

Remark 10.3
.

Holomorphic coordinates in a set of type (A) is defined later (cf. Definition
11.3
).

11.
The Poincaré-type metric and its estimates

11.1.
Poincaré-type metric

Recall the Poincaré metric

(11.1)

g_{poin}=\mbox{Re}\left(\frac{dz\oplus d\bar{z}}{|z|^{2}(\log|z|^{2})^{2}}\right)\mbox{ on }\mathbb{D}^{*}.

Using the canonical section
\sigma_{j}\in{\mathcal{O}}(\Sigma_{j})
and the Hermitian metric
h_{j}
on
{\mathcal{O}}(\Sigma_{j})
given in Section
10
, we define the Poincare-type metric on
M
.

Definition 11.1
.

Let
\bar{\omega}
be the Kähler form on
\bar{M}
. Scale the metric
h_{j}
such that
|\sigma_{j}|_{h_{j}}<1
and define

(11.2)

\omega=\bar{\omega}-\frac{\sqrt{-1}}{2}\sum_{l=1}^{L}\partial\bar{\partial}\log(\log|\sigma_{j}|_{h_{j}}^{-2}).

By scaling
\bar{\omega}
if necessary, we can assume that
\omega
defines a positive form. We denote by
g
the Kähler metric on
M
induced by the Kähler form
\omega
.

Definition 11.2
.

Fix
j
and define on
\bar{M}\backslash\bigcup_{i\neq j}\Sigma_{i}
the Kähler form

(11.3)

\omega+\frac{\sqrt{-1}}{2}\partial\bar{\partial}\log\log|\sigma_{j}|_{h_{j}}^{-2}=\bar{\omega}-\frac{\sqrt{-1}}{2}\sum_{i\neq j}\partial\bar{\partial}\log\log|\sigma_{i}|_{h_{i}}^{-2}.

Define
g_{\Sigma_{j}}
to be the restriction to
\Sigma_{j}\backslash\bigcup_{i\neq j}\Sigma_{i}
of the Kähler metric associated to this Kähler form. Thus, away from
\bigcup_{i\neq j}\Sigma_{i}
, the metric
g_{\Sigma_{j}}
is the smooth part of the metric
g|_{\Sigma_{j}}
.

Below we derive some estimates for the metric
g
in a set of type (A) and of type (B) (See (
10.9
) and (
10.10
) for definitions of a set of type (A) and (B)). These are an expanded form of the estimates derived by Mochizuki
[
M
]
.

11.2.
Metric estimates in set of Type (A)

Let
\pi_{j}^{-1}(\Omega)\simeq\Omega\times\bar{\mathbb{D}}
be a set of type (A) with the standard product coordinates
(z^{1},z^{2})
. (Recall that
\Omega\subset\Sigma_{j}
does not intersect
\Sigma_{i}
for
i\neq j
.)
We will write

z^{1}=x+iy\ \mbox{ and }\ z^{2}=re^{i\theta}.

Fix a local trivialization
e
of
{\mathcal{O}}(\Sigma_{j})
, holomorphic with respect to the complex structure
J_{\bar{M}}
.
Define

(11.4)

b:\Omega\times\bar{\mathbb{D}}\rightarrow[0,\infty),\ \ b=|e|^{-2}_{h_{j}}.

With
\sigma_{j}
the canonical section of
{\mathcal{O}}(\Sigma_{j})
as before, define a function
\zeta
on
\Omega\times\bar{\mathbb{D}}
by

(11.5)

\sigma_{j}=\zeta e.

Thus,
\zeta
is holomorphic with respect to
J_{\bar{M}}
.

Definition 11.3
.

We refer to

(z^{1},\zeta)

as the
holomorphic coordinates (with respect to
J_{\bar{M}}
) on a set
\Omega\times\bar{\mathbb{D}}
of type (A).

Since
z^{2}=0=\zeta
on
\Omega\times\{0\}
and
z^{2}\neq 0
,
\zeta\neq 0
on
\Omega\times\bar{\mathbb{D}}^{*}
,

d\log z^{2}-d\log\zeta=\frac{dz^{2}}{z^{2}}-\frac{d\zeta}{\zeta}=O(1)(dz^{1}+d\bar{z}^{1}+dz^{2}+d\bar{z}^{2}).

Taking real and imaginary parts,

\displaystyle\frac{dr}{r}-\frac{ds}{s}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle O(1)(dz^{1}+d\bar{z}^{1}+dz^{2}+d\bar{z}^{2})

(11.6)

\displaystyle d\theta-d\eta

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle O(1)(dz^{1}+d\bar{z}^{1}+dz^{2}+d\bar{z}^{2}).

Let

a:\Omega\times\bar{\mathbb{D}}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}^{*}

be a smooth function bounded above and bounded away from 0 satisfying

(11.7)

ad\zeta\big|_{\Omega\times\{0\}}=dz^{2}\big|_{(p,0)},\ \ \forall z^{1}\in\Omega.

Thus,

ad\zeta=dz^{2}(1+O(r))=\frac{dz^{2}}{z^{2}}z^{2}(1+O(r))=\left(\frac{d\zeta}{\zeta}+O(1)\right)z^{2}(1+O(r)).

Plugging in
\frac{\partial}{\partial\zeta}
in the above equation, we obtain

(11.8)

a=\frac{z^{2}}{\zeta}(1+O(r)).

From this, we immediately obtain

\displaystyle|a\zeta|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle r(1+O(r))

\displaystyle\log|a\zeta|^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\log r^{2}+\log(1+O(r))=\log r^{2}+O(r).\

This implies

\log|\sigma_{j}|_{h_{j}}^{-2}=\log b|\zeta|^{-2}=\log b-\log r^{2}+\log|a|^{2}+O(r)=-\log r^{2}+A+O(r)

where

A(z^{1})=\log b(z^{1},0)+\log|a(z^{1},0)|^{2}.

The function
a
depends on the choice of
e
and
\sigma
whereas the function
b
depends on the choice of
e
and
h_{j}
. Thus, by scaling
\sigma
if necessary, we can assure that
b
satisfies the following two conditions:

(11.9)

\displaystyle-\log|\zeta|^{2}+\log b

\displaystyle>

\displaystyle 0\mbox{ on }\Omega\times\bar{\mathbb{D}}

(11.10)

\displaystyle\log b+\log|a|^{2}

\displaystyle>

\displaystyle 0\mbox{ on }\Omega\times\{0\}.

We compute

(11.11)

\displaystyle-\frac{\sqrt{-1}}{2}\partial\bar{\partial}\log\log|\sigma_{j}|_{h_{j}}^{-2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\sqrt{-1}}{2}\left(\frac{\partial\log|\sigma_{j}|_{h_{j}}^{2}\wedge\bar{\partial}\log|\sigma_{j}|_{h_{j}}^{2}}{(\log|\sigma_{j}|_{h_{j}}^{2})^{2}}-\frac{\partial\bar{\partial}\log|\sigma_{j}|_{h_{j}}^{2}}{\log|\sigma_{j}|_{h_{j}}^{2}}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\sqrt{-1}}{2}\left(\frac{(\partial\log\zeta+\partial\log b)\wedge(\bar{\partial}\log\bar{\zeta}+\bar{\partial}\log b)}{(\log|\sigma_{j}|_{h_{j}}^{2})^{2}}-\frac{\partial\bar{\partial}\log b}{\log|\sigma_{j}|_{h_{j}}^{2}}\right).

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\sqrt{-1}}{2}\left(\frac{\partial\log\zeta\wedge\bar{\partial}\log\bar{\zeta}}{(\log|\sigma_{j}|_{h_{j}}^{2})^{2}}+\frac{\partial\log\zeta\wedge\bar{\partial}\log b}{(\log|\sigma_{j}|_{h_{j}}^{2})^{2}}\right.

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ +\frac{\partial\log b\wedge\bar{\partial}\log\bar{\zeta}}{(\log|\sigma_{j}|_{h_{j}}^{2})^{2}}+\left.\frac{\partial\log b\wedge\bar{\partial}\log b}{(\log|\sigma_{j}|_{h_{j}}^{2})^{2}}-\frac{\partial\bar{\partial}\log b}{\log|\sigma_{j}|_{h_{j}}^{2}}\right).

Note that because of (
11.7
) and since
b
is a smooth function bounded away from 0, we have

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\log\zeta\wedge\bar{\partial}\log\zeta}{(\log|\sigma_{j}|_{h_{j}}^{2})^{2}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{dz^{2}\wedge d\bar{z}^{2}}{r^{2}(-\log r^{2}+A)^{2}}+Error_{1}+Error_{2}

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\log\zeta\wedge\bar{\partial}\log b}{(\log|\sigma_{j}|_{h_{j}}^{2})^{2}},\ \frac{\partial\log b\wedge\bar{\partial}\log b}{(\log|\sigma_{j}|_{h_{j}}^{2})^{2}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle Error_{1}+Error_{2}

(11.12)

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\log b\wedge\bar{\partial}\log b}{(\log|\sigma_{j}|_{h_{j}}^{2})^{2}},\ \frac{\partial\bar{\partial}\log b}{\log|\sigma_{j}|_{h_{j}}^{2}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle Error_{2}.

where
Error_{1}
is a form of the type

O\left(\frac{1}{r(-\log r^{2}+A)^{2}}\right)dz^{1}\wedge d\bar{z}^{2}\ \mbox{or}\ O\left(\frac{1}{r(-\log r^{2}+A)^{2}}\right)dz^{2}\wedge d\bar{z}^{1}

and
Error_{2}
is a form of the type

O\left(\frac{1}{(-\log r^{2}+A)^{2}}\right)dz^{1}\wedge d\bar{z}^{1}\ \mbox{or}\ O\left(\frac{1}{(-\log r^{2}+A)^{2}}\right)dz^{2}\wedge d\bar{z}^{2}.

In coordinate
z^{1}
of
\Omega\subset\Sigma_{j}
, let the local expression of the metric
g_{\Sigma_{j}}
given by Definition
11.2
be
\lambda\,dz^{1}d\bar{z}^{1}
. Then it follows from the above estimates that in the coordinates
(z^{1},z^{2})
of
\Omega\times\bar{\mathbb{D}}
and with
r=|z^{2}|
, the metric expression of
g
is

(11.13)

\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}g_{1\bar{1}}&g_{1\bar{2}}\\
g_{2\bar{1}}&g_{2\bar{2}}\end{array}\right)=\left(\begin{array}[]{ll}\lambda+O\left(\frac{1}{(-\log r^{2}+A)^{2}}\right)&O\left(\frac{1}{r(-\log r^{2}+A)^{2}}\right)\\
O\left(\frac{1}{r(-\log r^{2}+A)^{2}}\right)&\frac{1}{r^{2}(-\log r^{2}+A)^{2}}+O\left(\frac{1}{(-\log r^{2}+A)^{2}}\right)\end{array}\right).

Furthermore, the local expression for the inverse
g^{-1}
is

(11.14)

\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}g^{1\bar{1}}&g^{1\bar{2}}\\
g^{2\bar{1}}&g^{2\bar{2}}\end{array}\right)=\left(\begin{array}[]{ll}\frac{1}{\lambda}+O\left(r^{2}\right)&O\left(r\right)\\
O\left(r\right)&r^{2}(-\log r^{2}+A)^{2}+O(r^{2})\end{array}\right).

The it product metric
P
on
\Omega\times\bar{\mathbb{D}}
is defined by taking the dominant terms of
g
. More precisely, let

(11.15)

\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}P_{1\bar{1}}&P_{1\bar{2}}\\
P_{2\bar{1}}&P_{2\bar{2}}\end{array}\right)=\left(\begin{array}[]{lc}\lambda&0\\
0&\frac{1}{r^{2}(-\log r^{2}+A)^{2}}\end{array}\right).

The inverse
P^{-1}

\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}P^{1\bar{1}}&P^{1\bar{2}}\\
P^{2\bar{1}}&P^{2\bar{2}}\end{array}\right)=\left(\begin{array}[]{lc}\frac{1}{\lambda}&0\\
0&r^{2}(-\log r^{2}+A)^{2}\end{array}\right).

Thus,

(11.16)

g^{-1}-P^{-1}=\left(\begin{array}[]{ll}O\left(r^{2}\right)&O\left(r\right)\\
O\left(r\right)&O\left(r^{2}\right)\end{array}\right).

Comparing the local expression of
g
and
P
, we obtain

(11.17)

d\mbox{vol}_{g}=d\mbox{vol}_{P}\left(1+O\left(\frac{1}{(-\log r^{2}+A)^{2}}\right)\right).

A straightforward computation gives

\displaystyle d\mbox{vol}_{P}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle d\mbox{vol}_{g_{\Sigma_{j}}}\wedge\frac{dz^{2}\wedge d\bar{z}^{2}}{-2ir^{2}(-\log r^{2}+A)^{2}}

(11.18)

\displaystyle P^{1\bar{1}}d\mbox{vol}_{P}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle g_{\Sigma_{j}}^{1\bar{1}}d\mbox{vol}_{g_{\Sigma_{j}}}\wedge\frac{dz^{2}\wedge d\bar{z}^{2}}{-2ir^{2}(-\log r^{2}+A)^{2}}

\displaystyle P^{2\bar{2}}d\mbox{vol}_{P}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle d\mbox{vol}_{g_{\Sigma_{j}}}\wedge\frac{dz^{2}\wedge d\bar{z}^{2}}{-2i}.

The metric
P
(and hence the metric
g
) is of finite volume over
\Omega\times\mathbb{D}^{*}
since

g_{\Sigma_{j}}
is a smooth metric on
\Omega\subset\Sigma_{j}
and

\displaystyle\mbox{Vol}_{P}(\Omega\times\mathbb{D}^{*})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mbox{Area}(\Omega)\cdot 2\pi\lim_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0}\int_{\epsilon}^{1}\frac{rdr}{r^{2}(-\log r^{2}+A)^{2}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\mbox{Area}(\Omega)\cdot 2\pi\lim_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0}\int_{\epsilon}^{1}\frac{d(-\log r^{2}+A)}{2(-\log r^{2}+A)^{2}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\mbox{Area}(\Omega)\cdot\pi\lim_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0}\left.\frac{1}{-\log r^{2}+A}\right|_{\epsilon}^{1}<\infty.

Moreover, since

\displaystyle 2\pi\mbox{Area}_{g_{\Sigma_{j}}}(\Omega)\log\frac{r_{2}}{r_{1}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega\times\mathbb{D}_{r_{1},r_{2}}}d\mbox{vol}_{g_{\Sigma_{j}}}\wedge\frac{dr\wedge d\theta}{r}=\int_{\Omega\times\mathbb{D}_{r_{1},r_{2}}}P^{\theta\theta}d\mbox{vol}_{P},

there exists a constant
C>0
such that

(11.19)

\displaystyle\left|\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\Omega\times\mathbb{D}_{r_{1},r_{2}}}g^{\theta\theta}d\mbox{vol}_{g}-\mbox{Area}(\Omega)\log\frac{r_{2}}{r_{1}}\right|\leq C,\ \ \ \ 0<r_{1}<r_{2}<1.

Lemma 11.4
.

The Poincaré type metric
g
defined by Definition
11.1
satisfies the following: There exists
c>0
such that, on the set
\Omega\times\mathbb{D}^{*}_{\frac{1}{4}}
away from the crossings with holomorphic coordinates
(z^{1},\zeta=re^{i\theta})
,

\frac{1}{c}\,d\mbox{vol}_{g}\ \leq\ \rho d\rho\wedge d\phi\wedge\frac{rdr\wedge d\theta}{r^{2}(-\log r^{2})^{2}}\ \leq\ c\,d\mbox{vol}_{g}

Proof.

This is immediate from the fact that the metric
g_{\Sigma_{j}}
given by Definition
11.2
is smooth combined with (
11.17
).
∎

Remark 11.5
.

The key feature of the metric
P
is the following: Define
Q
to be the product metric

Q=g_{\Sigma_{i}}\oplus\mbox{Re}\left(\frac{dz^{2}d\bar{z}^{2}}{-2i}\right)=g_{\Sigma_{i}}\oplus(dr^{2}+r^{2}d\theta^{2})\ \mbox{
on $\Omega\times\mathbb{D}^{*}$.}

Then

P^{2\bar{2}}d\mbox{vol}_{P}=Q^{2\bar{2}}d\mbox{vol}_{Q}.

This is important in Section
13
below where we estimate the energy of
v
. In particular, we have

\int_{\Omega\times\mathbb{D}}P^{2\bar{2}}\left|\frac{\partial v}{\partial z^{2}}\right|^{2}d\mbox{vol}_{P}=\int_{\Omega\times\mathbb{D}}Q^{2\bar{2}}\left|\frac{\partial v}{\partial z^{2}}\right|^{2}d\mbox{vol}_{Q}=\int_{\Omega}\left(\int_{\{z^{2}\}\times\mathbb{D}}\left|\frac{\partial v}{\partial z^{2}}\right|^{2}\frac{dz^{2}\wedge d\bar{z}^{2}}{-2i}\right)d\mbox{vol}_{g_{\Sigma_{j}}}.

Note that the inside integral on the right hand side above is exactly the energy of the harmonic map
v|_{\{z^{1}\}\times\mathbb{D}}
from the disk. Indeed, we have already recorded an estimate for this integral in Lemma
12.1
. We will take advantage of this in the proof of Lemma
13.1
below.

11.3.
Metric estimates in a set of Type
(B)

First recall that a type (B) set of Section
10
is a set
\bar{\mathbb{D}}_{z^{1}}\times\bar{\mathbb{D}}_{z^{2}}
with

\bar{\mathbb{D}}_{z^{1}}\subset\Sigma_{j},\ \bar{\mathbb{D}}_{z^{2}}\subset\Sigma_{i}\ \mbox{ and }(0,0)\in\Sigma_{j}\cap\Sigma_{i}

such that the standard product coordinates
(z^{1},z^{2})
are also holomorphic coordinates with respect to complex structure
J_{\bar{M}}
.
Since
|\sigma_{i}|_{h_{i}}=|z^{1}|
and
|\sigma_{j}|_{h_{j}}=|z^{2}|
,

\displaystyle-\frac{\sqrt{-1}}{2}\left(\partial\bar{\partial}\log\log|\sigma_{i}|_{h_{i}}^{-2}\partial\bar{\partial}\log\log|\sigma_{j}|_{h_{j}}^{-2}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\log z^{1}\wedge\bar{\partial}\log\bar{z}^{1}}{(-\log|z^{1}|^{2})^{2}}+\frac{\partial\log z^{2}\wedge\bar{\partial}\log\bar{z}^{2}}{(-\log|z^{2}|^{2})^{2}}.

In the coordinates
(z^{1},z^{2})
and with
\rho=|z^{1}|
and
r=|z^{2}|
, the local expression of the metric
P
associated to the above Kähler form is

(11.20)

\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}P_{1\bar{1}}&P_{1\bar{2}}\\
P_{2\bar{1}}&P_{2\bar{2}}\end{array}\right)=\left(\begin{array}[]{ll}\frac{1}{\rho^{2}(\log\rho^{2})^{2}}&0\\
0&\frac{1}{r^{2}(\log r^{2})^{2}}\end{array}\right)

and the inverse
P^{-1}
is given by

\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}P^{1\bar{1}}&P^{1\bar{2}}\\
P^{2\bar{1}}&P^{2\bar{2}}\end{array}\right)=\left(\begin{array}[]{ll}\rho^{2}(\log\rho^{2})^{2}&0\\
0&r^{2}(\log r^{2})^{2}\end{array}\right).

With
\diamond=O(\rho^{2}(\log\rho^{2})^{2})
and
\Box=O(r^{2}(\log r^{2})^{2})
, the local expression of the metric
g
and its inverse
g^{-1}
is

\displaystyle\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}g_{1\bar{1}}&g_{1\bar{2}}\\
g_{2\bar{1}}&g_{2\bar{2}}\end{array}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\begin{array}[]{ll}\frac{1}{\rho^{2}(\log\rho^{2})^{2}}+O(1)&O(1)\\
O(1)&\frac{1}{r^{2}(\log r^{2})^{2}}+O(1)\end{array}\right)

\displaystyle\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}g^{1\bar{1}}&g^{1\bar{2}}\\
g^{2\bar{1}}&g^{2\bar{2}}\end{array}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\begin{array}[]{ll}\rho^{2}(\log\rho^{2})^{2}(1+\diamond+\Box)&\diamond\Box\\
\diamond\Box&r^{2}(\log r^{2})^{2}(1+\diamond+\Box)\end{array}\right).

Thus,

(11.31)

g^{-1}-P^{-1}=\left(\begin{array}[]{ll}\rho^{2}(\log\rho^{2})^{2}(\diamond+\Box)&\diamond\Box\\
\diamond\Box&r^{2}(\log r^{2})^{2}(\diamond+\Box)\end{array}\right)

(11.32)

d\mbox{vol}_{g}=d\mbox{vol}_{P}\left(1+O\left(\frac{1}{(-\log r^{2}+A)^{2}}\right)+O\left(\frac{1}{(-\log r^{2}+A)^{2}}\right)\right).

A straightforward computation gives

\displaystyle d\mbox{vol}_{P}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{dz^{1}\wedge d\bar{z}^{1}}{-2i\rho^{2}(\log\rho^{2})^{2}}\wedge\frac{dz^{2}\wedge d\bar{z}^{2}}{-2ir^{2}(\log r^{2})^{2}}

(11.33)

\displaystyle P^{1\bar{1}}d\mbox{vol}_{P}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{dz^{1}\wedge d\bar{z}^{1}}{-2i}\wedge\frac{dz^{2}\wedge d\bar{z}^{2}}{-2ir^{2}(\log r^{2})^{2}}

\displaystyle P^{2\bar{2}}d\mbox{vol}_{P}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{dz^{1}\wedge d\bar{z}^{1}}{-2i\rho^{2}(\log\rho^{2})^{2}}\wedge\frac{dz^{2}\wedge d\bar{z}^{2}}{-2i}.

Similarly to (
11.19
), we also obtain for subsets
\mathbb{D}_{r_{1},r_{2}}\times\Omega
,
\Omega\times\mathbb{D}_{r_{1},r_{2}}
of
\bar{\mathbb{D}}_{z^{1}}\times\bar{\mathbb{D}}_{z_{2}}

\displaystyle\left|\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{D}_{r_{1},r_{2}}\times\Omega}g^{\phi\phi}d\mbox{vol}_{g}-\mbox{Area}_{g_{\Sigma_{i}}}(\Omega)\log\frac{r_{2}}{r_{1}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C,

(11.34)

\displaystyle\left|\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\Omega\times\mathbb{D}_{r_{1},r_{2}}}g^{\theta\theta}d\mbox{vol}_{g}-\mbox{Area}_{g_{\Sigma_{j}}}(\Omega)\log\frac{r_{2}}{r_{1}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C.

Lemma 11.6
.

The Poincaré type metric
g
defined by Definition
11.1
satisfies the following: There exists
c>0
such that in neighborhood

\bar{\mathbb{D}}_{\frac{1}{4}}^{*}\times\bar{\mathbb{D}}_{\frac{1}{4}}^{*}
near a crossing with holomorphic coordinates
(z^{1}=\rho e^{i\phi},z^{2}=re^{i\theta})
,

\frac{1}{c}\,d\mbox{vol}_{g}\ \leq\ \frac{\rho d\rho\wedge d\phi}{\rho^{2}(-\log\rho^{2})^{2}}\wedge\frac{rdr\wedge d\theta}{r^{2}(-\log r^{2})^{2}}\ \leq\ c\,d\mbox{vol}_{g}.

Proof.

This is immediate from (
11.3
).
∎

12.
The prototype section

The goal of this section is to construct a
prototype section

with logarithmic energy growth near the divisor. The key is the fiber-wise harmonic sections on the normal bundle of the divisor
\Sigma
, the existence of which follows from the Dirichlet problem on the punctured disk (cf. Theorem
7.1
).

The first step in the construction is to define local sections near the divisor. Recall the sets of type (A) and of type (B) described in (
10.9
) and (
10.10
) respectively. In Subsection
12.2
, we construct a local prototype section in a set of type (A). In Subsection
12.2
, we construct a local prototype section in a set of type (B). In Subsection
12.3
, we glue these sections together to define a prototype section near the divisor and extend it to all of
M
.
In summary, we construct a locally Lipschitz global section

v:M\rightarrow\tilde{M}\times_{\rho}\tilde{X}

of logarithmic energy growth near the divisor.

12.1.
In a neighborhood away from the junctures

The goal of this subsection is to construct a local prototype section in a set of type (A) and derive some energy estimates. We start with the following:

•

\Omega\times\bar{\mathbb{D}}
is a set of type (A) with
\Omega\subset\Sigma_{j}

•

(z^{1},z^{2})
are the standard product coordinates of
\Omega\times\bar{\mathbb{D}}
(cf (
10.1
))

•

r
,
\theta
are parameters defined by

z^{2}=re^{i\theta}

•

\mathbb{S}^{1}_{\theta}
is the boundary
\partial\bar{\mathbb{D}}_{z^{2}}
of
\bar{\mathbb{D}}_{z^{2}}

•

\mathbb{R}_{\theta}\rightarrow\mathbb{S}^{1}
is the universal cover

•

\widetilde{\bar{\mathbb{D}}^{*}}\rightarrow\bar{\mathbb{D}}^{*}
is the universal cover

•

[\mathbb{S}^{1}_{\theta}]
is the element of
\pi_{1}(\mathbb{S}^{1}_{\theta})\simeq\pi_{1}(\Omega\times\bar{\mathbb{D}}^{*})
associated to the loop
\mathbb{S}^{1}_{\theta}\simeq\{z^{1}\}\times\mathbb{S}^{1}_{\theta}

•

\rho^{\prime}:\pi_{1}(\Omega\times\bar{\mathbb{D}}^{*})\rightarrow\mathsf{Isom}(\tilde{X})
is a homomorphism

•

(\Omega\times\widetilde{\bar{\mathbb{D}}^{*}})\times_{\rho^{\prime}}\tilde{X}\rightarrow\Omega\times\bar{\mathbb{D}}^{*}
is a fiber bundle

•

k:\Omega\times\bar{\mathbb{D}}^{*}\rightarrow(\Omega\times\widetilde{\bar{\mathbb{D}}^{*}})\times_{\rho^{\prime}}\tilde{X}
is a locally Lipschitz section

•

\mathbb{R}_{\theta}\times_{\rho^{\prime}}\tilde{X}\rightarrow\mathbb{S}^{1}_{\theta}
is a fiber bundle

•

\mathsf{E}_{j}
is the infimum of the energies of sections
\mathbb{S}^{1}_{\theta}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{\theta}\times_{\rho^{\prime}}\tilde{X}
.

12.1.1.
Construction of a prototype section in a set of type (A)

We define

(12.1)

v:\Omega\times\bar{\mathbb{D}}^{*}\rightarrow(\Omega\times\widetilde{\bar{\mathbb{D}}^{*}})\times_{\rho^{\prime}}\tilde{X}

by setting
v
to be
the fiber-wise harmonic section with boundary values given by
k\big|_{\Omega\times\mathbb{S}^{1}}
. More precisely, we apply Theorem
7.1
as follows: For each
z^{1}\in\Omega
, the restriction

v_{z^{1}}:=v\big|_{\{z^{1}\}\times\bar{\mathbb{D}}^{*}}:\bar{\mathbb{D}}^{*}\approx\{z^{1}\}\times\bar{\mathbb{D}}^{*}\rightarrow\widetilde{\bar{\mathbb{D}}^{*}}\times_{\rho^{\prime}}\tilde{X}

is the unique harmonic section with logarithmic energy growth and boundary values

v_{z^{1}}\big|_{\mathbb{S}^{1}\approx\{z^{1}\}\times\mathbb{S}^{1}}=k\big|_{\mathbb{S}^{1}\approx\{z^{1}\}\times\mathbb{S}^{1}}.

12.1.2.
Derivative estimates in a set of type (A)

Lemma 12.1

(Derivative estimates in set of type (A))
.

There exists a constant
C
such that

\displaystyle\left|\frac{\partial v}{\partial z^{1}}(z^{1}_{0},z^{2}_{0})\right|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C,\ \ \forall(z^{1}_{0},z^{2}_{0})\in\Omega\times\bar{\mathbb{D}}^{*}

\displaystyle\int_{\{z^{1}_{0}\}\times\mathbb{D}_{r,r_{0}}}\left|\frac{\partial v}{\partial z^{2}}\right|^{2}\frac{dz^{2}\wedge d\bar{z}^{2}}{-2i}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C+\mathsf{E}_{j}\log\frac{r_{0}}{r},\ \ \ \forall z^{1}_{0}\in\Omega

where
0<r<r_{0}\leq\frac{1}{4}
and
z^{2}=re^{i\theta}
.

Proof.

Denote the Lipschitz constant of
k
on
\Omega\times\mathbb{S}^{1}
by
L
.
Let
z^{1}_{0}
,
z^{1}\in\Omega
. Since
v_{z^{1}_{0}}
and
v_{z^{1}}
are harmonic sections, the function
z\mapsto d^{2}(v_{z^{1}_{0}}(z),v_{z^{1}}(z))
is subharmonic in
\mathbb{D}^{*}
(cf.
[
KS1
, Remark 2.4.3]
).

Figure 1.
The map
z\mapsto d^{2}(v_{z^{1}_{0}}(z),v_{z^{1}}(z))
is defined on
\mathbb{D}
.

By Theorem
7.1
(iii) and the triangle inequality,

\lim_{z\rightarrow 0}\ d^{2}(v_{z^{1}_{0}}(z),v_{z^{1}}(z))+\epsilon\log|z|=-\infty,\ \ \forall\epsilon>0.

Thus,
d^{2}(v_{z^{1}_{0}},v_{z^{1}})

extends to subharmonic function on
\mathbb{D}
(cf. Lemma
7.2
). Thus, the maximum principle implies that for any
z^{2}_{0}=r_{0}e^{i\theta_{0}}
, we have that,

(12.2)

\displaystyle d^{2}(v_{z^{1}_{0}}(r_{0}e^{i\theta_{0}}),v_{z^{1}}(r_{0}e^{i\theta_{0}}))

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sup_{\theta\in\mathbb{S}^{1}}d^{2}(k(z^{1}_{0},e^{i\theta}),k(z^{1},e^{i\theta}))

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle L^{2}|z_{0}^{1}-z^{1}|^{2}.

In other words, for every fixed
z^{2}_{0}=r_{0}e^{i\theta_{0}}
, the map
z_{1}\mapsto v_{z^{1}}(z^{2}_{0})
is Lipschitz which immediately implies the first estimate. The second estimate follows from
Theorem
7.1
.
∎

12.2.
In a neighborhood of the juncture

The goal of this subsection is to construct a local prototype section in a set of type (B) and derive some derivative estimates. We start with the following:

•

\bar{\mathbb{D}}_{z^{1}}\times\bar{\mathbb{D}}_{z^{2}}
is a set of type (B) with
\mathbb{D}_{z^{1}}\subset\Sigma_{j}
and
\mathbb{D}_{z^{2}}\subset\Sigma_{i}

•

(z^{1},z^{2})
are the standard product (and holomorphic) coordinates

•

\rho
,
\phi
,
r
,
\theta
are the parameters defined by

z^{1}=\rho e^{i\phi}\ \mbox{ and }z^{2}=re^{i\theta}

•

\mathbb{S}^{1}_{\phi}
is the boundary of
\bar{\mathbb{D}}_{z^{1}}
and
\mathbb{S}^{1}_{\theta}
is the boundary of
\bar{\mathbb{D}}_{z^{2}}

•

\mathbb{R}_{\phi}\rightarrow\mathbb{S}^{1}_{\phi}
and
\mathbb{R}_{\theta}\rightarrow\mathbb{S}^{1}_{\theta}
are the universal covers

•

\widetilde{\bar{\mathbb{D}}^{*}_{z^{1}}}\rightarrow\bar{\mathbb{D}}^{*}_{z^{1}}
and
\widetilde{\bar{\mathbb{D}}^{*}_{z^{2}}}\rightarrow\bar{\mathbb{D}}^{*}_{z^{2}}
are the universal cover

•

\mathbb{S}^{1}_{\phi}\times\mathbb{S}^{1}_{\theta}
is the boundary of
\bar{\mathbb{D}}_{z^{1}}\times\bar{\mathbb{D}}_{z^{2}}

•

\mathbb{R}_{\phi}\times\mathbb{R}_{\theta}\rightarrow\mathbb{S}^{1}_{\phi}\times\mathbb{S}^{1}_{\theta}
is the universal covering map

•

[\mathbb{S}^{1}_{\phi}]
is the element of
\pi_{1}(\bar{\mathbb{D}}^{*}_{z^{1}})\simeq\pi_{1}(\mathbb{S}^{1}_{\phi})
generated by
\mathbb{S}^{1}_{\phi}
and
[\mathbb{S}^{1}_{\theta}]
is the element of
\pi_{1}(\bar{\mathbb{D}}^{*}_{z^{2}})\simeq\pi_{1}(\mathbb{S}^{1}_{\theta})
generated by
\mathbb{S}^{1}_{\theta}

•

[\mathbb{S}^{1}_{\phi}]
and
[\mathbb{S}^{1}_{\theta}]
also are the elements of
\pi_{1}(\bar{\mathbb{D}}^{*}_{z^{1}}\times\bar{\mathbb{D}}^{*}_{z^{2}})
generated by
\mathbb{S}^{1}_{\phi}\simeq\mathbb{S}^{1}_{\phi}\times\{z^{2}\}
and
\mathbb{S}^{1}_{\theta}\simeq\{z^{1}\}\times\mathbb{S}^{1}_{\theta}
respectively

•

\pi_{1}(\bar{\mathbb{D}}_{z^{1}}^{*})\simeq\pi_{1}(\mathbb{S}^{1}_{\phi})
and
\pi_{1}(\bar{\mathbb{D}}_{z^{2}}^{*})\simeq\pi_{1}(\mathbb{S}^{1}_{\theta})
are identified as a subgroup of
\pi_{1}(\bar{\mathbb{D}}^{*}_{z^{1}}\times\bar{\mathbb{D}}^{*}_{z^{2}})
by the above identification

•

\rho^{\prime}:\pi_{1}(\bar{\mathbb{D}}^{*}_{z^{1}}\times\bar{\mathbb{D}}^{*}_{z^{2}})\rightarrow\mathsf{Isom}(\tilde{X})
is a homomorphism and
\rho^{\prime}_{k}=\rho^{\prime}|_{\pi_{1}(\bar{\mathbb{D}}_{z^{k}}^{*})}
for
k=1,2

•

(\widetilde{\bar{\mathbb{D}}_{z^{1}}^{*}}\times\widetilde{\bar{\mathbb{D}}^{*}_{z^{2}}})\times_{\rho^{\prime}}\tilde{X}\rightarrow\bar{\mathbb{D}}_{z^{1}}^{*}\times\bar{\mathbb{D}}^{*}_{z^{2}}
and
\widetilde{\bar{\mathbb{D}}_{z^{k}}^{*}}\times_{\rho^{\prime}_{k}}\tilde{X}\rightarrow\bar{\mathbb{D}}_{z^{k}}^{*}
for
k=1,2
are fiber bundles

•

\mathbb{R}_{\phi}\times_{\rho^{\prime}_{1}}\tilde{X}\rightarrow\mathbb{S}^{1}_{\phi}
and
\mathbb{R}_{\theta}\times_{\rho^{\prime}_{2}}\tilde{X}\rightarrow\mathbb{S}^{1}_{\theta}
are fiber bundles

•

\mathsf{E}_{i}
is the infimum of the energies of sections
\mathbb{S}^{1}_{\phi}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{\phi}\times_{\rho^{\prime}_{1}}\tilde{X}

•

\mathsf{E}_{j}
is the infimum of the energies of sections
\mathbb{S}^{1}_{\theta}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{\theta}\times_{\rho^{\prime}_{2}}\tilde{X}

•

If
\rho^{\prime}([\mathbb{S}^{1}_{\theta^{1}}])
and
\rho^{\prime}([\mathbb{S}^{2}_{\theta^{2}}])\in\mathsf{Isom}(\tilde{X})
are semisimple, then call this set-up the
semisimple case
. If they are parabolic we call it the
parabolic case
. Note that by Proposition
3.2
,
\rho([\mathbb{S}^{1}])
and
\rho([\mathbb{S}^{2}])
are both semisimple or both parabolic.

•

k:\bar{\mathbb{D}}_{z^{1}}^{*}\times\bar{\mathbb{D}}^{*}_{z^{2}}\rightarrow(\widetilde{\bar{\mathbb{D}}_{z^{1}}^{*}}\times\widetilde{\bar{\mathbb{D}}^{*}_{z^{2}}})\times_{\rho^{\prime}}\tilde{X}
is a locally Lipschitz section

•

\tilde{k}:\widetilde{\bar{\mathbb{D}}_{w^{1}}^{*}}\times\widetilde{\bar{\mathbb{D}}^{*}_{w^{2}}}\rightarrow\tilde{X}
is the corresponding
\rho^{\prime}
-equivariant map

•

\kappa

is the restriction of
\tilde{k}
to
\mathbb{R}_{\phi}\times\mathbb{R}_{\theta}
.

12.2.1.
Construction of a prototype section in a set of type (B)

We construct the local section

v:\bar{\mathbb{D}}^{*}_{z^{1}}\times\bar{\mathbb{D}}^{*}_{z^{2}}\rightarrow(\widetilde{\bar{\mathbb{D}}^{*}_{z^{1}}}\times\widetilde{\bar{\mathbb{D}}^{*}_{z^{2}}})\times_{\rho^{\prime}}\tilde{X}

as follows:

•

Apply Lemma
3.3
in the semisimple case and Lemma
3.4
in the parabolic case. In either case,
for each
t\in[0,\infty)
,
there exist constants
a,b>0
and a
\rho^{\prime}
-equivariant map

\tilde{h}_{t}:\mathbb{R}_{\phi}\times\mathbb{R}_{\theta}\rightarrow\tilde{X}

and an associated section

h_{t}:\mathbb{S}^{1}_{\phi}\times\mathbb{S}^{1}_{\theta}\rightarrow(\mathbb{R}_{\phi}\times\mathbb{R}_{\theta})\times_{\rho^{\prime}}\tilde{X}

such that

(12.3)

\left|\frac{\partial h_{t}}{\partial t}\right|^{2}\leq 1,\ \ \ \left|\frac{\partial h_{t}}{\partial\phi}\right|^{2}\leq\frac{1}{2\pi}\mathsf{E}_{i}(1+be^{-at}),\ \ \ \left|\frac{\partial h_{t}}{\partial\theta}\right|^{2}\leq\frac{1}{2\pi}\mathsf{E}_{j}(1+be^{-at}).

•

Define the diagonal set

D=\{(\rho e^{i\phi},\rho e^{i\theta})\in\bar{\mathbb{D}}_{z^{1}}^{*}\times\bar{\mathbb{D}}^{*}_{z^{2}}:\rho\in(0,1],\ \ \phi,\theta\in\mathbb{S}^{1}\}.

•

Define
v_{D}:D\rightarrow X
as follows: Fix
(\theta,\phi)\in\mathbb{S}^{1}\times\mathbb{S}^{1}
.

\circ

For
\rho\in(0,\frac{1}{2}]
,
let

v_{D}(\rho e^{i\phi},\rho e^{i\theta})=h_{3(-\log\rho)^{\frac{1}{3}}}(\phi,\theta).

\circ

For
\rho\in[\frac{1}{2},1]
,
let
the curve

\rho\mapsto\gamma(\rho)\mbox{ for }\rho\in[\frac{1}{2},1]

be the the geodesic between
