(V)_{1}=\int_{V}\frac{\eta^{\prime}(N^{-1}\log b|\zeta|^{-2})}{N}\frac{\partial^{2}\log b}{\partial z^{1}\partial\bar{z}^{1}}dz^{1}\wedge d{\bar{z}}^{1}\wedge\left|\frac{\partial u}{\partial\bar{\zeta}}\right|^{2}d\bar{\zeta}\wedge d{\zeta}.

First, use the identity

\frac{\partial^{2}\log b}{\partial z^{1}\partial\bar{z}^{1}}(z^{1},\zeta)=\frac{\partial^{2}\log b}{\partial z^{1}\partial\bar{z}^{1}}(z^{1},0)+O(r)

to write

(V)_{1}=(V)_{1a}+(V)_{1b}.

We estimate

\displaystyle|(V)_{1b}|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{V}\left|\frac{\eta^{\prime}(N^{-1}\log b|\zeta|^{-2})}{N}\right|\left|\frac{\partial u}{\partial\bar{\zeta}}\right|^{2}O(\zeta)dz^{1}\wedge d{\bar{z}}^{1}\wedge d\zeta\wedge d\bar{\zeta}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle c\int_{V_{N}}\frac{r}{(-\log r^{2})}\left|\frac{\partial u}{\partial\bar{\zeta}}\right|^{2}dz^{1}\wedge d{\bar{z}}^{1}\wedge d\zeta\wedge d\bar{\zeta}\ \mbox{ (by (\ref{cleverbd1})}).

Thus, we can argue as in the proof of (
15.20
) to conclude

\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}(V)_{1b}=0.

Furthermore,

\displaystyle(V)_{1b}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{V}\frac{\eta^{\prime}(N^{-1}\log b|\zeta|^{-2})}{N}\frac{\partial^{2}\log b}{\partial z^{1}\partial\bar{z}^{1}}(z^{1},0)dz^{1}\wedge d{\bar{z}}^{1}\wedge\left|\frac{\partial u}{\partial\bar{\zeta}}\right|^{2}d\bar{\zeta}\wedge d{\zeta}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{V}\frac{\eta^{\prime}(N^{-1}\log b|\zeta|^{-2})}{N}\frac{\partial^{2}\log b}{\partial z^{1}\partial\bar{z}^{1}}(z^{1},0)dz^{1}\wedge d{\bar{z}}^{1}\wedge\left(\left|\frac{\partial u}{\partial\bar{\zeta}}\right|^{2}-\frac{\mathsf{E}_{j}}{8\pi r^{2}}\right)d\bar{\zeta}\wedge d{\zeta}

\displaystyle\ \ +\ \frac{\mathsf{E}_{j}}{2\pi}\int_{V}\frac{\eta^{\prime}(N^{-1}\log b|\zeta|^{-2})}{Nr^{2}}\frac{\partial^{2}\log b}{\partial z^{1}\partial\bar{z}^{1}}(z^{1},0)dz^{1}\wedge d{\bar{z}}^{1}\wedge d\bar{\zeta}\wedge d{\zeta}.

The first term on the right hand side above can be estimated by

\displaystyle\left|\int_{V}\frac{\eta^{\prime}(N^{-1}\log b|\zeta|^{-2})}{N}\frac{\partial^{2}\log b}{\partial z^{1}\partial\bar{z}^{1}}(z^{1},0)\left(\left|\frac{\partial u}{\partial\bar{\zeta}}\right|^{2}-\frac{\mathsf{E}_{j}}{2\pi r^{2}}\right)dz^{1}\wedge d{\bar{z}}^{1}\wedge d\bar{\zeta}\wedge d{\zeta}\right|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle c\int_{V_{N}}\left(\left|\frac{\partial u}{\partial\bar{\zeta}}\right|^{2}-\frac{\mathsf{E}_{j}}{2\pi r^{2}}\right)dz^{1}\wedge d{\bar{z}}^{1}\wedge\frac{d\bar{\zeta}\wedge d{\zeta}}{(-\log r^{2})}\ \left(\mbox{by}\ \frac{\partial^{2}\log b}{\partial z^{1}\partial\bar{z}^{1}}=O(1)\ \mbox{and}\ (\ref{cleverbd1})\right).

With these estimates, we can argue as in the proof of (
15.12
) to conclude

\displaystyle\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}(V)_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}(V)_{1a}+(V)_{1b}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\mathsf{E}_{j}}{2\pi}\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}\int_{V}\frac{\eta^{\prime}(N^{-1}\log b|\zeta|^{-2})}{Nr^{2}}\frac{\partial^{2}\log b}{\partial z^{1}\partial\bar{z}^{1}}(z^{1},0)dz^{1}\wedge d{\bar{z}}^{1}\wedge d\bar{\zeta}\wedge d{\zeta}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\mathsf{E}_{j}}{2\pi}\int_{\Omega}\frac{\partial^{2}\log b}{\partial z^{1}\partial\bar{z}^{1}}(z^{1},0)d{z}^{1}\wedge d{\bar{z}}^{1}\cdot\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}\int_{\bar{\mathbb{D}}^{*}_{\frac{1}{4}}}\frac{\eta^{\prime}(N^{-1}\log b|\zeta|^{-2})}{Nr^{2}}d\bar{\zeta}\wedge d{\zeta}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\mathsf{E}_{j}}{2\pi i}\int_{\Omega}\Theta({\mathcal{O}}(\Sigma_{j}))\cdot\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}\int_{0}^{\frac{1}{4}}\frac{\eta^{\prime}(-N^{-1}\log br^{2})}{Nr}dr

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\mathsf{E}_{j}}{2\pi i}\int_{\Omega}\Theta({\mathcal{O}}(\Sigma_{j})).

In the above
\Theta({\mathcal{O}}(\Sigma_{j}))
denotes the curvature of the hermitian metric
h_{j}
on the line bundle
{\mathcal{O}}(\Sigma_{j})
. The estimates for
(I)
,
(II)
,
(III)
,
(IV)
and
(V)
imply that (
15.5
) also holds for
V=\Omega\times\mathbb{D}^{*}_{\frac{1}{4}}
away from the crossings.
∎

15.2.
Proof of Theorem
9.9

Let
\chi_{N}
be as in the proof of Proposition
15.2
(cf. (
15.3
)).
We multiply the modified Siu-Bochner formula of Theorem
4.9
by
\chi_{N}
, integrate over
M
and apply integration by parts to obtain

(15.21)

\int_{M}d\chi_{N}\wedge\{\bar{\partial}_{E}\partial u,\partial u-\bar{\partial}u\}=\frac{1}{2}\int_{M}\chi_{N}\left(4\left|{\partial}_{E^{\prime}}\bar{\partial}u\right|^{2}+Q\right)\omega^{2}.

By the monotone convergence theorem and Proposition
15.2
,

(15.22)

\displaystyle\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}\int_{M}\chi_{N}\left(\left|{\partial}_{E^{\prime}}\bar{\partial}u\right|^{2}+Q\right)\omega^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{M}\left(\left|{\partial}_{E^{\prime}}\bar{\partial}u\right|^{2}+Q\right)\omega^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{M}\left(\left|{\partial}_{E^{\prime\prime}}\bar{\partial}\bar{u}\right|^{2}+Q\right)\omega^{2}.

By the assumption that
\tilde{X}
has strong non-positive curvature in the sense of Siu, the
\left|{\partial}_{E^{\prime}}\bar{\partial}u\right|^{2}+Q=\left|{\partial}_{E^{\prime\prime}}\bar{\partial}\bar{u}\right|^{2}+Q
is non-negative.
Let
V
be either a set
\Omega\times\mathbb{D}^{*}_{\frac{1}{4}}
away from the crossings or the set

\bar{\mathbb{D}}_{\frac{1}{4}}^{*}\times\bar{\mathbb{D}}_{\frac{1}{4}}^{*}
near a crossing (cf. Section
10
).
We claim that

(15.23)

\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}\int_{V}d\chi_{N}\wedge\{\bar{\partial}_{E^{\prime}}\partial u,\partial u-\bar{\partial}u\}=0

for any such set
V
. This implies that the integral (
15.22
) is equal to 0 from which we conclude
\partial_{E^{\prime}}\bar{\partial}u=0=\partial_{E^{\prime}}\bar{\partial}\bar{u}
.

The rest of the proof is devoted to proving (
15.23
). For the sequel, the constant
c>0
is an arbitrary constant independent of the parameter
N
.
First, consider the set

V=\bar{\mathbb{D}}_{\frac{1}{4}}^{*}\times\bar{\mathbb{D}}_{\frac{1}{4}}^{*}
at a crossing with local holomorphic coordinates
(z^{1}=\rho e^{i\phi},z^{2}=re^{i\theta})
(c.f. Theorem
14.7
).
We have

\displaystyle\partial u-\bar{\partial}u

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\frac{\partial u}{\partial z^{1}}dz^{1}-\frac{\partial u}{\partial\bar{z}^{1}}d\bar{z}^{1}\right)+\left(\frac{\partial u}{\partial z^{2}}dz^{2}-\frac{\partial u}{\partial\bar{z}^{2}}d\bar{z}^{2}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle i\left(\frac{\partial u}{\partial\rho}\rho d\phi-\frac{\partial u}{\partial\phi}\frac{d\rho}{\rho}\right)+i\left(\frac{\partial u}{\partial r}rd\theta-\frac{\partial u}{\partial\theta}\frac{dr}{r}\right)

and

\displaystyle d\chi_{N}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\eta(-N\log\rho^{2})\frac{\eta^{\prime}(-N^{-1}\log r^{2})}{N}\frac{2dr}{r}-\frac{\eta^{\prime}(-N^{-1}\log\rho^{2})}{N}\eta(-N\log r^{2})\frac{2d\rho}{\rho}.

Thus,

(15.24)

\displaystyle\int_{V}d\chi_{N}\wedge\{\bar{\partial}_{E^{\prime}}\partial u,\partial u-\bar{\partial}u\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{2}{N}\int_{V}\eta(-N^{-1}\log\rho^{2})\eta^{\prime}(-N^{-1}\log r^{2})\frac{dr}{r}\wedge\{\partial_{E^{\prime}}\bar{\partial}u,\frac{\partial u}{\partial z^{1}}dz^{1}-\frac{\partial u}{\partial\bar{z}^{1}}d\bar{z}^{1}\}

\displaystyle\ -\frac{2i}{N}\int_{V}\eta(-N^{-1}\log\rho^{2})\eta^{\prime}(-N^{-1}\log r^{2})\frac{dr}{r}\wedge\{\partial_{E^{\prime}}\bar{\partial}u,\frac{\partial u}{\partial r}rd\theta\}

\displaystyle\ \ \ -\frac{2}{N}\int_{V}\eta^{\prime}(-N^{-1}\log\rho^{2})\eta(-N^{-1}\log r^{2})\frac{d\rho}{\rho}\wedge\{\partial_{E^{\prime}}\bar{\partial}u,\frac{\partial u}{\partial z^{2}}dz^{2}-\frac{\partial u}{\partial\bar{z}^{2}}d\bar{z}^{2}\}

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ -\frac{2i}{N}\int_{V}\eta^{\prime}(-N^{-1}\log\rho^{2})\eta(-N^{-1}\log r^{2})\frac{d\rho}{\rho}\wedge\{\partial_{E^{\prime}}\bar{\partial}u,\frac{\partial u}{\partial\rho}\rho d\phi\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(i)+(ii)+(i^{\prime})+(ii^{\prime}).

We will show that all the terms
(i)
,
(ii)
,
(i^{\prime})
and
(ii^{\prime})
go to 0 as
N\rightarrow\infty
. We start with
(i)
.
Note that
|\eta(-N^{-1}\log\rho^{2})|
has support in
\rho\geq e^{-\frac{N}{3}}
and
|\eta^{\prime}(-N^{-1}\log r^{2})|
has support in
e^{-\frac{N}{3}}\leq r\leq e^{-\frac{N}{4}}
(cf. (
15.6
)). Thus, the integrand of
(i)
has support in

D_{N}:=\mathbb{D}_{z^{2},e^{-\frac{N}{3}},\frac{1}{4}}\times\mathbb{D}_{z^{1},e^{-\frac{N}{3}},e^{-\frac{N}{4}}}.

We estimate

(15.25)

\displaystyle|(i)|\leq c\int_{D_{N}}\left|\frac{\eta^{\prime}(-N^{-1}\log r^{2})}{N}\right||\partial_{E^{\prime}}\bar{\partial}u|\left|\frac{\partial u}{\partial z^{1}}\right|\rho d\rho\wedge d\phi\wedge\frac{rdr\wedge d\theta}{r}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle c\left(\int_{D_{N}}|\partial_{E^{\prime}}\bar{\partial}u|^{2}dz^{1}\wedge d\bar{z}^{1}\wedge dz^{2}\wedge d\bar{z}^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\ \times\left(\int_{D_{N}}\left(\frac{\eta^{\prime}(-N^{-1}\log r^{2})}{N}\right)^{2}\left|\frac{\partial u}{\partial z^{1}}\right|^{2}dz^{1}\wedge d\bar{z}^{1}\wedge\frac{dz^{2}\wedge d\bar{z}^{2}}{r^{2}}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \mbox{ (by Cauchy-Schwartz and (\ref{cleverbd!}))}.

The first integral above limits to 0 as
N\rightarrow\infty
by Proposition
15.2
, volume estimate (
11.32
) and Lebesgue’s dominated convergence theorem. The limit as
N\rightarrow\infty
of the second integral exists by Lemma
15.1
by following the proof of (
15.14
). Thus

\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}(i)=0.

An analogous argument shows
\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}(i^{\prime})=0.

Next,

\displaystyle|(ii)|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle c\int_{V}\left|\frac{\eta^{\prime}(-N^{-1}\log r^{2})}{N}\right||\bar{\partial}_{E^{\prime}}\partial u|\left|\frac{\partial u}{\partial r}\right|dz^{1}\wedge d\bar{z}^{1}\wedge\frac{dz^{2}\wedge d\bar{z}^{2}}{r}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle c\left(\int_{D_{N}}|\bar{\partial}_{E^{\prime}}\partial u|^{2}dz^{1}\wedge d\bar{z}^{1}\wedge\frac{dz^{2}\wedge d\bar{z}^{2}}{r^{2}(-\log r^{2})^{2}}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\ \times\left(\int_{D_{N}}\left|\frac{\partial u}{\partial r}\right|^{2}dz^{1}\wedge d\bar{z}^{1}\wedge dz^{2}\wedge d\bar{z}^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle(\mbox{by Cauchy-Schwartz and (\ref{cleverbd!})}).

The first integral limits to 0 as
N\rightarrow\infty
by Proposition
15.2
, volume estimate (
11.32
) and Lebesgue’s dominated convergence Theorem. The second integral also limits to 0 by Theorem
14.7
and Lebesgue’s dominated convergence theorem. Thus,
\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}(ii)=0
, and an analogous argument shows

\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}(ii^{\prime})=0
.

Next, consider a set
V=\Omega\times\mathbb{D}^{*}_{\frac{1}{4}}
away from the crossings with holomorphic coordinates
(z^{1},\zeta=re^{i\theta})
.
Since

\partial u-\bar{\partial}u=\left(\frac{\partial u}{\partial z^{1}}dz^{1}-\frac{\partial u}{\partial\bar{z}^{1}}d\bar{z}^{1}\right)+i\left(\frac{\partial u}{\partial r}rd\theta-\frac{\partial u}{\partial\theta}\frac{dr}{r}\right),

we have

(15.26)

\displaystyle\int_{M}d\chi_{N}\wedge\{\bar{\partial}_{E^{\prime}}\partial u,\partial u-\bar{\partial}u\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle i\int_{M}d\chi_{N}\wedge\{\bar{\partial}_{E^{\prime}}\partial u,\frac{\partial u}{\partial r}rd\theta\}+i\int_{M}d\chi_{N}\wedge\{\bar{\partial}_{E^{\prime}}\partial u,\frac{\partial u}{\partial\theta}\frac{dr}{r}\}

\displaystyle\ +\int_{M}d\chi_{N}\wedge\{\bar{\partial}_{E^{\prime}}\partial u,\frac{\partial u}{\partial z^{1}}dz^{1}-\frac{\partial u}{\partial\bar{z}^{1}}d\bar{z}^{1}\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(I)+(II)+(III)

where the integrals
(I)
,
(II)
, and
(III)
are estimated below. Let

G_{N}:=\Omega\times\mathbb{D}_{z^{1},c_{1}e^{-\frac{N}{3}},c_{2}e^{-\frac{N}{4}}}.

Since,

\displaystyle d\chi_{N}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{\eta^{\prime}(N^{-1}\log br^{-2})}{N}\left(\frac{2dr}{r}-\frac{db}{b}\right),

integral
(I)
is bounded by

\displaystyle\left|(I)\right|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left|\int_{V}\frac{\eta^{\prime}(N^{-1}\log br^{-2})}{N}\left(\frac{2dr}{r}-\frac{db}{b}\right)\wedge\{\bar{\partial}_{E^{\prime}}\partial u,\frac{\partial u}{\partial r}rd\theta\}\right|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle c\int_{V}|\bar{\partial}_{E^{\prime}}\partial u|\left|\frac{\partial u}{\partial r}\right|\rho d\rho\wedge d\phi\wedge\frac{rdr\wedge d\theta}{r(-\log r^{2})}\ \ \left(\mbox{since}\ \frac{db}{b}=O(1)\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle c\left(\int_{G_{N}}|\bar{\partial}_{E^{\prime}}\partial u|^{2}dz^{1}\wedge d\bar{z}^{1}\wedge\frac{d\zeta\wedge d\bar{\zeta}}{r^{2}(-\log r^{2})^{2}}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\ \ \times\left(\int_{G_{N}}\left|\frac{\partial u}{\partial r}\right|^{2}dz^{1}\wedge d\bar{z}^{1}\wedge d\zeta\wedge d\bar{\zeta}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\ \ \mbox{(by Cauchy-Schwartz)}.

The first integral limits to 0 by Proposition
15.2
, volume estimate (
11.17
) and Lebesgue’s dominated convergence theorem. The second integral also limits to 0 by Theorem
14.6
(with
s=r
) and Lebesgue’s dominated convergence theorem. Thus,
\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}(I)=0
.

Next, we estimate
(II)
and note that the modified Siu’s Bochner formula is crucial. Indeed, we hightlight the cancellation
\frac{dr}{r}\wedge\frac{dr}{r}=0
below:

\displaystyle|(II)|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left|\int_{V}\frac{\eta^{\prime}(N^{-1}\log br^{-2})}{N}\left(\frac{2dr}{r}-\frac{db}{b}\right)\wedge\{\bar{\partial}_{E^{\prime}}\partial u,\frac{\partial u}{\partial\theta}\frac{dr}{r}\}\right|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle c\int_{V}\left|\frac{\eta^{\prime}(N^{-1}\log br^{-2})}{N}\right||\bar{\partial}_{E^{\prime}}\partial u|\left|\frac{\partial u}{\partial\theta}\right|\rho d\rho\wedge d\phi\wedge\frac{rdr\wedge d\theta}{r}

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \left(\mbox{since}\ \frac{db}{b}=O(1)\mbox{ and }\frac{dr}{r}\wedge\frac{dr}{r}=0\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle c\left(\int_{G_{N}}|\bar{\partial}_{E^{\prime}}\partial u|^{2}dz^{1}\wedge d\bar{z}^{1}\wedge d\zeta\wedge d\bar{\zeta}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\ \ \times\left(\int_{G_{N}}\left|\frac{\partial u}{\partial\theta}\right|^{2}dz^{1}\wedge d\bar{z}^{1}\wedge\frac{d\zeta\wedge d\bar{\zeta}}{r^{2}(-\log r^{2})^{2}}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\ \mbox{ (by Cauchy-Schwartz)}.

The first integral limits to 0 by Proposition
15.2
, volume estimate (
11.17
) and Lebesgue’s dominated convergence theorem. The second integral also limits to 0 by Theorem
14.6
(with
r=s
and
\theta=\eta
) and Lebesgue’s dominated convergence theorem. Thus,
\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}(II)=0
.

Finally,

\displaystyle|(III)|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left|\int_{V}\frac{\eta^{\prime}(N^{-1}\log br^{-2})}{N}\left(\frac{2dr}{r}-\frac{db}{b}\right)\wedge\{\bar{\partial}_{E^{\prime}}\partial u,\frac{\partial u}{\partial z^{1}}dz^{1}-\frac{\partial u}{\partial\bar{z}^{1}}d\bar{z}^{1}\}\right|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle c\int_{G_{N}}|\bar{\partial}_{E^{\prime}}\partial u|\left|\frac{\partial u}{\partial z^{1}}\right|dz^{1}\wedge d\bar{z}^{1}\wedge\frac{d\zeta\wedge d\bar{\zeta}}{r(-\log r^{2})}

\displaystyle\ \ \ \left(\mbox{since}\ \frac{db}{b}=O(1)\ \mbox{and by}\ (\ref{cleverbd1})\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle c\left(\int_{G_{N}}|\bar{\partial}_{E^{\prime}}\partial u|^{2}dz^{1}\wedge d\bar{z}^{1}\wedge d\zeta\wedge d\bar{\zeta}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\ \times\left(\int_{G_{N}}\left|\frac{\partial u}{\partial z^{1}}\right|^{2}dz^{1}\wedge d\bar{z}^{1}\wedge\frac{d\zeta\wedge d\bar{\zeta}}{r^{2}(-\log r^{2})^{2}}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\ \ \ \ \mbox{ (by Cauchy-Schwartz)}.

The first integral limits to 0 by Proposition
15.2
, volume estimate (
11.17
) and Lebesgue’s dominated convergence theorem. The second integral also limits to 0 by Theorem
14.6
(with
r=s
) and Lebesgue’s dominated convergence theorem. Thus,
\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}(III)=0
.

We now conclude that (
15.26
)
\rightarrow 0
as
N\rightarrow\infty
, which combined with the fact that (
15.24
)
\rightarrow 0
as
N\rightarrow\infty

implies (
15.23
). This concludes the proof of Theorem
9.9
.

16.
Appendix to Chapter
4

We conclude this chapter with the following calculus result which was used in the derivation of the energy estimates.

Lemma 16.1
.

Let
\Omega\times\mathbb{D}^{*}_{\frac{1}{4}}
be a subset of
\Omega\times\bar{\mathbb{D}}
of type (A) or
\Omega\times\bar{\mathbb{D}}^{*}:=\mathbb{D}_{\frac{1}{4},1}\times\bar{\mathbb{D}}
of type (B)
1
with standard product coordinates
(z^{1},z^{2}=re^{i\theta})
.
If a locally Lipschitz map
f
defined on
\Omega\times\mathbb{D}^{*}_{\frac{1}{4}}

satisfies

(16.1)

\int_{\Omega\times\bar{\mathbb{D}}^{*}_{\frac{1}{4}}}\left(\left|\frac{\partial f}{\partial\theta}\right|^{2}-c\right)dz^{1}\wedge d\bar{z}^{1}\wedge\frac{dz^{2}\wedge d\bar{z}^{2}}{r^{2}}\leq C

where

\int_{\{z^{1}\}\times\partial\mathbb{D}_{r}}\left|\frac{\partial f}{\partial\theta}\right|^{2}d\theta\geq c\geq 0,

then

\int_{\Omega\times\bar{\mathbb{D}}^{*}_{\frac{1}{4}}}\left|\frac{\partial f}{\partial\theta}\right|^{2}dz^{1}\wedge d\bar{z}^{1}\wedge\frac{dz^{2}\wedge d\bar{z}^{2}}{r^{2}(-\log r^{2})^{2}}\leq C^{\prime}

where
C^{\prime}
is a constant depending only on
C
and
c
.

Proof.

We start with the following claim: For any function
\psi:[0,\frac{1}{4}]\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
satisfying
\psi(r)\geq c
,

(16.2)

\int_{0}^{\frac{1}{4}}\psi(r)\frac{dr}{r(\log r^{2})^{2}}\leq c\log 2+\int_{0}^{\frac{1}{4}}\left(\psi(r)-c\right)\frac{dr}{r}.

To prove (
16.2
), first note that

2^{-i-1}\leq r\leq 2^{-i}

implies

\frac{1}{(\log r^{2})^{2}}\leq\frac{1}{(\log 2^{-2i})^{2}}=\frac{1}{4(\log 2)^{2}}\frac{1}{i^{2}}.

Furthermore,
by the assumption that
\psi(r)\geq c\geq 0
,

\int_{2^{-i-1}}^{2^{-i}}\psi(r)\frac{dr}{r}=c\int_{2^{-i-1}}^{2^{-i}}\frac{dr}{r}+\int_{2^{-i-1}}^{2^{-i}}\left(\psi(r)-c\right)\frac{dr}{r}\leq c\log 2+\int_{0}^{\frac{1}{4}}\left(\psi(r)-c\right)\frac{dr}{r}.

The above two inequalities imply

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\frac{1}{4}}\frac{\psi(r)}{r(\log r^{2})^{2}}dr

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i=2}^{\infty}\int_{2^{-i-1}}^{2^{-i}}\frac{\psi(r)}{r(\log r^{2})^{2}}dr

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{1}{4(\log 2)^{2}}\sum_{i=2}^{\infty}\frac{1}{i^{2}}\int_{2^{-i-1}}^{2^{-i}}\psi(r)\frac{dr}{r}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{4(\log 2)^{2}}\sum_{i=2}^{\infty}\frac{1}{i^{2}}\cdot\left(c\log 2+\int_{0}^{\frac{1}{4}}\left(\psi(r)-c\right)\frac{dr}{r}\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle c\log 2+\int_{0}^{\frac{1}{4}}\left(\psi(r)-c\right)\frac{dr}{r}

which proves (
16.2
).

Let

\psi(r):=\int_{\{z^{1}\}\times\partial\mathbb{D}_{r}}\left|\frac{\partial f}{\partial\theta}\right|^{2}d\theta.

Since
\psi(r)\geq c
, we have by (
16.2
) that

\int_{0}^{\frac{1}{4}}\left(\int_{\{z^{1}\}\times\partial\mathbb{D}_{r}}\left|\frac{\partial f}{\partial\theta}\right|^{2}d\theta\right)\frac{dr}{r(\log r^{2})^{2}}\leq c\log 2+\int_{0}^{\frac{1}{4}}\left(\int_{\{z^{1}\}\times\partial\mathbb{D}_{r}}\left|\frac{\partial f}{\partial\theta}\right|^{2}d\theta-c\right)\frac{dr}{r}

for a.e.
z^{1}\in\Omega
.
Thus,

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega\times\mathbb{D}_{z^{2},\frac{1}{4}}}\left|\frac{\partial f}{\partial\theta}\right|^{2}\frac{dz^{1}\wedge d\bar{z}^{1}}{-2i}\wedge\frac{rdr\wedge d\theta}{r^{2}(\log r^{2})^{2}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\int_{0}^{\frac{1}{4}}\left(\int_{\{z^{1}\}\times\partial\mathbb{D}_{r}}\left|\frac{\partial f}{\partial\theta}\right|^{2}d\theta\right)\frac{dr}{r(\log r^{2})^{2}}\wedge\frac{dz^{1}\wedge d\bar{z}^{1}}{-2i}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\left(c\log 2+\int_{0}^{\frac{1}{4}}\left(\int_{\{z^{1}\}\times\mathbb{D}_{z^{2},\frac{1}{4}}}\left|\frac{\partial f}{\partial\theta}\right|^{2}d\theta-c\right)\frac{dr}{r}\right)\frac{dz^{1}\wedge d\bar{z}^{1}}{-2i}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle c\log 2\cdot\int_{\Omega}\frac{dz^{1}\wedge d\bar{z}^{1}}{-2i}

\displaystyle\ +\int_{\Omega\times\bar{\mathbb{D}}_{\frac{1}{4}}}\left(\left|\frac{\partial f}{\partial\theta}\right|^{2}-c\right)\frac{dz^{1}\wedge d\bar{z}^{1}}{-2i}\wedge\frac{dr\wedge d\theta}{r}.

∎

Chapter 5
Harmonic maps in higher dimensions

The goal of this section is to prove Theorem
1
.
The proof closely follows the argument in
[
M
]
with some modifications due to the fact that we are dealing with a more general situation. The preparation for the proof is contained in Section
17
. The proof, contained in Section
18
, is by induction on the dimension of
\bar{M}
. Namely, the unique pluriharmonic map of logarithmic growth from a domain of dimension
n
is constructed by piecing together pluriharmonic maps from lower dimensional subvarieties.

17.
Preliminary set-up

Throughout this section,
M
,
\tilde{X}
and
\rho:\pi_{1}(M)\rightarrow\mathsf{Isom}(\tilde{X})
are as in Theorem
1
.
Furthermore, we let
\bar{M}
be a compact projective variety and
\Sigma
be a normal crossing divisor such that
M=\bar{M}\backslash\Sigma
.
We start by recording the following version of the Lefschetz hyperplane theorem (cf.
[
M
, Lemma 21.8]
).

Lemma 17.1
.

If
\bar{Y}\subset\bar{M}
is a sufficiently ample smooth divisor such that
\bar{Y}\cap\Sigma
is a normal crossing divisor, then
the map
\iota_{*}:\pi_{1}(Y)\rightarrow\pi_{1}(M)
is onto where
Y=\bar{Y}\backslash\Sigma
and

\iota:Y\rightarrow M

is the inclusion map.

We will use the following notation throughout this section. For

\bar{Y},\ Y=\bar{Y}\backslash\Sigma,\ \mbox{ and }\ \iota:Y\rightarrow M

as in Lemma
17.1
:

•

\rho_{Y}:\pi_{1}(Y)\rightarrow\mathsf{Isom}(\tilde{X})
is the homomorphism
\rho_{Y}:=\rho\circ\iota_{*}

•

K=\mbox{kernel}(\iota_{*}:\pi_{1}(Y)\rightarrow\pi_{1}(M))

•

\tilde{Y}
is the universal cover of
Y

•

\tilde{Y}/K
is the set of orbits
[p]
of
p\in\tilde{Y}
by the action of
K

•

\hat{\rho}_{Y}:\pi_{1}(Y)/K\simeq\pi_{1}(M)\rightarrow\mathsf{Isom}(\tilde{X})
is the homomorphism

\hat{\rho}_{Y}([\gamma])\mapsto\rho_{Y}(\gamma)

Lemma 17.2
.

The homomorphism
\rho_{Y}:\pi_{1}(Y)\rightarrow\mathsf{Isom}(\tilde{X})
is proper.

Proof.

Lemma
17.1
implies the image of
\rho_{Y}
is equal to the image of
\rho
. Thus,
\rho_{Y}
is proper since
\rho
is proper.∎

Let
\iota^{*}(\tilde{M})\rightarrow Y
denote the pullback bundle of the universal cover
\tilde{M}\rightarrow M
. Since
\pi_{1}(Y)/K\simeq\pi_{1}(M)
, we have the identification
\iota^{*}(\tilde{M})\simeq\tilde{Y}/K
.
The commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{Y}/K\simeq\iota^{*}(\tilde{M})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}

induces an injective morphism of fiber bundles

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{Y}/K\times_{\hat{\rho}_{Y}}\tilde{X}\simeq{\iota^{*}}(\tilde{M}\times_{\rho}\tilde{X})\ \ \ \ \ \ \hookrightarrow}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{M}\times_{\rho}\tilde{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}

Finally, by the identification

\tilde{Y}\times_{\rho_{Y}}\tilde{X}\rightarrow\tilde{Y}/K\times_{\hat{\rho}_{Y}}\tilde{X},\ \ \ [(p,x)]_{\rho_{Y}}\mapsto[([p],x)]_{\hat{\rho}_{Y}}

we obtain

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{Y}\times_{\rho_{Y}}\tilde{X}\ \ \ \ \ \ \hookrightarrow}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{M}\times_{\rho}\tilde{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}

Thus, we conclude that any section

(17.1)

s:Y\rightarrow\tilde{Y}\times_{\rho_{Y}}\tilde{X}

of the fiber bundle
\tilde{Y}\times_{\rho_{Y}}\tilde{X}\rightarrow Y
can be viewed as a map
Y\rightarrow\tilde{M}\times_{\rho}\tilde{X}
. Conversely,
given a section
u:M\rightarrow\tilde{M}\times_{\rho}\tilde{X}
and
Y\subset M
, its restriction

(17.2)

u|_{Y}:Y\rightarrow\tilde{M}\times_{\rho}\tilde{X}

defines a section
Y\rightarrow\tilde{Y}\times_{\rho_{Y}}\tilde{X}
which we denote by

(17.3)

u_{Y}:Y\rightarrow\tilde{Y}\times_{\rho_{Y}}\tilde{X}.

In Definition
6.9
, we defined the notion of sub-logarithmic growth for harmonic maps from a domain of complex dimension 1.
We use the identification of the restriction (
17.2
) of a section from
M
to a section (
17.3
) from a subvariety
Y\subset M
to define the notion of
sub-logarithmic growth
for a pluriharmonic map from a domain of arbitrary dimensions.
To this end,
let

L
be an ample line bundle over
\bar{M}
and
H^{0}(\bar{M},L)
be the space of global holomorphic sections. Define the projectified space

(17.4)

\mathbb{P}=\mathbb{P}(H^{0}(\bar{M},L)^{*}).

Let

\mathcal{C}
be the set of sufficiently ample smooth curves
\bar{\mathcal{R}}\subset\bar{M}
with the following properties:

•

\mathcal{R}:=\bar{\mathcal{R}}\backslash\Sigma=\{s_{1}=0\}\cap\dots\cap\{s_{n-1}=0\},\ s_{1},\dots,s_{n-1}\in\mathbb{P}
with
\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\bar{M}=n
.

•

\bar{\mathcal{R}}\cap\Sigma
is a divisor in
\bar{M}
with normal crossings.

Definition 17.3
.

We say a pluriharmonic section
u:M\rightarrow\tilde{M}\times_{\rho}\tilde{X}
has
sub-logarithmic growth
if, for
\bar{\mathcal{R}}\in\mathcal{C}
, the section
u_{\mathcal{R}}:\mathcal{R}\rightarrow\tilde{\mathcal{R}}\times_{\rho_{\mathcal{R}}}\tilde{X}
(as defined by (
17.3
)) has sub-logarithmic growth (cf. Definition
6.9
).

Remark 17.4
.

It follows from the definition that if
u:M\rightarrow\tilde{M}\times_{\rho}\tilde{X}
is a pluriharmonic section of sub-logarithmic growth and
\bar{Y}
is as in Lemma
17.1
, then its restriction
u_{Y}:Y\rightarrow\tilde{Y}\times_{\rho_{Y}}\tilde{X}
(as defined by (
17.3
)) is a pluriharmonic section of sub-logarithmic growth.

18.
Proof of the Theorem
1
, Theorem
2
, and Theorem
3

We will prove Theorem
1
by an induction on the (complex) dimension of
M
.
Consider the following:

statement

(n)
: Theorem
1
is true whenever
\dim_{\mathbb{C}}M=n
.

18.1.
Intial step of the induction

In this subsection, we prove
statment
(
2
). Thus, assume
\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\bar{M}=2
. We have already shown that there exists a pluriharmonic section
u:M\rightarrow\tilde{M}\times_{\rho}\tilde{X}
in Theorem
9.9
. What remains to be shown is that
u
is the unique pluriharmonic section of sub-logarithmic growth.

Lemma 18.1
.

If
u:M\rightarrow\tilde{M}\times_{\rho}\tilde{X}
is the pluriharmonic section of Theorem
9.9
(i.e.
\dim_{C}\bar{M}=2
), then
u
has sub-logarithmic growth.

Remark 18.2
.

Intuitively, the proof is clear. Near the divisor,
M
has local holomorphic coordinates
(z^{1},\zeta)
. From the energy estimate Theorem
14.6
, the section
u
is Lipschitz in the direction parallel to the divisor (in the
z^{1}
-direction) and has logarithmic energy growth when restricted to the transverse disks to the divisor (i.e. on the holomorphic disks given by parameter
\zeta
). Since harmonic maps from the punctured disk have sub-logarithmic growth (cf. Theorem
7.1
),
u
restricted to the transverse holomorphic disks has sub-logarithmic growth. It then follows that the restriction of
u
to
\mathcal{R}
for any
\bar{\mathcal{R}}\in C
also has sub-logarithmic growth. Details are below.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{R}\in\mathcal{C}
. Since
u:M\rightarrow\tilde{M}\times_{\rho}\tilde{X}
is pluriharmonic,
u_{\mathcal{R}}:\mathcal{R}\rightarrow\tilde{\mathcal{R}}\times_{\rho_{\mathcal{R}}}\tilde{X}
(as defined by (
17.3
)) is harmonic.
Let
\bar{\mathcal{R}}\cap\Sigma=\{p^{1},\dots,p^{n}\}
be the punctures of
\mathcal{R}
. Fix a puncture
p^{k}

and let
V
be a neighborhood of
p^{k}
.
The assumption that
\bar{\mathcal{R}}\cup\Sigma
is a divisor with normal crossings means that
p_{k}
is not a juncture of
\Sigma
. Thus, we may assume that
V=\Omega\times\mathbb{D}_{\frac{1}{4}}
is a subset of a set
\Omega\times\mathbb{D}
away from the crossing (cf. Theorem
14.6
) and such that
p^{k}\in\Omega\times\{0\}
.

Let
(z^{1},\zeta)
be the holomorphic coordinates of
V
(cf. (
11.3
)). Choose
V_{0}
to be a compactly contained subset of
V
. More precisely, we choose an open subset
\Omega_{0}
of
\Omega
and
\rho_{0}>0
such that
p^{k}\in\Omega_{0}\times\{0\}
and set

V_{0}:=\{(z^{1},\zeta):\ z^{1}\in\Omega_{0},\zeta\in\mathbb{D}_{\rho_{0}}^{*}\}\subset V.

For each
z^{1}\in\Omega_{0}
, the disk
\mathcal{D}_{\rho_{0}}^{z^{1}}:=\{(z^{1},\zeta)\in V_{0}:\ \zeta\in\mathbb{D}_{\rho_{0}}\}
is holomorphic in
\bar{M}
. Define the subsets

\mathcal{D}_{\rho_{0}}^{z^{1}*}:=\{(z^{1},\zeta):\ \zeta\in\mathbb{D}^{*}_{\rho_{0}}\},\ \ \ \mathcal{D}_{r,r_{0}}^{z^{1}*}:=\{(z^{1},\zeta):\ \zeta\in\mathbb{D}^{*}_{r,r_{0}}\}.

Let
\mathbb{D}
be a holomorphic disk of
\mathcal{R}
centered at
p:=p^{k}
and
z
be its holomorphic coordinate. Assume that
\mathbb{D}\subset\bar{\mathcal{R}}\cap V_{0}
and let

(z^{1}(z),\zeta(z))
be the coordinates of
z\in\mathbb{D}^{*}
as a point in
V_{0}
.
Since
\bar{\mathcal{R}}
is transverse to
\Sigma_{j}
and
\zeta(z)
is an analytic function in
z
, there exists
m\in\mathbb{N}\backslash\{0\}
and a constant
c>0
such that

(18.1)

\frac{1}{c}\leq\left|\frac{\zeta(z)}{z^{m}}\right|\leq c.

Define the restriction map

u_{z^{1}}=u\big|_{\mathcal{D}^{z^{1}*}}:\mathcal{D}_{\rho_{0}}^{z^{1}*}\rightarrow\tilde{\mathcal{R}}\times_{\rho}\tilde{X}.

The pluriharmonicity of
u
implies the harmonicity of
u_{z^{1}}
and
u_{z^{1}_{0}}
for any
z^{1},z^{1}_{0}\in\Omega_{0}
. Thus,
the function

\zeta\mapsto d^{2}(u_{z^{1}}(\zeta),u_{z^{1}_{0}}(\zeta)),\ \zeta\in\mathbb{D}^{*}_{\rho_{0}}\simeq\mathcal{D}_{\rho_{0}}^{z^{1}*}\simeq\mathcal{D}_{\rho_{0}}^{z_{0}^{1}*}

is a subharmonic function. (Note that this is analogous to the function
z\mapsto d^{2}(v_{z^{1}_{0}}(z),v_{z^{1}}(z))
defined in proof of Lemma
12.1
and Figure
1
.)

Theorem
14.6
and Fubini’s theorem, for a.e.
z^{1}\in\Omega_{0}
,
that there exists a constant
C=C(z^{1})>0
such that

\int_{\mathcal{D}_{r,r_{0}}^{z^{1}*}}\left|\frac{\partial u}{\partial r}\right|^{2}+\frac{1}{r^{2}}\left|\frac{\partial u}{\partial\theta}\right|^{2}\ \frac{dz^{1}\wedge d\zeta}{-2i}\leq\frac{\Delta_{I}^{2}}{2\pi}\log\frac{r_{0}}{r}+C(z^{1}),\ \ 0<r<r_{0}<\rho_{0}.

Thus, noting the lower bound of Lemma
7.3
, we conclude that
for a.e.
z^{1}\in\Omega_{0}
,

(18.2)

\frac{\Delta_{I}^{2}}{2\pi}\log\frac{r_{0}}{r}\leq E^{u_{z^{1}}}[\mathcal{D}_{r,r_{0}}^{z^{1}*}]\leq\frac{\Delta_{I}^{2}}{2\pi}\log\frac{r_{0}}{r}+C(z^{1}),\ \ \ 0<r<r_{0}\leq\rho_{0}.

Let
\Omega_{0}^{\prime}\subset\Omega_{0}
be the set of all
z^{1}\in\Omega_{0}
such that (
18.2
) holds.
By Theorem
7.1
,
u_{z^{1}}
has sub-logarithmic growth for
z^{1}\in\Omega_{0}^{\prime}
.
In other words,
u_{z^{1}}
has sub-logarithmic growth for a.e.
z_{1}
. We will show that this implies that
u_{z^{1}}
has sub-logarithmic growth
for every
z_{1}
since
z^{1}\mapsto u(z^{1},\zeta)
is Lipschitz continuous.

Indeed, given
z^{1},z^{1}_{0}\in\Omega_{0}^{\prime}
, the triangle inequality implies that for all
\epsilon>0
,

\lim_{\zeta\rightarrow 0}d^{2}(u(z^{1},\zeta),u(z^{1}_{0},\zeta))+\epsilon\log|\zeta|=-\infty.

Hence,
\zeta\mapsto d^{2}(u(z^{1},\zeta),u(z^{1}_{0},\zeta))
extends as a subharmonic function on
\mathbb{D}_{\rho_{0}}
by Lemma
7.2
. The maximum principle therefore implies that
for
z^{1},z^{1}_{0}\in\Omega_{0}^{\prime}
and for
\zeta\in\mathbb{D}_{\frac{\rho_{0}}{2}}
,

(18.3)

\displaystyle d^{2}(u(z^{1},\zeta),u(z^{1}_{0},\zeta))

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sup_{\eta\in\partial\mathbb{D}_{\frac{\rho_{0}}{2}}}d^{2}(u(z^{1},\eta),u(z^{1}_{0},\eta))

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle L^{2}|z^{1}-z^{1}_{0}|^{2}

where
L
is the Lipschitz bound of
u
in a compact set of
M
containing
\{(z^{1},\zeta):z^{1}\in\Omega_{0},\ |\zeta|=\frac{\rho_{0}}{2}\}
.
The continuity of
u
implies that (
18.3
) is valid for any
z^{1},z^{1}_{0}\in\Omega_{0}
. In other words, we have shown the Lipschitz continuity of
z^{1}\mapsto u(z^{1},\zeta)
.

We now finish the proof.
Fix
\epsilon>0
and let
z^{1}_{0}\in\Omega_{0}^{\prime}
. Since
u_{z^{1}_{0}}
has sub-logarithmic growth, there exists
\delta\in(0,\rho_{0})
be such that, with
f_{0}
as in Definition
6.9
,

(18.4)

d^{2}(u(\zeta),f_{0}(\zeta))\leq-\frac{\epsilon}{2cm}\log|\zeta|,\ \ \ \forall p^{\prime}\in V_{0}\text{ and }|\zeta|<\delta

where
c
,
m
as in (
18.1
).
For a point
p\in V_{0}
, write

(z^{1}(p),\zeta(p))

in terms of the holomorphic coordinates
(z^{1},\zeta)
of
V
.
Furthermore, let
p^{\prime}\in V_{0}
, write
(z_{0}^{1},\zeta(p^{\prime}))
with
|\zeta(p^{\prime})|<\delta
. Then

\displaystyle d^{2}(u_{\mathcal{R}}(p),f_{0}(p))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle d^{2}(u(p),f_{0}(p))

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 2d^{2}(u(p),u(p^{\prime}))+2d^{2}(u(p^{\prime}),f_{0}(p))\ \ \ \ \mbox{(by the triangle inequality)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2d^{2}(u(z^{1}(p),\zeta(p)),u(z^{1}_{0},\zeta(p^{\prime})))-\frac{\epsilon}{2cm}\log|\zeta(p)|\ \ \ \ \ \ \mbox{(by (\ref{ppl}))}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 2L^{2}|z^{1}(p)-z^{1}_{0}|^{2}-\frac{\epsilon}{2cm}\log|\zeta(p)|\ \ \ \ \mbox{(by (\ref{mp})}).

By (
18.1
),

\lim_{|\zeta(p)|\rightarrow 0}d^{2}(u_{\mathcal{R}}(p),P_{0})+\epsilon\log|\zeta(p)|=-\infty.

Since
p
is an arbitrary puncture of
\mathcal{R}
, we have shown that
u_{\mathcal{R}}
has sub-logarithmic growth.
∎

For any element
s\in\mathbb{P}=\mathbb{P}(H^{0}(\bar{M},L)^{*})
, we set

{\bar{Y}}_{s}:=s^{-1}(0)\ \mbox{and}\ Y_{s}={\bar{Y}}_{s}\backslash\Sigma

and

U=\{s\in\mathbb{P}:{\bar{Y}}_{s}\ \mbox{smooth}\ \mbox{and}\ {\bar{Y}}_{s}\cap\Sigma\ \mbox{ is a normal crossing}\}.

For
q\in M
, consider the subspace

V(q)=\{s\in H^{0}(\bar{M},L):s(q)=0\},\ \ \ \mathbb{P}(q):=\mathbb{P}(V(q)^{*})

and

U(q)=U\cap\mathbb{P}(q).

The sets
U
,
U(q)
are Zariski open subsets of
\mathbb{P}
,
\mathbb{P}(q)
respectively.

Lemma 18.3
.

The pluriharmonic section
u:M\rightarrow\tilde{M}\times_{\rho}\tilde{X}
of Theorem
9.9
(i.e.
\tilde{M}
is a complex surface) is unique amongst all sections
M\rightarrow\tilde{M}\times_{\rho}\tilde{X}
with sub-logarithmic growth.

Proof.

Let
v:M\rightarrow\tilde{M}\times_{\rho}\tilde{X}
also be a pluriharmonic section with sub-logarithmic growth. For
q\in M
and
s\in U(q)
,
the restrictions
u_{Y_{s}}:Y_{s}\rightarrow\tilde{Y}_{s}\times_{\rho_{Y_{s}}}\tilde{X}
and
v_{Y_{s}}:Y_{s}\rightarrow\tilde{Y}_{s}\times_{\rho_{Y_{s}}}\tilde{X}
(as in (
17.3
)) have sub-logarithmic growth.
By Theorem
6.6
,
u_{Y_{s}}=v_{Y_{s}}
. Since
q
is an arbitrary point in
M
, we conclude
u=v
.
∎

We observe that
statement

(2)
follows from Theorem
9.8
, Theorem
9.9
, Lemma
18.1
and Lemma
18.3
.

18.2.
Inductive step

The goal of this subsection is to
assume
statement

(2)
,
\dots
,
statement

(n-1)
, then to prove
statement
(
n
).

To this end, we start with the following:
For each
s\in\mathbb{P}
, let

u^{s}:Y_{s}\rightarrow\tilde{Y}_{s}\times_{\rho_{Y_{s}}}\tilde{X}\simeq\iota^{*}(\tilde{M}\times_{\rho}\tilde{X})\hookrightarrow\tilde{M}\times_{\rho}\tilde{X}

be the pluriharmonic section of logarithmic growth whose existence is guaranteed by the inductive hypothesis.

Lemma 18.4
.

For
q\in M
and
s_{1},s_{2}\in U(q)
, we have

u^{s_{1}}(q)=u^{s_{1}}(q).

Proof.

For
i=1,2
and
q\in M
, let

U(s_{i},q)=\{s\in U(q):{{\bar{Y}}_{s}}\ \mbox{transversal to}\ {\bar{Y}}_{s_{i}}\ \mbox{and}\ \Sigma\cap{\bar{Y}}_{s}\cap{\bar{Y}}_{s_{i}}\ \mbox{normal crossing}\}.

The set
U(s_{i},q)
is Zariski open in
U(q)
which implies
U(s_{1},q)\cap U(s_{2},q)\neq\emptyset
. Fix
s\in U(s_{1},q)\cap U(s_{2},q)
.
Let
\iota:Y_{s_{i}}\cap Y_{s}\rightarrow M
be the inclusion map. Consider the pluriharmonic sections of sub-logarithmic growth

u^{s}:Y_{s}\rightarrow\tilde{Y}_{s}\times_{\rho_{Y_{s}}}\tilde{X}\ \mbox{ and }\ \tilde{u}^{s_{i}}:Y_{s_{i}}\rightarrow\tilde{Y}_{s_{i}}\times_{Y_{s_{i}}}\tilde{X}

and their restriction

u^{s}_{Y_{s_{i}}\cap Y_{s}}:Y_{s_{i}}\cap Y_{s}\rightarrow{\widetilde{Y_{s_{i}}\cap Y_{s}}}\times_{\rho_{Y_{s_{i}}\cap Y_{s}}}\tilde{X}

and

u^{s_{i}}_{Y_{s_{i}}\cap Y_{s}}:Y_{s_{i}}\cap Y_{s}\rightarrow{\widetilde{Y_{s_{i}}\cap Y_{s}}}\times_{\rho_{Y_{s_{i}}\cap Y_{s}}}\tilde{X}.

Since these restrictions are both pluriharmonic sections of sub-logarithmic growth by
statement

(n-2)
, they are in fact the same section; i.e. the sections
u^{s}
and
u^{s_{i}}
restricted to
Y_{s_{i}}\cap Y_{s}
is equal equal.
Since
q\in Y_{s_{i}}\cap Y_{s}
, we conclude
u^{s_{i}}(q)=u^{s}(q)
. ∎

By Lemma
18.4
, we can define

u:M\rightarrow\tilde{M}\times_{\rho}\tilde{X},\ \ u(q):=u_{s}(q)\text{ for }s\in U(q).

To complete the inductive step, we are left to show that
u
is a pluriharmonic section with sub-logarithmic growth and moreover unique amongst pluriharmonic sections with sub-logarithmic growth.
Given
q\in M
and any complex 1-dimensional subspace
\mathcal{P}\subset T_{q}^{1,0}(M)
, we consider the non-empty algebraic set

A_{\mathcal{P}}(q)=\{s\in\mathbb{P}(q):\mathcal{P}\subset T_{q}^{1,0}(Y_{s})\}

and its Zariski open subset

U_{\mathcal{P}}(q)=A_{\mathcal{P}}(q)\cap U(q).

Let
s\in U_{\mathcal{P}}(q)
. By the construction of
u
, its restriction
u_{Y_{s}}
is the unique pluriharmonic section of sub-logarithmic growth. Thus, all derivatives of
u|_{Y_{s}}
exist in the direction of
\mathcal{P}
and

\partial_{E^{\prime}}\bar{\partial}|_{\mathcal{P}}u=\partial_{E^{\prime}}\bar{\partial}|_{\mathcal{P}}u_{s}=0,

thereby proving that
u
is pluriharmonic with sub-logarithmic growth.

To prove uniqueness of
u
, let
v:M\rightarrow\tilde{M}\times_{\rho}\tilde{X}
be a pluriharmonic section of sub-logarithmic growth. For
q\in M
, let
s\in U(q)
.
The restriction
v_{Y_{s}}
(as in (
17.3
)) is a pluriharmonic map of sub-logarithmic growth. By the uniqueness assertion in
statement (
n-1
)
, we conclude
u_{Y_{s}}=v_{Y_{s}}
. Since
q
is an arbitrary point in
M
, we conclude
u=v
. This completes the proof of Theorem
1
.

18.3.
Other cases

We include the proofs of Theorem
2
and Theorem
3
in this subsection.

Proof of Theorem
2
.

Follows from an argument exactly as in
[
Siu1
]
.

\Box

Proof of Theorem
3
.

The only difference between the proof of Theorem
1

and this case is that the uniqueness of
\rho
-equivariant harmonic maps here follows from Theorem
6.6
(i) instead of Theorem
6.6
(ii).

\Box

Chapter 6
Euclidean buildings and Teichmüller spaces

In this chapter, we consider harmonic maps into a locally finite Euclidean building
\tilde{X}
and the Weil-Petersson completion of Teichmüller space.
Both these spaces are singular spaces, and
harmonic maps into
\tilde{X}
may have singular points.

19.
Singlar sets of harmonic maps

Definition 19.1
.

Let
u:\Omega\rightarrow\tilde{X}
be a harmonic map from a Riemannian domain into an NPC space
\tilde{X}
. A point
x\in\Omega
is said to be a
regular point
if there exists a neighborhood
{\mathcal{N}}
of
x
and a totally geodesic subspace
M\subset\tilde{X}
isometric to a smooth Riemannian manifold such that
u(\mathcal{N})\subset M
.
A
singular point
of
u
is a point of
\Omega
that is not a regular point.
A regular (resp. singular) set of
u
is the set of all regular (resp. singular) points of
u
.

We adopt the following definition from
[
GS
, Section 7]
.

Definition 19.2
.

A harmonic map
u:\Omega\rightarrow\tilde{X}
is said to be
pluriharmonic
provided it is pluriharmonic in the usual sense (i.e.
\partial_{E^{\prime}}\bar{\partial}u=0
as in Section
4
) on the regular set.

In Section
15
, we proved that a harmonic map into a smooth manifold is pluriharmonic. The idea was to
multiply the Bochner formula by a smooth function
\chi_{N}
with support away from the divisor. We then apply integration by parts to obtain equalities (
15.4
) and (
15.21
). For a harmonic map into a singular space considered here, we need to
justify integration by parts in the presence of its singular set. To do so, we need the following cut-off functions.

Recall that a
k
-dimensional Euclidean building
\tilde{X}
is the union of
k
-flats. The following theorem due to Gromov and Schoen (cf.
[
GS
, Theorem 6.4]
asserts that away from a small closed set, a harmonic map into a locally finite Euclidean building maps is locally a harmonic map into a
k
-flat.

Theorem 19.3
.

If
u:\Omega\rightarrow\tilde{X}
is a harmonic map into a locally finite Euclidean building, then the the singular set
{\mathcal{S}}(u)
is a closed set of Hausdorff codimension greater or equal to 2.
For any compact subdomain
\Omega_{1}
of
\Omega
, there is a sequence
of Lipschitz functions
\{\psi_{i}\}
with
\psi_{i}\equiv 0
in a neighborhood of
{\mathcal{S}}(u)\cap\bar{\Omega}_{1}
,
0\leq\psi_{i}\leq 1
and
\psi_{i}(x)\rightarrow 1
for all
x\in\Omega_{1}\backslash{\mathcal{S}}(u)
such that

\lim_{i\rightarrow\infty}\int_{\Omega}|\nabla\psi_{i}|d\mu=0

and

\lim_{i\rightarrow\infty}\int|\nabla\nabla u|\,|\nabla\psi_{i}|d\mu=0.

Proof.

The assertions of this theorem except for the first equality are contained in
[
GS
, Theorem 6.4]
.
The first equality can proved by following the proof of
[
GS
, Theorem 6.4]
. We omit the details.
∎

Lemma 19.4
.

For
u
as in Theorem
19.3
,

\int\partial\bar{\partial}\{\bar{\partial}u,\bar{\partial}u\}\chi_{N}=\int\{\bar{\partial}u,\bar{\partial}u\}\wedge\bar{\partial}\partial\chi_{N}.

Proof.

With
\psi_{i}
as in Theorem
19.3
, we have

\displaystyle\int\partial\bar{\partial}\{\bar{\partial}u,\bar{\partial}u\}\chi_{N}\psi_{i}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int\bar{\partial}\{\bar{\partial}u,\bar{\partial}u\}\wedge\partial(\chi_{N}\psi_{i})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int(\bar{\partial}\{\bar{\partial}u,\bar{\partial}u\}\wedge\partial\chi_{N})\psi_{i}+\int(\bar{\partial}\{\bar{\partial}u,\bar{\partial}u\}\wedge\partial\psi_{i})\chi_{N}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\int\left(\{\bar{\partial}u,\bar{\partial}u\}\wedge\bar{\partial}\partial\chi_{N}\right)\psi_{i}+\int\{\bar{\partial}u,\bar{\partial}u\}\wedge\partial\chi_{N}\wedge\bar{\partial}\psi_{i}+\int\left(\bar{\partial}\{\bar{\partial}u,\bar{\partial}u\}\wedge\partial\psi_{i}\right)\chi_{N}.

Furthermore, there exists a constant
C>0
depending only on the Lipschitz constant of
u
and
\chi_{N}
such that

\displaystyle\left|\int\{\bar{\partial}u,\bar{\partial}u\}\partial\chi_{N}\wedge\bar{\partial}\psi_{i}\right|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C\int|\nabla\psi_{i}|\omega^{2},

\displaystyle\left|\int\left(\bar{\partial}\{\bar{\partial}u,\bar{\partial}u\}\wedge\partial\psi_{i}\right)\chi_{N}\right|\leq C\int|\nabla\nabla u||\nabla\psi_{i}|\omega^{2}.

Thus, the assertion follows from letting
i\rightarrow\infty
and applying Theorem
19.3
.
∎

Lemma 19.5
.

For
u
as in Theorem
19.3
,

-\int_{M}\chi_{N}d\{\bar{\partial}_{E}\partial u,\partial u-\bar{\partial}u\}=\int_{M}d\chi_{N}\wedge\{\bar{\partial}_{E}\partial u,\partial u-\bar{\partial}u\}.

Proof.

With
\psi_{i}
as in Theorem
19.3
, we have

\displaystyle-\int_{M}\chi_{N}\psi_{i}d\{\bar{\partial}_{E}\partial u,\partial u-\bar{\partial}u\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{M}\psi_{i}d\chi_{N}\wedge\{\bar{\partial}_{E}\partial u,\partial u-\bar{\partial}u\}+\int_{M}\chi_{N}d\psi_{i}\wedge\{\bar{\partial}_{E}\partial u,\partial u-\bar{\partial}u\}.

Thus, there exists a constant
C>0
depending only on the Lipschitz constant of
u
in the support of
\chi_{N}
such that

\left|\int_{M}\chi_{N}d\psi_{i}\wedge\{\bar{\partial}_{E}\partial u,\partial u-\bar{\partial}u\}\right|\leq C\int|\nabla\nabla u|\,|\nabla\psi|,

the assertion follows from letting
i\rightarrow\infty
and applying Theorem
19.3
.
∎

20.
Proof of Theorem
4
and Theorem
5

Proof of Theorem
4
.

First assume
\dim_{C}M=2
. To
apply Theorem
9.8
, we use the fact that the isometry group of a Euclidean building without a Euclidean factor consists of only elliptic and hyperbolic elements (cf.
[
Pa
, Theorem 4.1]
).
Therefore, any element (resp. commuting pair) of the isometry group has exponential decay (cf. Definition
2.1
and Definition
3.1
).
We can thus invoke Theorem
9.8
to conclude that, with the Poincaré-type Kähler metric
g
on
M
(cf. Section
11.1
), there exists a harmonic section

u:M\rightarrow\tilde{M}\times_{\rho}\tilde{X}

of logarithmic energy growth.

Next, we follow the proof of inequality (
15.5
) to prove

\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}\int\partial\bar{\partial}\chi_{N}\wedge\{\bar{\partial}u,\bar{\partial}u\}<\infty.

Consequently, Lemma
19.4
implies

\int\bar{\partial}\partial\{\bar{\partial}u,\bar{\partial}u\}=\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}\int\bar{\partial}\partial\{\bar{\partial}u,\bar{\partial}u\}\chi_{N}<\infty.

Since Siu’s Bochner formula (cf. Theorem
4.8
) holds for
u
in the regular set which is of full measure, the conclusion of Proposition
15.2
holds. Consequently, we can now apply the proof of (
15.23
) to show
that

\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}\int_{V}d\chi_{N}\wedge\{\bar{\partial}_{E^{\prime}}\partial u,\partial u-\bar{\partial}u\}=0.

By Lemma
19.5
,

\int_{M}d\{\bar{\partial}_{E}\partial u,\partial u-\bar{\partial}u\}=\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}\int_{M}\chi_{N}d\{\bar{\partial}_{E}\partial u,\partial u-\bar{\partial}u\}=0.

Since the variation of the Siu’s Bochner formula of Theorem
4.9
holds for
u
in the regular set, we conclude that
\left|{\partial}_{E^{\prime}}\bar{\partial}u\right|=0
on the regular set of
u
. In other words,
u
is pluriharmonic.

The rest of the proof follows exactly as in the case when
\tilde{X}
is a symmetric space. Namely, the uniqueness follows from the same argument as Lemma
18.1
and Lemma
18.3
. Finally, the inductive argument of Chapter
5
proves the conclusion of Theorem
1
for
\dim_{C}M>2
.

\Box

We next prove Theorem
5
.
Set theoretically, recall that the Weil-Petersson completion
\overline{\mathcal{T}}
of the Teichmüller space
\mathcal{T}
is the augmented Teichmüller space, i.e.
\overline{\mathcal{T}}
is obtained by adding points corresponding to nodal Riemann surfaces to
\mathcal{T}
. Its boundary
\partial\mathcal{T}
can be stratified by smooth open strata of lower dimensional Teichmüller spaces. In other words,
\overline{\mathcal{T}}
is a stratified space (with the original Teichmüller space
\mathcal{T}
being the top dimensional open stratum).

Proof of Theorem
5
.

We repeat the proof of Theorem
4
but with the following three differences:

•

The isometry group
\mathsf{Isom}(\overline{\mathcal{T}})
consists of only elliptic and hyperbolic elements by Thurston’s classification of mapping classes and
[
DW
]
. (Also see
[
B
, Theorem A]
.)

•

We use our regularity theorem for harmonic maps into
\overline{\mathcal{T}}

[
DM4
, Theorem 1.5 and Theorem 1.6]
instead of the Gromov-Schoen regularity theorem for Euclidean buildings (i.e.
[
GS
, Theorem 6.4]
or Theorem
19.3
). Consequently, Lemma
19.4
and Lemma
19.5
hold for harmonic map
u:\Omega\rightarrow\overline{\mathcal{T}}
.

•

The assumption that
\rho
does not fix an unbounded closed convex strict subset is not needed. Indeed, in the proof of Theorem
18.3
, we apply part (i) instead of part (ii) of Theorem
6.6
to prove the uniqueness of harmonic maps with sub-logarithmic growth. This is possible because the Weil-Petersson metric has negative sectional curvature.

\Box
